=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
PARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIN
September 17, 1934.
Tokyo's political report for July,
1934:
There is nothing of importance in the
section on relations with the United States
which is new, the principal item in that
section -- Mr. Saito's reported statements
on American-Japanese relations -- having
been adequately covered by a separate
despatch now being circulated through the
Division.
The report contains several items of
interest in regard to relations with Brazil
and Peru (pages 10 and 1l). It will be
recalled that the Constitution of Brazil was
recently amended so that the restrictions
placed upon immigration into Brazil will
operate to limit the number of Japanese
permitted to enter that country annually
to something like 3,000 persons as compared
with the 20,O00 Japanese who entered Braz11
last year.  It now transpires that the
Constitution of Brazil was also amended
to require that companies with alien
shareholders shall not publish newspapers
containing political or general information.
In regard to relations with Peru, a law has
been passed in Peru requiring that 80% of
the

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
the employees of business concerns in that
country shall be of Peruvian citizenship.
In connection with the question raised
by the plan of the British garrison at
Peiping to hold manoeuvers near Shanhaikwan
it is reported that the Tokyo Foreign Office
1s studying the problem of "clarifying the
position of Manchukuo as an independent
state". In connection with that problem
it is reported (pages 16 and 17) that the
Foreign Office is considering a plan con-
taining the following points:
(a) Negotiations with regard to
problems affecting "Manchukuo" shall
be discussed directly by the interested
countries with "Manchukuo";
(b) International obligations
imposed on "Manchukuo" which "clash
against the honor of a state" shall
be removed;
(c) Favorable treatment shall be
accorded foreign countries which
recognize "Manchukuo";
(d) Preferential treatment shall
be given those foreign countries, such
as Soviet Russia, which assign con-
sular officers to Manchuria after
obtaining recognition from the
"Manchukuo" government.

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
The plan is reported also to envisage the
abolition of extraterritoriality in Man-
churia, although one official of the
Foreign Office is quoted as stating that the
prison system of Manchuria will have to be
improved before that step can be taken.
The "Manchukuo" government is reported
to have proposed to the Japanese Government
the establishment of "Manchukuo" customs
houses at places in Chosen which would serve
as eastern terminals of the recently com-
pleted line leading to Kirin.
I suggest that you read through the
section on pages 22 and 23 on the
Soviet-"Manchukuo" Waterways Conference
which is reported to have reached an agree-
ment. The most recent despatch from Tokyo
states, however, that previous reports
regarding the conclusion of agreement are
believed to havo been somewhat too
optimistic.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
I.
RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES:
(a)
American Independence Day Trans-Pacific
Broadcast- Speeches of the American Ambassador
and Prince Tokugawa.
As part of the American Independence Day broadcast,
the American Ambassador and Prince Tokugawa spoke by radio in
Tokyo to audiences in the United States and Japan*. The
Ambassador cited President Roosevelt's "Good Neighbor
Policy" as the basis on which the long-standing friendship
between the United States and Japan could be progressively
strengthened; he stressed as important the spirit of ap-
proach to the eventual solution of American-Japanese problems
and a determination that the continuance and development of
the friendship betwee the two countries is all essential,
stating that to be effective this determination must be
mutual; he referred to the reaffirmati n by Jponese states-
men of this fri nship and goodwill on the occasion of the
recent celebration in J: pan of th 8oth :nniversury of the
signing of the first treaty betweer the two countries, and
also to the exchange of gcodwill messages by Mr. Hirota
and Mr. Hull; and he then stated: "We may therefore con-
fidently hope and expect that this genuine and abundant
testimony of a constructive policy of fr endship on both
sides of the Pacific shall be translated into fact and shall
be reflected in acts and measures mutually beneficial,
equitable and neighborly." It will be recalled that state-
ments of like tenor emphasizing the importance of translating
goodwil1
* Embassy's despatch No. 881 of July 5, 1934.

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
2
odwill into actions were made by the Ambassador on
several occasions, namely, on April 22, 1934, during the
celebation at Shimoda of the 80th Anniversary of the
Signing of the First Treaty between the United States and
Japan,* on April 25, 1934, in a conversation with the
Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairs**, and on July 14,
1934, in an address delivered before the America-Japan
Student Conference***.
Prince Tokugawa's speech, which was brief and
stereotyped, lauded the United States for its achievements
in the space of 150 years, compared the two nations in their
"spirit of youth and of enterprise and progress", and ex-
pressed the hope that the two countries would continue "to
cooperate closely and wholeheartedly toward the peace and
welfare of the world".

(b)
Arrival in Japen of Mr. Hirgshi Saita,
Japanese Ambassador to the United States.*
The Japanese Ambassador to the United States,
Mr. Hiroshi Saito, arrived in Japan on July 17. The purpose
of his coming to Japan was to confer with his Government
concerning American-Japanese relations and particularly
to renort his views concerning American naval policy
and the probable aims of the United States at the naval
conference scheduled to be held in l935. Among the state-
ments a ttributed by the press to Mr. Saito since his arrival
are the following: that Japan's policy toward the United
States will suffer no change whatever as a result of the
formation of the new Cabinet, particularly because Mr.
Hirota has remained as Foreign Minister; that American
sentiment toward Janan has become more friendly recently;
that, contrary to recent reports, Japan has not proposed
the conclusion of a non-aggression pact with the United
States; that American officials, "like Japanese authorities,"
are of the opinion that the Hull-Hirota good-will messages
are sufficient for the readjustment of American-J panese
relations  no that there is no neea for the conclusion of
an anti-war or an nti-ag ression pact; that America's
basic naval policy is to m intain the Washington and
Iondon Neval Treaties or make them even stronger; that
any question between the two countries could be solved
without war; that he has "never asked the American people
to recognize Minchukuo" and has no intention of doing so
at present; that the American people regard the immigration
question
* Bmbassy's despatch No. 912, of August 3, 1934.

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
question as a purely domestic problem and that it is
advisable for the Japanese to leave the matter as it is;
that he proposed to the American Government the adoption
of the barter system in commerce between the two countries,
but that his proposal met with failure because the Americans
thought that "instead of adopting the barter system
between two nations, a third country should be invited to
join an agreement to develop a triangular trade".

Estonia- Provisional Agreement on Commerce,
Customs, and Navigation Concluded with Japan.
According to an announcement made by the Japanese
Foreign Office on July 19, a provisional agreement on com-
merce, customs, and navigation, containing the most-favored-
nation clause, has been entered into by Japan and Estonia
as a result of notes exchanged on June 21 by the Japanese
and Estonian Ministers at Warsaw*. The Foreign Office
statement gave as the reason for concluding such a pro-
visional agreement, pending the conclusion of a formal
treaty of commerce between the two countries, the fact
that Japanese imports into Estonia, which have been steadily
increasing, have been under a disadvantage as compared with
imports from countries with which Estonia has treaties of
commerce. In the agreement exception is made, in the appli-
cation of most-favored-nation treatment, to special pri-
vileges that might be extended to "Manchukuo", China, and
Soviet Russia by Japan in tariff duties on account of
special geographical relations. Mention of "Manchukuo"
in this agreement cn be regarded s tacit recognition of
"the new state" by Estonia, in the opinion of the TOkYO
NICHI NICHI of July 20, which further states that this
occasion is the first time that "Manchukuo" has been
mentioned in a treaty nd that it will be interesting to
observe

(1) The Shanhaikwan Issue.
NE
A Rengo despatch from Tientsin reported that
Colonel Giga, Chief of the Special Service Section of the
Kwantung Army, had approached the garrison headquarters
of various nations at Tientsin with the demand that they give
notice to him prior to conducting any military operations near
the border of "Manchukuo". The despatch εlso reported that
the commander of the British garrison, after consultation
with the British Minister to China, had replied to Colonel
Giga that the British could not comply with the Japanese
demand, since the Boxer Protocol gave the signatory Powers
the rigit to conduct military operations long the Peiping-
Mukden Railway line. The commander of the British garrison
is also reported to have stated that the British Government
had not recognized "Manchukuo" and hence reserved the right
to take whatever action was deemed necessary along the rail-
way line. While the sume news agency termed the incicent
"acute", it later appeared that the matter woula be settled
to the satisfaction of both sides. A member of the British
Legation at Peiping is reported to have said: 'We are now
negotiating this matter with the Kwantung Army, and solution
is expected soon. We do not consider the question serious
or difficult of settlement". According to THE JAPAN ADVER-
TISER of July 24, the spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office
expressed the belief that the question of military opera-
tions on "Manchukuo" soil could be settled locally by the
army authorities concerned.

The new Trade Agreement between India nd Japan, which
had been initialled at Delhi on April 19 of this year, was
formally signed in London on July 12. The terms of the
agreement had been under discussion between representatives
of Japan and India since September, 1933, when negotiations
were started for the conclusion of a new agreement to replace
the

Indo-Japanese Commercial Treaty of 1905, which had

the new agreement are as follows: that the quota of Japa-
nese cotton piece-goods to be exported to India in a period
of one year beginning April 1 shall be fixed in accordance
with the quantity of Indian raw cotton exported to Japan
in a period of one year beginning January 1 of the same year;
that the basic quota of Japanese piece-g ods to be exported
to India in a picce-goods year shall be 325,000,000 yards

400,000,000 y rds; that Japan and Ind1a shall accord each other

remain in force until March 31, 1937.

(6)
Boviet-Lananese Relations.
E
1. Chinese Eastern Railroad.
Interest in relations with Soviet Russia was re-
vived in Japan towards the end of July by renewed efforts
to settle the problem of the sale of the Chinese Bastern
Railroad. Although the Soviet Ambassador apparently dis-
cussed this problem with Foreign Minister Hirota on July 2
it seemed then as though serious negotiations might be at
an end for the summer. This belief was strengthened by
the departure on July 5 of Mr. Stephen Kunetsov, assistant
director of the railroad and a Soviet delegate to the Con-
ference. However, on July 2l, newspaper reports indicated
that the Foreign Minister was drafting a final compromise
plan, and on the 23rd such a plan was submitted to Ambassador
Yureneff by Mr. Hirota. The details of the plan were closely
guarded, but the Japanese press hastened to hail this final
mediatory effort as a paragon of moderation presaging a
speedy solution of the problem. It is at least possible
that the "Manchukuo" bid for the railway was considerably
raised. In any event, in contrast to occasions when certain
other compromise proposals were submitted to him, Ambassador
Yureneff is believed to have cabled his government for in-
structions. According to press reports, on the day after
the Japanese Foreign Minister submitted the "Manchukuo"
offer for the railroad to the Soviet Ambassador, he out-
lined its terms to the "Manchukuo" Minister in Tokyo who
forthwith accepted them. The press theroupon explained
that the success of the negotiations depended entirely upon
th

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
"sincerity" of the Soviets. A pleasant atmosphere
of optimism prevailed and was spurred on by the Foreign
Office spokesman who spoke in glowing terms on July 25 of
the generosity of the offer. On the 28th the Foreign
Minister of "Manchukuo" issued a statement in which he
spoke quite feelingly of the generosity of the "Manchukuo"
offer drafted by Japan and intimated that under its terms
"Manchukuo" would be made liable for "an enormous sum".
On July 30 Ambassador Yureneff delivered the Soviet reply
to Mr. Hirota. From information so far available it ap-
pears that the offer was found unacceptable and that the
Soviets made some sort of counter proposal. Mr. äirota,
however, reiterated that the "Manchukuo" offer was final
and that, since his mediation had not been successful,
he could only suggest that the Soviets approach the
"Manchukuo" authorities directly and deal with them. While
the spread between the bid and offer prices may now have
been somewhat narrowed, there is no concrete evidence in
support of this, and as the month closes it appears
probable that negotiations will again lapse. This outcome
was in fact suggested by a member of the Soviet Embassy
staff several days ago in conversation with a secretary
of the French Embassy.

(J). China.
(1) Resumption of the Peiping-Mukden Train Service.
Through train service between Peiping and Mukden
was resumed on July 1, in accordance with an agreement reached
on June 28 by Japan, China, and "Manchukuo", after negotia-
tions that had lasted for a year*. The first train that ran
over the line was blown up by bombs that had been placed on the
rails five miles northeast of Tangku, in Chinese territory.
Neither the Japanese Foreign Office nor the Japanese Army
appeared to attach any im ortance to the incident at the
time. However, ten days later the Japanese Army Garrison
was reported to have issued  sharp warning against a repe-
tition of the charge that had been made by the Chinese press
that secret Japanese plans had been responsible for the bombing
of the express train on July 1.

III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS:
(a)
Cabinet- Saito Cabinet Resigns and New
Cabinet is Formed Under Premiership of
Admiral Okada.
On July 3 the long-awaited resignation of the
Saito Cabinet took place en bloc and on July 4 Admiral
Okada, former Minister of the Navy, was charged by
the Emperor with the formation of a new Cabinet* The
resignation of the Saito Cabinet was due mainly to the
Education *** and Finance****, which involved Messrs
Nakajima, Hatoyama, and Kuroda, respectively, in breaches
of "official discipline". Behind this immediate cause
for the resignation there was a general dissatisfaction
on the part of the public over what was considered the
indecisive, procrastinating policy of the Saito Govern-
ment and this dissatisfaction probably influenced the
Cabinet members in their decision to resign.
The principal reasons advanced for the appointment
of Admiral Okada to nead the new Cabinet were that a naval
man was needed in view of the forthcoming Naval Conference
and that a compromise candidate had to be chosen in order
not to antagonize either the extreme patriots or the
liberals * *. On July 8 the Cabinet of Premier Okada
was formally installed. The choice of Ministers was
generally

** Embassy's despatch No. 896 of July 18, 1934.

herally considered as an indication that the relative
policy of moderation of the Saito Government would not be

The statement of policy of the Okada Cabinet, which
was not issued until July 20, contained nothing new and little
of specific interest, consisting mainly of generalities and
promising implicitly to meet the desires of all sectors of
the Japanese nation, the Army, the Navy, the iberals, the
rural population, the working classes in cities end towns,
and the industrialists. This statement of policy, as pub-
lished by THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 21, is uoted below:
" The country is now face to face with an extremely
complicated situation in politics, foreign relations,
finance, economics, and thought, but the Govern-
ment will devote itself to the establishment of fair
and just measures to meet these situations, hoping
for the cooperation of the nation.
" The Government announces as follows the fundamentals
of the policies it will pursue:
" 1. The Government believes that serenity and fair-
ness of administration are essential to the
procurement for the Government of the faith of
the nation, and therefore it will purify poli-
tics and enforce official discipline strictly,
at the same time rectifying the evils that
formerly were present in central and local adminis-
trations. Thus it hopes to awaken the nation
to the true functioning of constitutional adminis-
tration, thereby contributing to the advancement
of national welfare.
n2.
The Government intends to foster the National
Spirit and purify the thought of the nation by
adhering to the spirit of the Imperial Rescripts
granted the nation. By making clear the national
status to the mind of the people, the causes of
dangerous thought will be eliminated.
3.

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
32
"3. The Government will endeavor to insure inter-
national peace and to contribute thereby to the
welfare of mankind, which is indeed the inflex-
ible State policy or Japan. While culti-
vating amicable relations with foreign Powers,
the Government intends to accomplish everything
that the existing position of Japan requires, and
will leave nothing undone for the fulfillment
of the mission that is Japan's.
"4.
National defense is an indispensable factor
in the existence of nations, and the Government
will endeavor to substantiate defense measures
to meet the demands of the time and the going
state of affairs among the Powers. In the
coming disarmament negotiations, the Government
will exert every effort for the assurance of
national safety through fair and justifiable
means.
"5.
The Government believes that reinforcement
of th foundations of State finance is of para-
mount importance in this eventful period in both
foreign and domestic affairs. Considerable
difficulty is of course to be anticipated in
restoring State finance to normal because of
the present extraordinary circumstances, but every
endevor will be made by the Government toward
improvement of national resources, thereby re-
storing the ecuilibrium of income and outgo.
n6.
Stabilization of the means of livelihood for
everyone requires the most urgent attention,
and because of the multiplieity and the com-
plexity of the domestic economic problems, and
because of the peculiar trend of international
economics, the Government knows it must make
plans to meet many situations and take all the
many measures necessary. Therefore the Govern-
ment will endeavor to elevate the living cond1-
tions of the nation and will particularly devote
itself to improvement in the condition of the
rural populations engaged in farming, fishing
and forestry, at the same time endeavoring to
enhance the welfare of the working class of the
population.
n7.
The Government sees the necessity of paying
increased attention to the promotion of indus-
tries, development of natural resources and ad-
ancement of foreign commerce so as to increase
the income of the nation. Therefore, the Govern-
ment intends to establish a general industrial
policy that will embrace all branches of economic
interest, giving attention in so doing to domes-
tic and foreign factors and especially con-
sidering the need of concord and control among
various branches of industrial enterprise so as
to cope with the tendency toward increased
complexity in their relations.
8

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.99)
33
"8.
The Government will pay especial attention
to the cultivation of the "Wippon Spirit" and
the refinement of the individual personality
of the nation, as well as the development of
intellectual faculties befitting the times in
parallel with enhancement of the physical
culture of the nation. Careful consideration
will be paid to the educational systems.
"9.
The Government recognizes that amity between
Japan and Manchukuo is making a tremendous
contribution toward peace in the Far East, and
the Government is determined to collaborate with
the Empire of Manchukuo for the wholesome develop-
ment of that Empire, and, acting on the basic
principle of mutual assistance and mutual bene-
fit, endeavor to harvest an optimistic result.
"10.
The Government recognizes the necessity of paying
careful attention to the mechanism and functions
of the gans of administration so as to insure
fairness and promptitude in governmental under-

with the requirements of the State and the de-
sires of the nation by keeping pace with the daily
business of the public. Consequently, the Govern-
ment intends to make : thoroughgoing study of the
me ns of improving the entire range of administra-
tive organs."
The policy of the new Government as announced was re-
ceived with indifference by the public and with skepticism,
even with sorcas , by most of the newspapers. The TokYo
NICHI NICHI of July ? observes that most of the points in
the C binet's ol:tforn heve been enunciated by previous
Cabinets, remarks that no one could possibly object to these
items, and doubts that the Government will be able to har-
monize the duty of defending the nation with international
peace, financial conditions, and st bilization of the life
of the people. The ASAHI SHIMBUN of July 21 states that
financiai circles were greatly disappointed with the Cabinet's
announcement, particularly with the absence of any definite
declaration regarding farm relief measures. The ASAHI SHIMBUN
of the following day attacks the statement as hazy and states
that it "is nothing more than an orray of phrases which have
no particular meaning". The KOKUMIN of July 22 remarks
that the platform of the Government is one that every
Government is called upon to adopt and that such a platform
leads one to question the need of appointing Admiral Okada
to the

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)

to the premiership. The HOCHI of July 21 is of the

diplomacy and at the same time to strengthen national defense
.is "right and fair" but that as a policy for an emergency
Cabinet it is "deplorably weak". Both the JIJI and the
YOMIURI are concerned more with speculation as to the new
Ministry's capacity to carry out its declarations than with
the declarations themselves. The CHUGAI, more sympathetic

government cannot be expected to state anything startlingly
new", and that it is not fair to criticize the Okada C binet
until it has been given a fair trial.

(b) Political Parties.
The Okada Cabinet has the support, at present, of the
Minseito but is opposed by the Seiyukai, the majority party
in the last Diet. The reasons generally given for the
alienation of the Seiyukai from the present Ministry and its
consequent refusal to sunport the Government or to participate
in the Cabinet are the following: that Admiral Okada consulted
the Minseito Party concerning participation in his Cabinet
before he consulted the Seiyukai; that when he eventually
approached the Seiyukai leaders, Admiral Okada named the posts
available to the Party instead of inquiring what posts the
Party would like to fill; and that Dr. Suzuki, President
of the Seiyukai, aspired to the portfolio of Home Minister,
which was actually given to Mr. Goto , who had been Minister
for Agriculture in the Saito Cabinet. Whatever the
reasons may have been for the Seiyukai's disaffection, this
Party expelled Messrs Tokonami, Y masaki, and Uchida from its
ranks when they accepted the posts of Minister for Communi-
cations, Minister for Agriculture and Forestry, and Minister
for Railways, respectively, in the new Cabinet. It will be
remembered that for some time Mr. Tokonami has been the
leader

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
36
leader of a faction of the Seiyukai opposed to its
President, Dr. Suzuki. It is stated by the press that
Mr. Tokonami and his followers, about thirty in number,
will either cooperate closely with the Minsieto or merge
with that Party to form a new political party. Meanwhile
the Minseito has, according to most reports, lost no time
in taking advantage of the conflict between the Okada Cabinet
and the Seiyukai to attempt to gain political supremacy.
This Party agreed to supoort the new Cabinet shortly after
Admiral Okada's appointment and as a consequence received
two portfolios, those of Education Minister ( Mr. Genji
Matsuda) and Minister for Commerce and Industry (Mr. Chuji
Machida). Mr. Machida is well-known for having seconded
Mr. Tokonami in pleading for a coalition of the Minseito
and the Seiyukai during the last session of the Diet. The
Minsi to stated its desire for the appointment of 10 members
of the Party as parliamentary vice-ministers and parliamentary
counselors instead of the 8 members who held these positions
under the Sito Government. When the parliamentary counselors
were finally selected, on April l9, the Minseito received
9 of these posts, the Seiyukai 9, aná the House of Peers 6.
The selction represented an increase of one official for the
Minseito and a decrease of one for the Seiyukai, as compared
with the alignment under the Saito régime . The Seiyukai
Party promptly expelled the nine members who had accepted
posts as parliamentary vice-ministers and parliamentary
counselors.
According to the newspapers, there is a possibility
that Premier Okada will convoke the Diet and then dissolve
it and call a general election in an effort to obtain a
Government majority in that body. The JAPAN ADVERTISER
of

At the same time, it is reported that the Seiyukai wants
the convocation of a special session of the Diet in order
that it may submit a bill of lack of confidence in the
Cabinet, and clearly demonstrate its political supremacy
in the ensuing election.
(c) House of Peers.
Accorain to press reports, keen resentment was felt
by the House of Peers over the failure of Premier Okada
to appoint any of its members to his Cabinet. The Tokyo

as having said that the Premier "showed a woeful lack of
respect for the House of Peers when he did not try to take
Peers into his Cabinet, but instead offered posts as par-
lianentary vice-ministers to members of the Kenkyukai and
Koseikai, two of the most influential groups in the House
of Peers". This resentment is saià to have been the reason
for the deliberate indifference displ yed by the Peers
when the Okada Cabinet's representativesapproached them
with an offer of anpointments to the secondary positions of
parliamentary vice-ministers and parliamentary counselors.
The Peers are reported to have felt further slighted by
the fact that the Okada Cabinet did not offer them these
secondary appointments until the last minute and did not
interview at first the recognized old leaders of the
Kenkyukai and the Koseakai, but the independent younger groups.
Six members of the House of Peers were finally appointed
as parliamentary vice-ministers and parliamentary counselors
and these men are reputed to wield little influence in the
House of Peers. According to THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 22,

(a) The Diet.
For several months there had been insistent demands
for the calling of a special session of the Diet for the

and the aavent of a new cabinet gave impetus to these
demands. In fct, one of the principal criticisms of the
Okada statement of policy was that no provision was made

or for the relief of the farmers. In response to these
demands and this criticism, Premier Okada is reported y
THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 22 to have made the following

" I note the clamor for a special Diet session to
discuss agrarian problems. But before I can commit
myself I must have a concrete program for the relief
of conditions :mong the agricuitural population.
I will consider calling : session when I have com-
pleted pl ns, ano there will be a session if I see
the ned for it."

The Army
A national defense "state policy" prepared by the
higher officials of the Army was submitted to Premier
Okada by General Hayashi, Minister of War. The principal
items with which the Army's platform deals are, according
to the ASAHI SHIMBUN of July 12, the following:
(1) Internation:l relations- measures to deal with the
forthcoming naval conference and with Japan's
formal with-
drawal from the League of Nations.
(2) Domestic problems - measures designeu for the
"encouragement of the peonle ind the stabilization of the
popular mind in the emergency situation"; relief of the
lower classes ("classes below the midole class"), parti-
cularly the farmers; reform of the educational system;
"thought problems"; official discipline.
(3) The Manchurian Issue- the strengthening of the
triple system of Japanese uthority in Manchuri: and the
Kwantung Leased Territory, the three heads of authority
being the Commander of the Kwantung Army Garrison (who
is, incidentally, for reasons of convenience, Japanese
Ambassedor to "ianchukuo"), the Governor of the Kwantung
Leased Territory, and the President of the South Manchuria
Railway; the abolishment of extraterritoriality; the problem
of transferring administrative rights in the South Man-
churia Railway Zone to "Manchukuo"; the problem of double
nationality of Japanese residents of "Manchukuo".
(4) Armament expansion- the renlenishment of the
standing army and the development of the aviation corps.

for the year-1935-36, the former newspaper believing that
it will exceed ¥ 500,000,000, due mainly to the proposed

"Because the Army has heretofore refrained from

Army air defense manomuvres were held at Osaka, the
industrial center of Japan, on July 26,27,and 28. Navy
bombers attached Osaka and the surrounding districts and
were "repulsed" by army planes,and anti-aircraft guns, the
citizens corps aiding in the defense.
(r) The Navy
Admiral Osumi, the Minister of the Navy, is
credited by the OSAKA MAINICHI of July 14 as having stated
that he would not remain with the Okada Cabinet if even
the smallest :lteration should be made in the two funda-
mental principles of naval policy adopted during the Saito
régime , namely, the abrogation of the existing 5:5:3 naval
ratio and the completion of the remaining part of the first
replenishment lan nd of the entire second renlenishment
plan*. The press renorted that on July 8, in trial manoeuvres
reminiscent of the recent passage of the American fleet
through the Prnama Canal, the Japanese Combined Fleet
consisting of 64 w rships head n sed through Shimonoseki
Straits, between Shimonoseki :nd oji, in the space of one
hour ano forty minutes.

DNFIDENTIAL
GOVERNMENT FINANCE:
Mr. Fujii, the new Minister of Finance, has difficulties
before him, the two principal ones being the probable demands
of the Army and Navy Ministries for greatly increased budgets
and the probability that the appropriation of emergency funds
for farm relief undertakings will become necessary. The
general opinion is that the present Minister of Finance will fol-
low the financial policies of his predecessor, Mr. Taka-
hashi, who is understood to have recommended Mr. Fujii for
appointment as Finance Minister. The TOKYO ASAHI of July 22
reported that the Ministry of Finance had contrived to cut
the 1935-36 budget by ¥ 600,000,000 out of the total sum of
000
¥ 2,700,O00 requested by the various Ministries. It is
doubtful whether such a decrease in the budget could be effected,
inasmuch as the Ministries of War and Navy, which have
heretofore had pretty much their own way in the matter of
"defense funds", are expected to demand a total appropriation
of ¥ 1,200,000,000 (about $360,000,000), the Army ¥500,000,000
and the Navy ¥ 700,000,000.
It is not feasible at present for the Government to
incur taxes to any apreciable extent, lthough greatly
increased Government expenuitures will probably take place*.
These increased budget deficits combined with growing foreign
restrictions against Japanese exports present an important
future threat to the stability of the yen, according to
the opinion of Professor Rogers, who has made : stady of
financial conditions in Japan for the Treasury Department of
the United States.**
* Embassy's telegram No. 156 of July 12, 4 p.m.
** Embassy's telegram No. 156 of July 1, 4 p.m.

GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS:
(a) Foreign Trade.
According to the preliminary figures, Japan's
exports for July were ¥179,306,000 (approximately
53,791,800) and imports were ¥ 169,430,000. Thus exports
decreased by ¥ 6,498,000 and imports decreased by
¥ 34,077,000. While there was an unfavorable trade balance
of ¥28,028,000 in May and of ¥17,703,000 in June, there
was a favorable balance of ¥9,876,000 in July.
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE of July 14 states that a telegram
has been received by the Japanese Foreign Office from the
Japanese Embassy in Paris to the effect that there is an in-
creasing feeling of alarm :mong French people over the
progress that Japanese products are making in world markets
and that the following suggestions are being made in France
to counteract Japanes exports:
1. Together with other European countries whose labour
conditions are higher than those in Japan, France
shoulo form a united front.
2. The European people should abandon class demagogy,
and capital and labour should cooperate in facing
Japan.
3. Like other countries, France should take a policy
to restrict Japanese goods in her colonies.
4. France should lighten the tax and social insurance
burden ana remove all handicaps from her competition
with Japan.
According to this newspaper, the Foreign office has received
from the Japanese Consul in Lyon a telegram in which it was
stated that there were indications in Lyon that the imports
of Japanese silk into France would be restricted and the imports
of

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
44
Italian silk increased.
With regard to trade with Great Britain's colonies
and dominions, Rengo announced during the month that
members of the Board of Ministers of Ceylon had declined
to introduce an anti-Japanese quota system and that New
Zealand would not select Japanese imports for special
discriminatory treatment.
(b) Farming Problems.
Despite the desire of the farmers end the Seiyukai
for the convocation of a special session of the Diet for relief
legislation to benefit the agricultural population, the
Cabinet has, according to newspaper renorts, decided not to
call a special sessi n of the Diet but is considering
emergency rice and silk cocoon me sures without legi lation
by the Diet.
THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 30 reports that the
Department of Agriculture nd Forestry has re ched a definite
decision to release rice from Government warehouses, where
it has been stored as  me ns of ruising the rice, in
order th t peonle suffering from the cute shortage of
rice may be fed. Mr. Y: me zaki, the new Minister of Agri-
culture and Forestry, is renorted to have made the following
statement to newspaper corresnondents apronos of the silk
crisis: "Personally, I do not think things are so critical
and pressing. I can hardly see the necessity for a special
Diet session to discuss measures for the new cocoon season".
The Japan Chamber of Commerce and Industry, taking a more
serious view of the silk situation, is reported by the
press to have decided on the following recommendations to
be presented to the Government:
1.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
45
1. Lowering of the cost of production .
This is liable to result in the overpro-
duction of mulberry leaves and yarn so that
measures must be devised to utilize the land
and labour in excess.
2. Enhancement of quality.
3. Stabilization of prices.
4. Proper control of production and marketing.
Control of production shall be made the main
item and marketing the auxiliary. Excessive
control is liable to hamper the progress of the
industry. It shall be conducted in such a manner
s will reduce the cost of production, and stabilize
the market.
5. Exploitation of new markets :nd new uses.
6. Cooperation of cocoon raisers, reelers and
dealers.
7. Administrative reaujustment. Matters relating
to sales and exports shall be transferred to the
Department of Commerce and Industry.
8. Appointment of a Silk Commission.
Industries.
(1). Aviation.
The HOCHI SHI BUN of July 19 states that on
the eve of the 1955-36 international crisis the Navy Depart-
ment has decided to promote the airolane manufacturing
industry by lending encourgement to the various manufac-
turing companies. This newsoeper re orts that in response
to the Navy Dep rtment's nolicy, the Tshika aj ma Airplane
Manufacturing Company has decided to establish : sister
company to be known as the Japan Air lane Company, and that
the K wasaki Dockyard Company, the Mitsubishi Heavy In-
dustry Company, no the Nakajima Airnlane Works re also
expanding their airplane lants under the encouragement
of the Navy Department.
Preparatory to the proposed inauguration of the civil
air line bet ween Taiwan nd J pan proper, a Japan air
Transport Company Fokker plane is reported to have made a
test flight on July 5, covering the distance of 931 miles
between Tachiarai, in Fukuoka Prefecture, Japan, and Taihoku
in Taiwan, in 7 hours and 20 minutes.

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)

(2) Shipping.
The ASAHI SHIMBUN of July 15 states that upon
completion of superior freight vessels through the ship
improvement aid granted by the Government, it is likely
that Japan will "grasp the supremacy of shipping in the
Pacific". According to this newspaper, in the shipning
trade between the Philipoines and the United States, Great
Britain used to rank first, with the United States second
and Japan third, but the order has changed to Japan first,
the United States next, nd Great Britain third.
(3)
The Sulphur Inaustry.
There has been recently a great increase in the
Japanese sulphur output, according to THE JAPAN TIMES of
July 14, which states that 104,000 metric tons of sul-
phur were produced in 1933 and that 140,00 metric tons
are expected to be roduced auring the present calendar
year. It is ointed out by this newsnaper that over
70 per cent of the entire sulphur oroduction is consumed
in Japan and that the increased domestic consumption
is due lrgely to the expansion of the rayon and paper
milling industries.
(d)Financial Trends.
The change of Cabinet had very little effect on
financial matters, most observers being of the opinion
that the Okada Cabinet will follow the financial policies
of Mr. Takahashi, Minister of Finance in the Saito Cabinet.

LABOR AND EMIGRATION:
(a) Labor.
More than 1000 workmen of the Osaka Mechanical
Works (Osaka Kikai Kosakusho), which manufactures various
kinds of machinery, went on strike on July 9 after their
demands for increased wages and other concessions had been
rejected by this concern. The employees and emoloyers
agr ed, however, to declare a truce from July 26 to July
28, inclusive, in order that both laborers and capitalists
might participate in the airplane manoeuvres at Osaka.*
According to the OSAKA ASAHI of July 20, representatives
of several patriotic leagues visited the Os ka Mechanfcal
Works and advised the chief officials to settle the strike
as soon as possible in view of the possible "1935-36 crisis",
since the Works was executing oroers of the Army and Navy.
On July 26 about fifty strikers demonstrated in front of the
Works and were arrested by the oolice but released on the
same day.
THE
JAPAN CHRONICLE of July 24 reported that labor
disputes were increasing in J: pan and that to meet this
problem the Department of Home Affairs was going to intro-
duce at the next Diet session a bill providing for compulsory
Government intervention in labor disputes, not only in
public utilities enterprises but also in private enterprises
when such disputes are considered harmful to industry or
the public interest.
(b) Emigration.
At the July 17 session of the American-Japan Student
Conference

onference in Tokyo the Japanese delegates are reported
to have stated that the problem of population was the most

had been suggested by the merican delegates as a remedy

deep-rooted family system in Japan; and that emigration to
Korea would be the best solution of the problem, the
Japanese replacing Koreans who could emigrate to "Man-
chukuo" where the standarc: of living re too low for
the Japanese but suited to the Koreans.

f. Japanese Reaction to Secretary Swanson's statement
in favor of a Reduotion of Naya Argenenta by 20
Per Cent on the Basis of the 5: 5:3 Ratio.
Several Japanese newspapers assailed the statement that
Secretary Swanson is reported to have made in a newspaper
interview on August 1 to the effect that he had not changed
his opinion concerning the necessity of the preservation of
the present 5:5:3 ratio and that he will recommend an all-
around 20 per cent naval reduction. According to prees
reports, Japanese naval circles stated that the proposal
of the Secretary of the Navy of the United States was
absolutely unacceptable because Japan would in the event
of such a reduction be compelled to scrap recently built
moderm vessels while the United States could dispense easily
with its over-age vessels, and also because a 20 per cent
reduction with the maintenance of the 5:5:3 ratio would
bring about a relatively greater weakening of the Japanese

NICHI SHIMBUN points out that Japanese raw silk is allowed

and quotas on other Japanese imports if Japan should reject
the French "demand". This newspaper believes that the "anta-
gonistic attitude of the French Government" is due partly to
the instigation of Great Britain and Italy and partly to the
fact that the excess of Japanese imports into France over
French exports to Japan amounted to over ¥ 10,000,000 (about
8,000,000) during the first six months of the present calendar
year.

some time been strongly opposed to unrestricted imports of
Japanese unbleached cotton goods, considering the quantity
of these imports excessive during recent months.

GDA.
Soviet Russias
The month of August was an important one in
relations between Japan and Soviet Russia. During this
month negotiations for the sale of the Chinese Eastern
Railway came to a halt once more with, however, pros-
pects of ultimate success, and the "Manchukuo" policy
of sabotage against the Soviet employees of the railway
nearly led to a serious crisis in Soviet-Japanese re-
lations.
On August 4 sabotage against the railway ap-
parently recommenced with the arrest of the (Soviet)
Assistant Station Master at Harhin *. A few days later,
on the l0th and 13th, several important conversations took
place in Tokyo between the Soviet Ambassador and Foreigm
Minister Hirota, betweem Mr. Hirota and Mr. Ohashi,
V1ce
* Embassy's despatch No. 935, of August 23, 1934.

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.98)
19
Vsee Minister for Foreig Affairs of "Memebaimo", and
between Mr. Ohashi and the Soviet Ambassador. The upshot
of these conversations was that Mr. Yureneff (The Soviet
Ambassador) again refused the Japanese-drafted "Manchskuo"
proposal of July 25, and Mr. Ohashi left Tokyo, making
it clear, nevertheless, that his action was not to be
interpreted as final disruption of the negotiations. The
vernacular press published extended and partially imagina-
tive accounts of these interviews and declared that "of-
ficial circles" were of the opinion that the Soviets wished
to prolong negotiations until Japan had become involved
in the "crisis of 1935-1936". There followed Soviet accu-
sations that Japan was planning to seize the railroad
and several despatches from Manchuria alleging that a "huge,
anti-Japanese, anti-Manchukuo plot"had been unearthed.
"Manchukuo" further announced in a formal statement of
policy that "Manchukuo" could make no further concession,
that Soviet Russia must bear the responsibility for the
failure of negotiations, and that "Manchukuo" intended
to regard the Chinese Eastern Railway as a commercial
organization under her jurisdiction.
On August 18, to the apparent astonishment of the
Japanese, the first news arrived that the Soviets had
released in Moscow the details* of the recent negotiations
for the sale of the railroad although it had been agreed
last Mareh that no details were to be given out. The Soviet
report was not, however, published in full until August
21 when a long standing press ban was lifted and the Japa-
nese Foreign Office, in its turn, released a commmique**
narrating
* Inclosure No. 1, Mmbassy's despatch No. 935, August 23, 193
2,

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
20
sreting the course of the negetiations. Waile both
stetements quite maturally attempted to place as favor-
able a oemstruction as possible upon the actions of the
respostive governments issuing them, they agreed on
all important points. There now remains a difference of
omly 340,000,000 between the "Manchukuo" b1d of ¥120,000,000
and the Soviet offering price of ¥160,000,000. In addi-
tion, 1t appears, "Manchukuo" 1s willing to pay 80,000,000
in discharge allowances to Soviet employees while Soviet
Russia is willing to receive two thirds of the purchase
price proper in Japanese goods to be evaluated in a manner
apparently still undecided.
The reaction in Japan to this startling development
was indignant. It is necessary to remember, however, that
the "Manchukuo" authrities were at that time in the midst
of a campaign to arrest Soviet employees of the railroad
on suspicion of complicity in the recent adcidents along
the eastern branch of the line, having arrested 19 men
on August 15. In addition there were the usual rumors
of the extent and efficiency of Soviet military prepara-
tions, and petty incidents, such as alleged insults to
Consul General Slavutsky who was proceeding to Moscow on
leave, were continually occurring. Accordingly the Japa-
nese press failed to seize upon the encouraging fact that
only a difference of ¥40,000,000 now prevented a successful
sale of the railroad but rather, on the other hand, empha-
sized the bad faith of the Soviets in giving out the con-
fidential details of the negotiations. For several days
the

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
Rassian situation was given the place of greetest
prominence and virtually all the papers commented edi-
terially. Two ideas predominated in these editerials,
first, that the question was of importance to the "pease
of East Asia", and second, doubt as to whether Soviet
Russia really ever intended to sell the railroed. of
constructive or really reflective comment there was
nono.
The reaction of the army was restrained but defi-
nite. With the apparent failure of the negotiations
sponsored by the Foreim Uffice it was felt in Tokyo
that it would only be natural for the Kwantung Army
to gain control, temporarily at least, of the Japanese-
"Manchukuo" policy towards the railroad- hence the
arrests of Soviet officials of the railroad. The War
Office spokesman gave out several statements indicating
that the Army intended to take a firm stand. He linked
the Soviet attitude towards the sale of the railroad
with a list of 15 border incidents* which had occurred
since the beginning of the year, and charged Soviot
officials with instigating 13 accidents which occurred
on the eastern branch of the line between April 17 and
July 25. Although the Army appeared dissatisfied, as
usual, with the attitude of the Japanese Foreim Office,
there were no threats that the Army would take matters
into its own hands. However, the Minister of War, General
Hayashi, told the press that he deemed the question of such
seriousness that all Cabinet members should be kept in
close touch with it.
The

The Fereign Office was, of course, somembet em-

the Sovtet 's breach of confidence, and seeking to place
the Soviet attitude during the negotiations in as un-
favorable light as possible.
At this time it was generally felt in Tokyo that
Soviet-Japanese relations had entered one of the period-
ical phases of increased tension, but within a few days
more serious events occurred and added to the cumulative
anxiety. It was revealed in Tokyo on the afternoon of
August 24 that the Soviet Ambassador had two days pre-
viously handed to the Foreign Minister a note of protest
concerning the arrest of Soviet officials of the Chinese
Bastern

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
23
Bastern Railroad which was expressed in language far
stronger even than the terms customary to Soviet

justify these lawless and causeless arrests of Soviet
employees, nonsensical accusations have been fabricated
alleging organized attacks on a Japanese military mission
and bandit attacks on trains and allegations that they
also organised train wrecks and conducted robberies,

circles". No formal reply was made to this protest during
August, but the Foreign Office spokesman indicated that
the whole question in the opinion of the Japanese Govern-
mont was a judicial issue of "Manchukuo" and therefore
did not directly concern Japan.
If, however, the Foreign Office did not reply to
the Soviet protest, immediate action, whether related
or not, was taken in Manchuria because on August 26 a
report reached Tokyo that 70 additional Soviet employees
of the railroad had been arrested in connection with al-
leged railroad plots. To some observers it appeared as
though a Soviet ultimatum might be the next step, but
fortunately the mutual and dangerously irresponsible
recriminations ceased, so that, at the end of the month,
the

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)

there were attempts to shift responsibility for this ac-
cident to the Soviet railroad officials.
While the Chinese Eastern Railroad situation was the
principal issue between the two countries during August,
the progress of the "Manchukuo"-Soviet Waterway Conference
was a topic of some interest in Japan. On August 27 a

reached providing for a joint technical commission to ad-
minister the waterways in question. There has as yet been
no public discussion of the problem as to whether Soviet
Russia's signature on this document will constitute re-
cogmition of "Manchukuo" or not. It is expected that it
will be signed early in September.
On August 15 the Japanese press published a report

likely- a revised rate should be agreed upon before the

In conclusion, the past month has witnessed twe ex-
ccedingly important developments in relations between the
Ussm and Japan. In the first place the negotiations for
the sale of the Chinese Eastern Railroad have brought the
two parties so close to agreement on a price that it seems
as though this problem would in time be successfully eli-
minated as one of the possible causes of hostilities between
Japan and Soviet Russia. In support of this opinion the
Embassy has received information through a reliable source
that the Soviet side is now more optimistic and that, while
not prepared to compromise further at present, would probably
reciprocate concessions initiated by the "Manchukuo" side
later on. The matter, it is believed, has now become largely
one of prestige to the Soviets. In the second place Japan
and the UssR were confronted with the near approach of a
serious crisis towards the end of August, and it became
evident that neither side was willing to face a showdom
at that time. Nor, it may be observed, has the continuance
of the controversy or the language in which it has been
disoussed , aroused the violent feelings which might have
been precipitated in Japan.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS:
a. Cabinet.
1. Bepart that the Cabinet is Considering
Batablishment of e Brain Trust.
The Cabinet is reported by the press to be con-
sidering the establishment of an advisory body in the nature
34.002
of a brain trust along lines different from President Roose-
velt's so-called "brain trust". The principal functions of
such an organization would, it is said, be the formulation of
national policies and the giving of advice to the Premier.
The OSAkA ASAHI believes that a brain trust like that of
President Roosevelt may be of some value as an advisory
body on purely technical matters but that it can do no
possible good as a political organ concerned with matters
which have to do with man and society.
2. Disagreement Among Cabinet Ministers Over
Various Issues.
There are, according to reports, several out-
standing differences among various Cabinet Ministers. The
Navy and War Ministers have been insisting on early abroga-
tion of the Washington Naval Treaty, while the Foreign
Minister has strongly opposed them, asserting that if notice
should
of abrogation is eventually decided upon, such action be
postponed until the October conversations in London have been
held.* The Ministers for War, Foreign Affairs, and Overseas
Affairs have been unable to come to an accord on the question
of unifying the present tripartite system of representation and
administration in Manchuria.** According to statements
attributed
* Embassy's despatch No. 939 of August 24, 1934.
** See Section If of this report, "Manchukuo".

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
28
attributed to him by the press, the Minister for Finance
is already at variance with the demands of the Ministers
for War and Navy for greatly increased appropriations
in the National Budget for 1935-36. It is doubtful
whether Mr. Fujii will be able to stand out against the
naval and military demands when the Budget is eventually
compiled. In addition to the disagreement between the
Navy and War Ministers on the one hand and the Foreign
Minister on the other over the question of abrogation of
the Washington Naval Conference, the TEITO NICHI NICHI
of August 20 reports that a feeling of discontent with the
Foreign Office is daily growing stronger in the War
Ministry, which advocates stronger action against Soviet
Russia and is highly displeased with the "peace-at-any-
price principle " of the Foreign Office.
It is well known that there are two camps definitely
opposed to each other within the Ministry for Foreign
Affairs; the liberal, moderate group headed by Mr. Hirota,
and the chauvinist group, which is headed by Mr. Shige-
mitsu, Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs,and which includes
in its ranks Mr. Amau, Director of the Bureau of Information
and Intelligence, and Mr. Kuriyama, Director of the Treaty
Bureau. The lattergroup has the support of the War and
Navy Ministries. There havebeen newspaper reports to the
effect that a shake-up in the Foreign Office is being
contemplated, the implication being that those opposed
to the Foreign Minister's policies will have to go. It
is unlikely, however, that the War and Navy Ministries
would agree to the dismissal of the chauvinist element
from their important positions in the Foreign Office.

S. Caneral Havashi's Statements Concerning The
Anmy's Budget Damands.
The ASAHI of August 9 quotes General Hayashi,
Minister of War, in an interview said to have been given
by him on August 10, as follows apropos of the Army's
demands for appropriations in the Budget for 1935-86:
" The Army wants to strengthen national defense,
but it cannot ignore the ability of the nation to
stand the burden. The budget demands are going to be
for indispensable amounts that will permit of no
bargaining. If the Finance Ministry finds the demands
objectionable in any way, I may make proposals
about financial matters, such as a suggestion that
taxes be increased. The present state of affairs
makes it imperative to realize our plans for the
strengthening of national defense at any cost."
According to the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of August 9, General
Hayashi told newspaper correspondents that, if the Ministry
for Finance should find it impossible to make available to
the Army the amount essential for national defense, the
Army would be obliged to make known its views.

I can say
nothing officially yet as to the Navy budget for the next

e. Reactionaries.
According to THE JAPAN TIMES of August 28, the
Procurator of the Tokyo District Court on August 28
demanded death sentences for the following members of the
Ketsumeidan or "Blood Brotherhood", a reactionary organi-
zation which plotted the overthrow of the form of govern-
ment in Japan in 1932: Nissho Inouye, leader of the or-
ganization; Tadashi Konuma, who in February, 1932, as-
sassinated Junnosuke Inouye, the then Finance Minister;
Goro Hishinuma, who in March, 1932, assassinated Baron
Takuma Dan, Managing Director of the Mitsui interests; and
Biji Furuchi, who acted as Nissho Inouye's right-hand
man in plotting the overthrow of the Government. The
Procurator is stated to have demanded sentences ranging
from life imprisonment to 7 years for the remaining
10 members of the Blood Brotherhood. This newspaper
also reports that the Procurator, in asking for the above-
mentioned

f. Commim

GOVERNMENT FINASCS:
Mr. Sedanobu Pajii, the mew Minister for Finanee,
is cenfronted with great difficulties in determining Japem's
future financial policy and furthermore, according to pre-
sent indications, he will probably be faced with even
greater diffioulties if be attempts to carry out a policy
94.51
of retrenchment. He has stated on several occasions
that he will in general pursue the poliey of his prede-
cessor, Mr. Takahashi. While Mr. Fujii has not declared
himself definitely on the question of taxation, the Premier
has several times expressed the belief that the time is
not yet ripe for an increase in taxes and the Finance
Minister himself recently stated that there are other
ways of raising revenue than increasing taxes and that
it is impossible now to state definitely whether taxes
will be raized or lowered for the next fiscal year. The
Finance Minister has indicated that he will attempt to
limit as much as possible the issue of "red ink bonds"
or bonds to cover budget deficits. He has said that he
has not considered the conversion of 5 per cent Govern-
ment bonds to a lower interest rate, despite newspaper
talk to the effect that the Finance Ministry would re-
sort to this step in order that Government expenditures
might be thereby reduced. It has been asserted more then
once by the Finance Minister that the budget will be
drastically reduced for the fiscal year 1935-36, yet the
estimated demands of the various Ministries amount to a
total of ¥2,800,000,000, an 1ncrease of nearly ¥700,000,000
(about $210,000,000) over the actual 1934-35 expenditures.
81nce

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
Sinee the largest items in the Japanese badget are
alseys the appropriations for the Navy and War Mimis-
tries, any material reduction effected would entail cuts
in these appropriations and Mr. Fujii would, of course,
meet with violent opposition from the naval and military
authorities in the event of an attempt on his part to
curtail the naval and military expenditures.* According
to the JIJI SHIMPO of August 25, the Finance Minister,
in an interview with a correspondent, emphasized the
necessity of drastic budget cuts in the absence of any
need to prepare for an immediate war, stating that there
was no reason to believe that the 1935-36 *emergency
period" meant actual danger of conflict. As stated elsewhere
in this report, there appears to be some doubt as to whother
Mr. Fujii will be in a position to resist the demands
of the Navy and Army for increased appropriations. If
he is unable to withstand these demands, he will be faced
with the necessity of maintaining at least the present
large budget. At the same time, it would appear from his
statements and those of the Premier that the Government
does not intend to employ such means of raising revenue
to offset the large expenditures as the issue of red
ink bonds", the lowering of the interest rate on 5 per cont
Governmentbonds, and an increase in taxes. It remains to
be seen just what method the Minister of Finance will adopt
to attempt to extricate the Government from Its difficult
financial position.

GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS:
a. The Arricultural Situation.
The agricultural situation during the month was
characterized by a continued decline in the price of
silk cocoons and a continued increase in the price of rice.
The fall in cocoon prices naturally has affected cocoon
farmers adversely and the rise in rice prices has caused
increased hardships to the already tax-burdened farmers
for the reasons that in most cases their rice crops had
already been sold at a lower price than now prevails, and
they arenow compelled to buy rice at the higher figure
in order merely to subsist, and when rice brokers make
their bids for the next crop the price of rice will
probably have fallen. The TOKYO NICHI NICHI and the
HOCHI point out that the price of rice is now higher
than that which prevailed at the time of the rice riot
in 1918, when large rice concerns were suspected of
cornering this product and rice storehouses were looted.
A poor rice crop and a consequent shortage of this food
for the farmers' consumption may result from the severe
droughts in certain rice-growing districts and the floods
in other districts.
In response to insistent requests, the Government
on August 3 approved a program of relief for raw silk
producers which called for an expenditure of ¥3,000,000
(about $900,000). The Seiyukai denounced this Government
measure as inadequate, stating that the fall in the price
of cocoons since the spring of this year had reduced the
total

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.99)
37
total incomes of the cocoon farmers by ¥850,000,000.
Leaders of the Kokumin Domei are reported by the JAPAN
CHRONICLE to have called on the Premier and declared
the Government's relief program inadequate. Subsequently
the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry announced that
he had drafted a plan for the relief of distressed seri-
culturists which would require the expenditure of
¥ 35,800,000 (about $10,740,000) in three years. He stated
that he would demand ¥13,500,000 (about 4,050,000) for
such relief in the budget for l935-36. According to the
newspapers, on August 8 Count Kuroki, Director of the
Silk and Cocoon Association, submitted a request to the
War Minister that a small percentage of the 1935-36 Army
appropriations be diverted to the relief of sericulturists,
and on August 10 made a similar request of the Navy Minister.
THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 16 stated that the War Minister
could not summarily reject this plea on account of the
Army's contention that the agricultural population is the
backbone of the Japanese Army. The JIJI states that General
Hayashi"listened with sympathy" to the plea of the cocoon
raisers and said that he woulc do his best in the direction
of promoting agrarian interests. In the opinion of the
CHUGAI, the War Minister will probably propose additional
budgetary expenditures for relief of the agricultural popu-
lation without reduction of the military estimates. A less
evasive answer was given to the request of the sflk and
Gocoon Association by the Minister of the Navy, according
to the Japanese language newspapers, which quoted Admiral
Osumi as having said that if the Ministry for Agriculture

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.99)

nd Forestry produced a proper relief program, the Wavy
Ministry might set aside a certain sum from its appro-
priation for the next fiscal year to relieve the plight
of the silk cocoon growers.

d.Financial Trends.
The finasial market in August was featured by the
continued downward trend of Government bonds, which
is attributed to the uncertainty of the Government's
monetary and bond policy and to heavy sales of spe-
culation security holdings as a result of the tight
short term money market. Stock market prices were weak,
since praders were awaiting the development of the
consideration of the 1935-36 budget by the Finance
Ministry. The short term money mmarket rates were the
highest that they have been since 1932.

November 16, 1934.
Tokyo's political report for
September, 1934.
Anti-Japanese demonstrations in
Arizona (page 1) are arousing strong re-
sentment in Japan. It is my own opinion
that nothing more likely to arouse Japanese
feeling against the United States has
occurred since the exclusion act. Japanese
can understand and appreciate differences
of opinion over policies, naval ratios, etc.,
but discrimination against their race
abroad touches their most sensitive nerve.
of course thinking people recognize the
problems involved.
The section on "U.S. Naval Plans
Disturb Japan" (page 8) should be read.
In connection with naval affairs also
see "Japanese Developments Prior to Naval
Conversations" (page 40) and "The Navy"
(page 45). Press reports state that
Admiral Suetsugu, arch opponent of naval
limitation, is to become member of Supreme
Military Council in December. This is
probably another step by the Navy to
insure the success of its plans for the
Japproaching naval conference.
