RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES
(a) Arisona Farming Diffionlty
The outbreak of anti-Japanese demonstrations in the
Salt River Valley of Arizona has caused a good deal of
excitement and newspaper publicity in Japan. Up to the
end of October the editorial comnents were few, but as
further activity on the part of the farmers against the
Japanese residents makes itself evident many unfavorable
comments are to be expected. However, with the assurance
of the Department of State that the Arizona authorities are
doing all in their power to maintain order, some of the
newspapers are of the opinion that justice will probably
be done to the Japanese farmers the native Americans are
trying to eject. Because there have been similar action
and threats of damage to foreigners other than Japanese
there has appeared little or no press comment to the ef-
fect that race prejudice has attended the so-called outrages.
Some of the news items infer that the bombing of Japanese
property was carried out by irresponsible persons, and does
not have the support of the solid citizens of the community,
although they report the unpopularity of the prosperous Japan-
ese farmers with the impoverished American land owmers.
The Foreign Office spokesman, according to the JAPAN TINES
of September 26, informed the Foreign correspondents here re-
garding the developments of the case, recounting the activities
of Mr. Fujii, Chargé d'Affaires ad interim, and the protests
of Mr. Hori, Japanese Consul in Los Angeles, and stated that
he was sure the American authorities could cope with the situ-
ation

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Hon. He said that the Japenese Government and people
were concerned over the case, and were trusting to the jus-
tice and fairness of the American Government and people
for an amicable settlement. The spokesman intimated that
American reaction to similar acts of violence to American
residents in Japan would probably be stronger, and reminded
the correspondents that when damage was done to certain
American property in Korea during the anti-Chinese movement
there, the United States Government lodged a strong protest
with the Japanese Government.
In evidence of the excitement caused in Japan by the
Salt Riverx Valley disturbances, it may be mentioned that
no less than three distinct an separate groups of poung
men called at the Embassy on September 25 and 26 to pro-
test against the mistreatment of their compatriots in Arizona.
They represented various branches of young men's patriotic
societies. Two of them were orderly and their spokesmen
made polite speeches of protest to the Chargé d'Affaires
but the spokesman of the largest group became somewhat voci-
ferous and disrepsectful whereupon the Chargé politely re-
fused to listen further. Each group wished to present a
written protest, but they were informed that the Embassy
could not receive them, and left. The authorities have made
efforts to prevent demonstrations.
(b) American Journalists Royally Welcomed in Japan
A party of fifteen American journalists with their
families arrived in Japan on September 18 as guests of the
Japan Press Association for the purpose of getting acquainted
1th

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
th the Japanese people, of sightseeing and of observing
at first hand how the people live and what their problems
are. The party was composed of editors, publishers, heads
of news syndicates and columnists, all having prominent
positions, and representing varied opinions and outlooks
on national and international questions. Only one member
had visited Japan before, and according to the spokesman
of the party, Mr. Lowell Mellet, editor of the Washington
News, they had not come to Japan to be interviewed, but
to interview and learn a few things about the Far East
through direct contact with Japan and leaders of Japanese
thought. "And so we have no opinions", he said on arriv-
ing at the dock, "We are merely student journalists who
have come here with the sincere intention of observing
and learning all that we can with open minds, that we
maycomprehend the many problems that are Japan's and that
are mutual to both Japan and the United States. We are only
too glad, if opinions must be given, to say that we are
extremely happy to have the opportunity to learn and to meet
the Japanese people.
Additional color was added to the reception of the
American journalists because of the arrival on the same
vessel of Prince and Princess Kaya from a seven months tour
of the world. Although almost overwhelmed by the Japanese
reporters on landing, the party declined to give individual
interviews, particularly on subjects of an international
nature, and thereby made a favorable impression upon the
press and public. As their visit lengthened they evidently
convinced the Japanese that they really were serious in their
desire to learn and to observe from as impartial a viewpoint

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
possible, as well as to refrain from making extrava-
gant statements so often made by foreign visitors.
The press party included Mr. Carroll Binder, assistant
to the publisher of the CHICAGO DAILY NEWS,and Mrs. Binder;
Mr. William Burkhardt, chief editor of the SAN PRANCIsCO
NEws, and Mrs. Burkhardt; Mr. George T. Cameron, president
and publisher of the SAN FRANCISCO CHRONICLE, and Mrs. Cameron;
Mr. J. Roscoe Drummond, executive editor of the CHRIsTIAN
SCIENCE MONITOR in Boston, and Mrs. Drummond; Mr. Edmund
Gilligan, foreign editor of the NEW YORK SUN, and Mrs.
Gilligan; Mr. James Hammond, president and publisher of the
COMMERCIAL APPEAL in Memphis, and Mrs. Hammond; Mr. Philip
Jackson, publisher of the PORTLAND JOURNAL, and Mrs. Jackson;
Mr. William Lydgate, editor of the TIME INC., and Mrs. Lydgate;
Mrs Lowell Mellet, editor of the WASHINGTON NEWS and leader
of the party, and Mrs. Mellet; Mr. B. Milliard, editorial
writer of the LOS ANGELES TIMES, and his sister, Mrs. Gladys
Dwiggins; Mr. Elzey M. Roberts, publisher of the ST. LOUIS
STAR, and his son, Elzey M. Roberts, Jr.; Mr. Arthur Sinnott,
chief editor of the NEWARK EVENING NEWS, and Mrs. Sinnott; Mr.
L.P. Thurston, president and general manager of theHONOLULU
ADVERTISER, and Mrs. Thurston; Mr. Frank Tripp, general manager
of theGANNETT chain of 18 newspapers; and Mr. James T. Williams,
Jr., editorial contributor on American affairs and foreim
relations for the HEARST newspapers. Mr. Roy Howard, chairman
of the board of directors of the SCRIPPS-HOWARD Newspapers,
accompanied the party as far as Honolulu.
The itinerary planned by the Japanese Press Association
for their distinguished visitors was the following:
Begianing September 19 with am afssrnoon reception given

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
for the journalists by the American Ambassador, at which
they met many prominent Japanese officials, newspaper and
business men, they were given a welcoming banquet by the
Press Association, attended a press conference, were re-
ceived by the Premier at his official residence, toured
the city, went to tea at the Foreign Minister's residence,
attended a reception given by the Tokyo ASAII SHIMBUN, were
given a tea by the America-Japan Society, and a theatre
party by the YOMIURI SHIMBUN. They then were conducted
on a trip to Nikko. Upon their return they visited the
HOCHI newspaper plant, met business men at the Industrial
Club, were given a banquet by the Chamber of Commerce, held
a conference with leading statesmen, attended a formal lun-
cheon offered by the Mitsui family, a tea given by the JIJI
SHIMPO, a banquet by the Tokyo NICHI NICHI, and an enter-
tainment offered by the Iwasaki family. After inspecting
the Imperial Bardens in Shinjuku, the party left on Septem-
ber 27 for Hakone and other scenic points near Mount Fuji.
On their return the 29th they were entertained by the Navy
Minister, and the next day visited several Shrines. They
then went to Nagoya where they were given further entertain-
ment, and on October 2 will leave for Korea. They expect
to tour Manchuria, visiting the Capital at Hsinking, and to
return to Japan on October 22. They will then sail for the
United States on October 25.
During the many dinners given the party of journalists
speeches of welcome were made by the most prominent Japanese:
Admiral Okada, the Premier, Imperial Prince Higashikuni,
Honorary President of the Press Association, Count Kiyoura,
President, the Foreign Office spokesman, prominent editors
ana

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
and business men. They all were impressed, said the Pre-
mier, with the fact that the visiting Americans represented
the most influential organs of public opinion in the United
States. That was apparently true because no oportunity was
lost to urge the visitors to learn all they could about
Japan's development, to ask questions with the view toward
correcting any wrong impressions they may have harbored,
and to see everything they possibly could. All speakers at-
tempted to give the journalists their interpretation of
what Japan is striving for internationally, and stressed
the point that inasmuch as most Americans did not under-
stand the "Japanese position in the Far East", they, the
visitors, could do untold good toward improving international
relations through telling the American public of the täings
they learned on their tour of Japan.
The journalists appeared to be very much impressed
with the excellent hospitality of the Japanese, and expressed
their gratitude in glowing terms. …r. Mellet and Mr. Cameron
spoke to the nation over the JOAk broadcasting system, ex-
plaining the purpose of their visit to Japan, likening
themselves to explorers on a voyage of discovery, and thank-
ing the public for its warm welcome.
(c) American Oil Interests in Japan
994.6363
CONEHDEHHHAL
The Japanese Petroleum Industry Law enacted last March,
continued to be an unsolvable problem for the American oil
interests in Japan during September. In addition to that
the question regarding enforcement of the petroleum sales
monopoly in Manchuria further obsessed American oil companies.
One of the primary objects of the Petroleum Industry law,
stated Mr. Saka1 of the Department of Commerce and Industry*,
1a
See Embassy's despatoh No. 957 of September 6, 1934.

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
abolish eventually imports of petroleum products
ato Japan. He said that Japanese refiners could not
dispose of all of their products because of the compe-
tition of foreign oil interests, and that it was im-
portant that they be given more opportunity to expand.
Neither the American nor the British oil interests
have thus far been able to obtain sufficiently definite
statements from the Japanese Government regarding future
enforcement or modification of the oil law to enable them
to formulate definite policies toward the Government. They
would like to be assured that some security for the enormous
investment entailed in maintaining six months petroleum
supply on hand, will be guaranteed. In like manner they
would like to know that the Government means shortly to
eliminate entirely foreign oil companies from Japan, in order
that they could decide whether to withdraw at once without
spending any more money on costly installations.
The British and Netherlands Governments are concerned
over the petroleum law, and their diplomatic representatives,
as well as the American Embassy, have made representations
to the Foreign Office stating their Government's positions
regarding the Japanese law. (See Embassy's Despatch No. 984
of September 21, 1934). The foreign oil companies have not
thus far complied with the onerous provisions of the law re-
garding holding large stocks in Japan because of their un-
certainty of the future. The Government on its part is
hesitant eBout being too severe on the foreign companies
because of its fear that they might be able to place a suc-
cessful embargo on exports to Japan, and because Japanese
companies, which are encouraged by the new law to build
refineries

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
refineries, cannot support the tremendous financial out-
lay at present. Nor could the Japanese companies comply with
the storage provision of the law without Government aid.
In his interview with the Foreign Office Officials,
the Chargé d'Affaires learned that the Foreign Office had
DHA
counselled the other Government Departments to proceed
very slowly with enforcement of the Petroleum Law in order
not to incite retaliatory measures by foreign Governments.
The Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Of-
fice stated that he did not believe that the enforcement
of the Manchurian oil monopoly was imminent because of the
unsettled con ition of Japanese administration there at
present. The Monopoly Law may be modified to a licensing
system later, but at present it has caused informal pro-
tests to the Japanese Government from foreign Governments
concerned.* American oil companies have not thus far com-
bined to refrain from bidding on contracts offered by the
Manchuria Oil Company. The Foreign Office is apparently
doing what it can to avoid raising the oil question to the
level of an international incident.** It remains to be seen,
however, what action the American, British and Dutch oil
companies will take, and how strong the retaliatory measures
that may be imposad by foreign Governments will be.
(d) U. S. Naval Plans Disturb Japan
According to the JIJI of September 3, the Japanese
Naval authorities have become somewhat perturbed over the
statement attributed to Secretary Swanson that if Japan
abrogated
*Bee Embassy's telegrams Nos. 191 of August 31, 196 of~Seßtember
Se 1934arand,Nos7213 of September 25, 1934.
** See Embassy's despatch No. 956 of September 6, 1934.

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
ebrogated the Washington Treaty the United States would
feel free to proceed with strengthening its Pacific naval
bases, constructing additional ones in Alaska and in the
Aleutian Islands. The JIJI said that the United States
had already sent a fleet of mine sweepers to Alaskan
waters and an Army and Navy air corps to investigate
the possibilities of establishing air and submarine bases
in that region. Instructions to the Japanese delegate
to the London preliminary Naval talks were delayed while
Army and Navy officials conferred over abrogation and
other disarmament qmestions. The Japanese Navy Minister,
the JIJI continued, despatched Vice-Admiral Koichi Shio-
sawa with the combined Fleet to the Kurile Islands for
the purpose of surveying possible sites for one or more
Naval air bases, which they deem necessary if the United
States plans fortifying Alaska and the Aleutian Islands.
The Navy is quoted as saying that it is quite natural that
Japan fortify its northern islands for national defense,
provided the United States carries out fortification plans
in an area so far distant from the mainland, an? so near
to Japan's northern territory. However, the article con-
cluded, that action will be dependent upon similar action
by the United States.
The NICHI NICHI of September 8 quotes the Navy Office
as being unafraid of America's proposed fortifications in
the Aleutians, Alaska, Hawaii and islands near ?anama,
which it says are foregone conclusions if Japan abrogates
the Washington pact. If Japan's new disarmament proposal
is adopted, the United States will be powerless to attack
Japn

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
the NICHI NICHI continues, and therefore Japan
insends to stick to its own proposals, even risking rup-
ture of the conference, as that plan will be best for the
nation.
The proposed maneuvers of the United States fleet
scheduled for next sumuer between Puget Sound, Alaska,
and Hawaii, have also disturbed Japanese officialdom,
according to the ADVERTISER of September 22. That paper
says that the Navy fears that such a show of strength
near to Japan may embolden China and Soviet Russia in
their attitudes toward Japan, and thus make more dif-
ficult Japan's policy of maintaining peace in the Far East.
The article states further that the Navy believes that two
Iarge-scale naval maneuvers by the American Navy within
the next year are proof that the United States' policy
is based on naval strength, and that if the United States
desires to make a show of strength in an effort to intimi-
date Japan on the eve of abrogating the Washington Treaty,
the outlook for the forthcoming negotiations is unfavorable.
(e) Ambassador Saito's Visit to Japan
9411
During his visit to Japan for conferences with the
Foreign Minister and with other Japanese Ambassadors, Mr.
Hiroshi Saito has been very much in the public eye. He
has made many statements and prophecies regarding American-
Japanese relations -- some of his assertions have been con-
sidered extravagant -- and has been highly praised for his
optimistic outlook. In company with Ambassador Sato 
Immmm, Ambassador Saito paid a visit to "Manchukuo" where
he is reported to have spoken about the great change in
American

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
eriean opinion regarding that new country.* He alse
added that the United States had practically abandoned
its "Stimson doctrine", and feared no Soviet-American
joint action against Japan. The Ambassador has been
votüble in extolling the progress already made in Man-
churia, since returning from his visit, and it has been
suggested that his remarks on the subject here are in
the nature of practice and preparation for addresses and
interviews to be delivered upon his return to Washington
early in October.
(f) The American Ambassador and Mrs. Grew Visit Peiping
The Ambassador and Mrs. Grew, accompanied by Mr. Par-
sons, the private secretary, sailed from Kobe September 20
on the S.S. FUSO MARU, boun for Peiping via Dairen. The
Ambassador availed himself of a much needed vacation. All
local English newspapers carried short articles about his
departure. Some anxiety was felt at first regarding the
safety of the party, owing to the f ct that their vessel
was thought to be in the path of the typhoon that ravaged
Osaka on the lst. However, the typhoon took another path,
and the vessel reached Moji without incident. Local cor-
respondents of Americ:n nevsp pers were responsible for the
scare stories appearing in the press in the United States,
although it would appear that they had no basis for their
alarm as one of them was told, on phoning the Embassy, that
there was definitely no cause for alarm and that the Embassy
had been assured that the vessel was not in the storm path.
(g) Japanese Interest in the Philippine Islands
Japan has been showing evidence of increasing interest
8118.01
1n
*See Embassy's Despatch No. 970 of September 14, 1934.

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
the Philippine Islands ever since their eventual inde-
pendence was guaranteed. The Japanese Consul General in
Manila is quoted as saying that trade with the United
States would naturally diminish and trade with Japan in-
crease as independence approaches, and implied that the
Islanders should cast their lot with Japan instead of
with the United States, which is so far away.* Business

RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES
(a) "Manchukuo"
1. Reform of Japanese Control in Manchuria
The military authorities are jubilant over their
vietory in the long struggle over the reorganization of
Japanese administration and representation in Manchuria. It
was a struggle between the military and civilian anti-mili-
tary elements, and although the plan finally adopted* made
a few concessions to the Foreign an Overseas Ministries,
the Army is considered by the NICHI NICHI and many other
papers as having won a signal victory, maintaining its domi-
nation of Japan's foreign policy. Considerable trouble was
experienced with the old triple system of control, but it
is difficult to try to replace civilian aministration
with military. That change is causing some difficulty now,
and the civilians in the Kwantung Government are objecting
strenuously. As the CHRONICLE of September 16 explains,
quoting the CHUGAI SHOGYO, it is patent that Japan shares
the responsibility for the de ence of the new country and
the maintenance of peace and order. While Japan desires
that "Manchukuo" enjoy all the rights of an independent
state, it is still too early. So long as present condi-
tions there obtain, says the CHRONICLE, the necessity of
investing the Ambassador to "Manchukuo" with extensive powers,
must be recognized.
In an editorial, the ADVERTISER of September 19 says
that the Army's intention from the outset was to exclude from
Manchuria the influence of the political parties, which
had spoiled and weakened the Japanese administration before
the
*See Embassy's despatch No. 1005 dated October 10, 1934.

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
the September l931 incident. Even the Overseas Ministry
itself, says the editorial, was a creation of the political
parsies, and its vitality will wax and wane with the for-
tumes of those parties.
The KOKUMIN says, in support of the new plan to put
the military in control in Manchuria, that there is almost
no Japanese police officer in Kwantung who does not make a
fortune of from twenty to thirty thousand yen during his
three years there, and further, that the Chinese are secretly
glad to see that the Japanese are susceptible to corruption.
(This statement resulted in strong Government censure, and
the KOKUmIN was forced to retract it. It has been rumored
that there may be some basis of f:ct in the charge, although
stoutly denied by all concerned.)
The change in administration an representation in
Manchuria is believed by the press to be one of the out-
standing problems confronting the present Cabinet. The
approved plan is being polished up to present to the emer-
gency session of the Diet, where another struggle is anti-
cipated. The Privy Council too, has to put its stamp of
approval on the plan. The TIMES of September 10 reported
some concern in the House of Peers over the reform plan,
and said that body agreed: (1) that it is impolitic for the
War Department to bear the brunt of direct administrative
responsibility from the standpoint of future industrial
and general development of Manchuria, as well as for its
own good, but (2) that as a practical question, however,
the position of the military authorities is strongest, and
the final plan to be adopted will be one that approaches
more nearly the militar position.

3. Provincial Reorganimation Plam
A RENGO despatch from Hsinking states that the "Manchukuo®
Government will put into effect on December 1 the following
new plan of provincial administration:
(1) The present Fengtien (Mukden) Province
will be divided into three new provinces: Fengtien --
the seat of provincial government; Chinchow and An-
tung. The present Kirin Province will be divided
into three new ones: Kirin, Sankiang (Chiamuszu), and
Chientao (Yenti). The present Heilungkiang Province
will be divided into two new ones: Keiho (Taheiho)
and Lungkiang (Chichihar). The Jehol Province remains
intact. A new province will be established in the
North Manchuria Special District, and will be named
Pingkiang Province with Harbin as the seat of govern-
ment.
(2) No change will be made in Hsingan Province.
The present four sub-provinces remain unchanged.
(3) The administrative office in charge of every
new province will be c: lled the Provincial Office,
and the head of the province the Governor.
(4) Each provincial office will consist of five
departments: general, civil, police, educational and
industrial affairs departments.
(5) All the sub-divisions (hsien) in the present
provinces will be abolished.
(6) As a general rule, a Manchou official will
be appointe Governor, while the chief of the general
affairs department will be a Japanese official of the
"Manchukuo" central government.

marked impression upon the many spectators that witnessed
the maneuvers.

(b) U.S.S.R.
Sale of the Chinese Eastern Railway
Newspaper extras issued late on September 23 proclaimed
the final agreement over the long discussed sale of the
Chinese Eastern Railway to "Manchukuo". Negotiations be-
tween "Manchukuo" and Soviet Russia had been virtually
deadlocked since Mr. Ohashi, "Manchukuo" Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs, returned sud enly to Manc uria after con-
ferring th Ambassador Youreneff on August 13. It began
to appear that sale negotiations would break down entirely,
and thus place a further strain on the already delicate
Soviet-Japanese relations. However, a compromise was sud-
denly agreed upon an the sales price of the railway fixed
at ¥ 170,000,000 -- "Manchukuo" having increased 1ts bid by
¥ 20,000,000 over the price suggsted by Mr. Hirota, and
the Russians having r uced their demand by a similar amount.*
The price agreed upon includes ¥ 30,000,000 for retire-
ment allowances to the Soviet officials of the railway. Of
the remainder about one-third is to be paid in cash and the
balance in merchandise over a three year period. All details

4. Soviet-"Manchukuo" Border Troubles
In addition t the troublesome bandit situation in Man-
1.93 Mmambmia
churia, the Japanese have been greatly irritate by the fre-
quent invasions of "Manchukuo" territory by Soviet troops,
and the Soviet Government has protested repeatedly against
"Manchukuo" and Japanese unauthorized intrusions into Soviet
territory. The border question was not so important until
"Manchukuo" became a nation, declared the ASAHI, whereupon
the Soviet fortifications were strengthened and more troops
were stationed near the line. Ninety-four Soviet citizens
have been arrested by "Manc ukuo" authorities since last year,
of which thirty-one have been deperted. The arreste were
made because of te rorist plots, sabotage activities along the
Chinese Eastern Railway, isturbing the peace and other sub-
versive activities.
The Foreign Office has denied the reports appearing in
the European press, according to the CHRONICLE of the 23rd,
to the effect that Japan had proposed to Soviet Russia the
mutual abolition of defenses along the Soviet-"Manchukuo"
border. It was mentioned in 1930, but no action was taken;
in 1931 when the Japanese Government rejected the Soviet
proposal to enter into a non-aggression pact because the Kel-
logg Pact was considered sufficient, it did state its ap-
proval of establishing a joint committee between Japan, "Man-
chukuo" and Soviet Russia for eliminating the causes of
border

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.98)
-25-
order trouble. But no proposal of border disarmament
has emanated from Japan.
Mr. Kawakami is again quoted in the JAPAN TIMES of the
18th, as stating that demilitarization of the Soviet-"Man-
chukuo" border and the conclusion of a Soviet-Japanese
non-aggression pact are essential to the preservation of
peace in the Far East. "History repeats itself", he said
"in that Japan has again to decice whether to ally itself
with Russia or with Britain, just as it did thirty-five
years ago."
Although there have been, anc doubtless will be many
causes for irritation between Japan and the USSR, they seem
somehow to be always possible of solution without resorting
to conflict. "Will Japan fight the USSR?" is the question
propounded by a TIMES editorial which reviews the situation.
It concludes that excluding the possibility of a break-up
of the entire Soviet regime, there is no probability of a war
either this year, next year or in the near future because
there are not sufficient reasons for action that drastic.

(d) Netherlands Indies
It seems that as soon as one bone of contention between
the Japanese and Dutch delegates to the Batavia Trade Parley
1s cast aside, another one appears on the scene. In July
the Netherlands Indies Government promulgated an ordinance
restricting imports of earthenware and in retaliation against
this measure the Japan Federation of Ceramic Export Associations,
th
* See Embassy's despatch No. 975 of September 18 and Monthly
Report for July.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)

th the support of Japanese shipowners, on August 3 sus-

Indies. On August 30 the Netherlands Indies Government
withdrew its restrictive measure against imports of earthen-
ware and in return the Java Pottery Importers' Association,
a Japanese organization objectionable to the Netherlands
Indies, dissolved itself. However, another snag was struck
when the Dutch delegates to the Parley reiterated their
demand that there be included in the regular agenda of the
Parley the question of concluding a Japanese-Dutch agreement
on shipping in the Pacific. The Japanese Foreign Office
took the attitude that it ha no means of prev iling upon

the inclusion of shipping in the agenda of the ParIey. The

latter. This proposal proviue that a shipping agreement
should be negotiated inform lly between the two Governments,
these negotiations to be conductei concurrently with the Batavia
Trae Parley but not as a part of the agonda. As a result of
this compromise, the tra: e conversations were resumed by the
Japanese and Dutch elegations. At the end of the month no
agreement had been reached, as the tvo delegations maintained
their widely divergent views with regard to the apportionment
of import tios between the Dutch an Japanese merchants
in the Netherlands Indies.*

1. Chinese Reactions to Chinese Eastern Railway Sale
A R NGO report states that the Chinese Government has
been greatly shocked by th news that an agreement has been
reached between the USSR and "Manchukuo" for the sale and
transfer of the Chinese Eastern Railway, which the Nanking
Government still considers belongs partly to the territorial
rights of China. Another RENGo despatch to the ADVERTISER
says that a protest is to be expected from the Nanking
Government on the grounds that the transfer is illegal.
The report quotes the opinion of the Chinese Foreign Office,
which is that the Russo-Chinese agreement binds the USSR to
obtain China's consent to any transfer of Soviet interests
1n

=== Page 157 === (confidence 1.00)
-32-
Chinese Eastern Railway. The Foreign Office spokesman,
reports, indicated that the illegality of the deal would
called to the attention of the countries concerned, and
that the matter might be submitted to the League of Nations.
Chinese envoys in Moscow and Tokyo have been instructed to
enquire into the matter.
The NICHI NICHI of the 27th says that the Nanking Govern-
ment is greatly worried over the railway sale because it may
presage Soviet recognition of "Manchukuo". The article indi-
cates that China will attempt to disrupt friendly relations
between the USSR, Japan and "Manchukuo" in an effort to hinder
the railway transfer. China has heretofore observed a fairly
friendly attitude toward Soviet Russia, making every effort
to avoid a clash, even when the USSR encroached upon Hsinking
Province. It pursued that policy based on the conviction
that a Soviet-Japanese war was unavoidable, and had more to
gain by allying itself with the USSR. The possibility of Soviet
recognition of "Manchukuo", however, the NICHI NICHI thinks,
may cause China to stiffen its attitude toward the Soviet
Union. The article observes that China's traditional policy
of checkmating Japan with Soviet influence and friendship has
completely failed, an two years' diplomacy has only served
further to isolate China internationally. China's policy
toward Japan, it says, may now be expected to become more moderat
with the sale of the Chinese East rn Railway, and because of
Japan's strong position on the continent China may abandon
its hostile attitude and cooperate, even though unwillingly,
with Japan as its only recourse. Japan on its part, RENGO
states, may be expected to redouble its efforts to settle dis-
agreements with Nanking in an endeavor to keep peace between
China and "Manchukuo". In that manner the two nations can co-
operate

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-33-
6perate in preventing Chinese chauvinistic elements in
Manchuria from causing trouble.

2.
. Chinese Scare over Anglo-Japanese Alliance Rumor
Unwarranted.
The fright which the rumors of a revival of the old
Anglo-Japanese alliance gave the Chinese toward the close
of August proved to be without foundation as denials of
the truth of those rumors were made on all sides during
September.* Chinese papers pointed out that of course
Britain, wishing t strengthen its position and protect
its interests in the Far East, would see: an alliance with
Japan, and China's great fears were regarding possible
further partition of its wide domain by those two powers.

2.
Collection by the Japanese Authorities of Taxes
from Tenants of Perpetual Lease Property and from
Perpetual Lease Holders.
The Japanese authorities became more active in Septem-
ber in the collection of taxes not only from tenants of per-
petual lease property but also from the leaseholders them-
selves. New tax demand notices were sent to foreign holders
of perpetual leases and to their tenants, calling for the
payment of the disputed municipal and prefectural taxes.
In ad ition, the Japanese municipalities have decreed that
such perpetual leaseholders an tenants must pay retroactive
taxes for : period of five years commencing with the year
1929. As a result of non-payment of these taxes, certain
American and British firms and individuals have either had
their property cistrained or been served with notices that
their property woul e  istrained if tax payment was not
made by a given date.

(n)
Japanese Developments Prior to Naval Conversations
In the Embassy's despatch No. 963 of September 6, 1934,
500A15a
the basic plan finally evolved by the Japanese Government
for the 1935 Naval Conference, was discussed together with
certain observations regarding the outlook for the conference.
Especial reference was made to the preliminary conversations
to be held in London, to which Rear A miral Isoroku Yamamoto
was to be sent as technical adviser. Since that time the
Japanese press has been replete with discussion of both the
preliminary conversations an the next year's conference.
The entire history of Japanese naval relations with other
countries, the weighing i arguments for and against abroga-
tion of the Washington Treaty, the fears of naval construc-
tion races and the attitudes of the United States and Great
Britain were thoroughly rehashed. A great eal of propaganda
was issued, and apparently Japan believes it has succeeded
in preparing the United States and Great Britain for its
determined proposal to abrogate the Washington Treaty. Much
of the propaganda too, was for the purpose of gaining the so-
lid support of the people behind the naval plan, and in that
also some success was achieved.
Admiral Yamamoto and his aides sailed for England on
the S.S. HIYE MARU on September 20, carrying with him the
outline

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
-41-
line of Japan's basic naval policy.* In his farewell
speeches he said his mission would be to try to make the
powers understand Japan's proposals and to let them know
that the entire Japanese nation has faith in its naval
program. He was reported by the ADVERTISER as saying that
the ratio system has been the root of all evil and highly
derogatory to Japan's national prestige and honor. He
scouted the idea of a naval building race following abroga-
tion.
According to press despatches published locally, the
reaction tn the Unite States to Japan's announced naval
program and abrogation policy was one of indifference. of
course the American Naval Authorities were reported to be
somewhat concerned over the possibilities of a naval con-
struction race, but the public remained apathetic, being
much more engrossed with domestic economic problems.
The ASAHI of September 1 quotes Mr. Swanson, Secre-
tary of the Navy,as follows:
" If Japan abrogates the Washington and London
Naval Treaties I can well imagine that the
American Navy will vigorously demand an ex-
pansion of it air and submarine bases and
of its fleet in the Pacific".
The same paper on the 26th r porte that President
Roosevelt and Mr. Hull had come to an agreement regaring
the United States' attitude towar Japan. They have de-
cided, the report st ted, that Japan's demand for equal
armament be definitely refused; and that if the report
that "Manchukuo" intends to build gunboats, and the subject
is brought up at the preliminary talks, it be also refused.
However, the ASAHI of the 29th said, the United States dele-
gates will wait until the Japanese announce their position
and
*See Embassy's telegram No. 203 of September 17, 1934.

=== Page 167 === (confidence 1.00)
-42-
proposals before setting forth the American attitude.
Then the American counter-proposals, calculated to cope
with Japanese demands, will be presented. The report
concluded with the information that the American Govern-
ment considers that in case the preliminary conversations
become deadlocked in London, American public opinion will
be soriously aroused.
Admiral Notumasa Suetsugu, Commander-in-Chief of the
Combined Fleet, on his recent visit to Manchuria, spoke
of the coming naval confercnce and disarmament, and charac-
terized them as being intimately and inseparably related
to the Manchurian problems, according to the JIJI SHIMPO
of the 19th. He is understooc to have said that unless
security for Japan's national defense is obtained there
will be no fundamental solution of the Manchurian problem,
which is the basis of Far Eastern peace. After discussing
the ratios an construction race possibilities, he concluded
with the observ tion that "Manchukuo" certainly needed a
river fleet at once, and that as soon as the new Government
has founded its finances it should have an ocean fleet.
In preparing for the preliminary conversations in
London, the Japanese Foreign Minister has held separate
talks with the British, French and Anerican Ambassadors for
the purpose of clearly outlining to them Japan's aims and
policy. Because of the absence from their posts of the
Japanese Ambassadors, the Foreign Minister wanted to take
no chance that the powers concerned misunderstand Japan's
naval program. The present preparations for the Naval
Conference itself appear to consist merely of seeing to it
that

present Minister of the Navy is spoken of as the most like-

INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS
(a) Cabinet
It does not appear to have been definitely decided
whether the Department of Overseas Affairs shall be
abolished or a new Overseas Minister shall be appointed
in place of Premier Okada, who has filled that portfolio
since his appointment as Premier. Now that this Depart-
ment has been virtually deprived of any direct control
in the administration of Manchuria, the newspapers believe
that Premier Okada will soon come to a definite decision
with regard to the future of the Department. THE OSAKA
MAINICHI of September 21 reported that the Premier had
made up his mind to appoint an Overseas Minister before
the end of the year and that the selection would probably
be mace from the House of Peers.
According to the ASAHI SHI BUN of September 27, the
Minister for Foreign Affairs has decided to improve the
mechanism of the Japanese Foreign Service and with this
end in view will appoint from among the personnel of the
Foreign Office a "Foreign Office Efficiency Committee"
which will revise the system of examination for entry
into the diplomatic service, adopt an age limit for diplo-
matic officials, and establish a closer contact between
the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and diplomatic and consular
offices abroad. This newspaper states that the Chairman
of the proposed Committee will be Mr. Mamoru Shigemitsu,
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, and the Assistant
Chairman, Mr. Shigeru Kuriyama, Chief of the Treaty Bureau.
(b) The Army
The KOKUMIN SHIMBUN had the temporary temerity to
publish an editorial on September 26 entitied "Future
Manchurian

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-45-
ministration," in which it was stated that reports had
been received to the effect that there was no Japanese
police official in the Kwantung Leased Territory who did
not corruptly amass a fortune of Y 20,000 during a three
years' term of office and that the Chinese were laughing
in elation at this state of affairs. Since most of the
police officials in the Kwantung Leased Territory are ex-
Army men, Army circles in Japan protested vigorously
against this editorial. The day following the publica-
tion of the editorial, the KOKUMIN SHIMBUN printed a
notice with the caption, "For the Sake of the Honor of
the Kwantung Police," announcing that it "cancelled" that
portion of the editorial which referred to the amassing
of fortunes by Kwantung police officials and adding that
such a statement was without foundation.
It is reported that on September 28 Prince Kanin,
Chief of the General Staff, was received in audience by the
Emperor, who sanctioned the proposed appointment of Generals
Sadao Araki and Nobuyuki Abe as the commanders of the
two opposing armies in the grand military maneuvers which
are to be held on November 11, 12, and 13.
(c) The Nayy
The newspapers state that Admiral Nobumasa Suetsugu,
Commander-in-Chief of the Combined Fleet, will be shifted
from his present post in December to become a member of the
Supreme Military Council, and that his successor as Com-
mander-in-Chief will probably be Admiral Osami Nagano, who
is now Commandant of the Yokosuka Naval Station.
Reports were published in the Japanese press that in
preparation

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)

and munitions factories in and around Tokyo had been start-

summoned for the purpose of submitting detailed reports
on the capacity of their plants, present equipment, plans
for expansion, the number of workmen employed, et cetera.
(d) The Political Parties
The Seiyukai an the Minseito have been unable to
agree on the question of rice control. The former party
is in favor of a State rice monopoly, with State control
of production and marketing, while the latter advocates
adjustment of rice supply and demand through voluntary
control of production by the farmers under State super-
vision.
(e)
Civil Aviation, Its Development as a Reservist
Air Force in the Event of War.
The Ministry of Communications, with the support of
the War and Navy Ministries, is reported to have instituted
a program for the expansion of civil aviation as a second
line of air defense that could be mobilized for military
purposes in an emergency. THE OSAKA MAINICHI, in advocat-
ing the strengthening of the army and navy air forces,
observes that since an extensive development of these air
forces will not only entail an enormous outlay of money,
but also "will incite other countries," the expansion of
military aviation must be stopped at a given limit, whereas
civil'aviation can be develope on a larger scale. According
to

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
-47-
cording th THE JAPAN CHRONICLE of September 4, Mr. Toko-
nami, the Minister for Communications, is demanding an
additional sum of Y 1,500,000 in the appropriations for
1935-36 for the extension and inauguration of air routes,
the improvement of aerodromes, an  the encouragement of
the aviation business. This newspaper states that the
Communications Minister contends that the development
of civil aviation as a reservist air force calls for
urgent attention; that large passenger planes could be
converted into heavy bombers and mail planes into scouting
planes; and that the expansion and improvement of this
branch of aviation will serve to train reservist aviators
who would be of use in th event of war.
(f) The Diet
Various political elements have for some time been
demanding the convocation of a special session of the
894.032
Diet before the holding of the regular session in December,
notably the Seiyukai, the majority political party.* Rea-
sons advanced for the extraordinary session have centered
around the necessity for the enactment of legislation to
relieve the rice and silk cocoon farmers, though the Sei-
yukai, in addition, has harbored the idea of injecting
political discussions into the Diet session, if called,
for this party is still at od s with the Cabinet. The
Government, which hac hitherto turned a deaf ear to such
demands, was placed in a position where it could no
longer refuse to call a special session when the results
of the devastation wrought by the Osaka typhoon and tidal
waye
*See previous Monthly Reports.

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
-48-
of September 21 became known. The press reported
that on the recommendation of Home Minister Goto, who had
made a tour of inspection in and around Osaka, the Cabinet
had on September 28 resolved to call a special session of
the Diet in October or November. The concensus of news-
paper opinion was to the effect that the Government had
decided upon this step solely because of the pressing need
for finaneial relief in the districts ravaged by the typhoon
and tidal wave, and not in response to the entreaties of the

the extraordinary Diet session to relief measures and pre-
vent discussion of such political questions as naval dis-
armament, the reorganization of the administrative system
in Manchuria, an dissolution of the Diet with consequent
general elections. Most of the newspapers are of the opinion,
however, that in any case the political parties will not use
the special session to criticize or attack the Government,
and that even if the disarmament question is brought to
the fore, the purpose of this move will be only to strengthen
the Government's han and unify public opinion on this issue.
(g) The Press
There have been indications that the Japanese Govern-
894.91
ment is showing an increasing tendency toward interfering
with the press, whether for the purpose of suppressing
opinions and news that are inimical to Japan's political
schemes or of curbing sensational statements that might
embarrass Japan in its foreign relations.* In September
negotiations
* See Embassy's despatch No. 955 of September 6, 1934.

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
-49
ogotiations were carried on for the consolidation of the
two important Japanese news agencies, RENGO and NIPPON
DMPO. The Government has urged such an amalgamation os-
tensibly for reasons of economy and for the elimination
of unnecessary competition, but it would appear probable
that the real motive behind the Government's exhortations
is its desire to establish stricter control over news
despatches, both incoming and outgoing. At the present
time the two news agencies are in competition and there-
fore sensational, and even alarming, reports are published
by the agency which is fortunate enough to secure such re-
ports first. The idea has been advanced that through the
consolidation of RENGO and NIPPON DE PO the Government
is aiming at eventual official control of news services
in order that during the so-called crisis of 1935-36
only the approved Japanese point of view may be presented
in Japan anć abroad.*
GDA.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION:
Nothing to report.

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
-50-
GOVERNMENT FINANCE
No definite policy has been announced by the Ministry
of Finance for coping with the difficult problems with
which that Department has to deal. The principal diffi-
culties to be solved are the following: the necessity
of curtailing the l934-35 budget demands of the various
Ministries in the face of the War and Navy Ministries'
firm insistence upon greatly increased military and
naval appropriations, which together constitute a pre-
ponderant allotment in every Japanese budget; the apparent
need, in the absence of : substantial increase in taxes,
of issuing a ditional red ink bonds, with the danger that
the saturation point may be reached in the issuance of
such bonds; and the insistent public demand for financial
relief of the impoverished farmers. The Finance Minister's
troubles have been considerably aggravated by the typhoon
and tidal wave which struck Osaka and its environs on
September 2l and caused damages estimated at slightly
over ¥5,900,000,000 (approximetely $150,000,000). Th1s
catastrophe will necessitate the expanding of the budget
for 1935-36 on account of the grants in aid that will be
given by the Government for the devastated districts and
also because of the loss in taxes from this section of the
country. Before the disaster the Ministry of Finance
had been counting on a natural increase in revenue of at least
¥ 100,000,000 during the fiscal year 1934-35, but since
Osaka is the industrial center of Japan and pays a large
portion of the nation's taxes, this amount of natural in-
crease in revenue will be substantially reduced. The
Government

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
-51-
vernment has decided to grant monetary assistance to
the afflicted districts and this will mean an added set-
back to Government finances. For this purpose, a special
session of the Diet will be called. Since there is an
obvious need for the passage of measures to assist the
distressed farming population and as there has been
for some time a clamor on all sides for such relief, this
question may possibly come up for discussion in the Diet's
special session, although the Government is opposed to
consideration of farm relief during the special session.
With regard to the budget for 1935-36, Mr. Fujii,
the Minister for Finance, has stated several times that
he is determined to restrict the total budget to about
¥ 2,000,000,000, which would represent a decrease of over
¥ 140,000,000 as compared with the 1934-35 budgetary ex-
penditures and would mean a shearing of ¥ 800,000,000 from
the estimated demands of the Ministries for 1935-36. The
conflict over appropriations in the next budget between
the Ministers of Navy and War on the one hand and the Minister
for Finance on the other, whic was discussed in the Monthly
Report for August, grew more intense during September, and
it still remains extremely doubtful that the Finance Minis-
ter will be able to effect any material curtailment in the
estimates of those Ministries. According to the Japanese
press, the Foreign Office has demanded an appropriation
of ¥9,450,000 in the 1935-36 budget, an increase of
¥21,600,000 over the current appropriation. The newspapers
state that this demand is based on the necessity for funds
to meet the so-called crisis of 1935-36 successfully and
peacefully

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
-52-
peacefully by means of diplomacy.
Government authorities have asserted in the past month
that the banks in Japan, which have large surpluses for in-
vestment, will be able to absorb large additional amounts
of deficit-covering Government bonds. However, a number
of financial pbservers and a part of the press do not
agree with the Government's argument. In this connection,
Mr. Kashiwagi, Manager of the Tokyo Branch of the Yokohama
Specie Bank, stated in a conversation with the Commercial
Attaché on September 14 that he strongly supported the
Bank of Japan's open market bond operations, and he ex-
pressed the opinion that the banks would have sufficient
available funds for some time to absorb all Government
budget deficit bond offerings. On the other hand, accord-
ing to THE JAPAN TIMES of Sept ember 27, Baron Goh, Pres1-
dent of the Japan Chamber of Commerce and Industry, stated
at a dinner given for members of the Okada Cabinet on
September 26 that in his opinion the issue of red ink bonds
"has almost reached its breaking point." At any rate, pre-
sent indications are that, unless an increase in taxes is
decided upon, the Government will be compelled to issue
additional bonds for the purpose of balancing the budget.
Mr. Tsushima, Vice Minister of Fin nce, stated to the
Commercial Attaché on September 18 that the Government was
seriously considering the cuestion of increasing taxes but
that care must be taken not to make any radical or sudden
changes in financial policy.
In September the Ministry of Finance announced statistics
covering the final returns on the l933-34 budget accounts,
closed on July 31, 1934. According to these statistics, the
total revenue received during the year amounted to
2,331,759,000 (about $699,527,700), wh12a expenditures

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.97)
-53-
amounted to ¥ 2,254,662,000. Thus, there was an excess of
revenue over expenditures of ¥ 77,097,000.
GDA.

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
-54
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS
(a) Foreign Trade
According to the preliminary figures, Japan's ex-
ports for the month of September were ¥ 171,463,000 (ap-
proximately $ 51,438,900) and imports were ¥ 155,902,000,
showing a favorable balance of  15,561,000. As compared
with August, the above statistics represent a decrease in
exports of ¥ 38,266,000 an a decrease in imports of
¥ 26,844,000. While there was an unfavorable balance of
trade in May ana June, there were favorable balances in
July, August, and September of ¥ 9,876,000, ¥ 26,983,000
and ¥ 15,561,000, respectively.
The decline in exports for the past few months has been
due partly to trade restriction mesunes adopted against
Japanese imports not only in old markets but also in newly
established markets in Africa and Latin America. According
to press reports, during the month of September El Salvador
anc Norway adopted restrictive measures against Japanese
goods, an Argentina, the Philippines, and the French colonies
and dependencies were considering the establishment of tariff
barriers and import quotas with regard to Japanese products.
The newspapers report that the Japanese Foreign Office
has completed a draft of a commercial treaty between Japan
and Australia for submission t the Australian Government
and that the treaty will be based on the principle that because
Japan buys more from Australia than it sells to that Dominion,
Japan should be given preference over third parties in the
matter of imports into Australia. It is also reported that
the Minister for Foreign Affairs has decided to send a trade
delegation to Australia to return the recent visit of the
former

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
-55-
mer Australian Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr.
Latham. Another agreement that is under consideration
is a proposal submitted by the Swedish Match Trust to the
Japanese Ministry of Commerce and Industry for an accord
on the question of sales prices of matches in overseas
markets.
An editorial appearing in THE JAPAN TIMES of September
1, states that certain trade circles are complaining of the
wowing adverse trade balance with respect to the United
States, as evidenced by the fact that this adverse balance
for the first seven months of the present calendar year
was ¥ 221,357,000, while the unfavorable balance for the
first seven months of 1933 was only ¥108,756,000. This
editorial points out, however, that the heavy imports from
the United States for the first seven months of 1934 are
explained chiefly by large purchases of raw materials,
particularly raw cotton, for manufacturing purposes in
Japan and that the main items of export to the United States
show gains over 1935, with  few exceptions, notably raw
silk, the value of exports of this comnodity declining sharp-
ly, principally because of the slump in prices.
A New York despatch dated September 8 from the Staff
correspondent of TH OSAKA MAINICHI states that keen compe-
tition is expected between Japanese and American merchants
in Central America if commercial agreements are concluded
between the United States an the various countries in that
region, and that the Commercial Attaché of the Japanese
Embassy in Washington, Mr. Yasundo Budo, will leave shortly
for Central America for the purpose of concluding commercial
treaties with the Central American states.
(b) Financial Trends
The financial situation was distinctly unsettled due
to

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.99)
-56-
the uncertainty of the Government's bond and tax
policy; the yen declined in value with relation to the
United States dollar; bank deposits increased to a new
high record; loans decreased to the lowest level reached
since November, 1929; the stock market experienced the
worst slump at the mid le of the nonth that has occurred
since l932, but stocks began to improve toward the end
of the month.
(c) Farming Problems
The agricultural situation showed no improvement
during September. The sharp decline in the price of
pring cocoons caused a shrinkage of ¥ 180,000,000 (about
$ 54,000,000) in the total income of silk cocoon farmers
and the plight of these farmers has become even more serious
because of a considerable slump in summer cocoon prices.
The price of rice continued to a vance, thus adding to the
burdens of some of the farmers through the increased cost
of subsistence.* The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
announced during the month of September that the damage
to the rice crop from droughts in 1934 had been estimated
at ¥ 100,000,000 (approximately * 30,000,000) and that
rice production for 1934 would be about 20% below the
yearly average for the past five years. According to THE
ASAHI of September 29, the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry has rawn up plans for the relief of rice and
silk cocoon farmers which will be submitted to the Diet
at its next regular session. This newspaper says that
the principal points of these plans are industrialization
of
* See Monthly Report for August, 1934.

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.99)
-57-
gricultural villages, more intensive control of the
mportation of rice from the colonies, joint storage of
hulled rice, and diversification of the enterprises of
sericulturists.
(d) Industties
Industrial establishments in and around Osaka suffered
a severe blow from the tidal wave and typhoon of September
21. The Department of Home Affairs announced that official
investigations up to September 26 had revealed that 12,917
factories in this district had sustained a total damage
estimated at ¥ 140,000,000 (about $ 42,000,000). The
machinery industry suffered the greatest loss, followed
by the textile industry.
GDA.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
-58-
LABOR AND EMIGRATION
(a) Labor
One of the largest labor disputes in Japan's history
occurred on September 5 when some 10,200 Tokyo tramway
.5d45
and bus employees went on strike in protest against
the decision of the Tokyo City Electric Bureau to dis-
miss permanently 380 employees, and to dismiss the re-
maining 9,820 employees and re-employ them the next day
with an average pay-cut of 40%. The Tokyo City Electric
Bureau ha hoped to save ¥ 5,500,000 annually in running
expenses by means of this curtailment of wages. Although
the strike lasted 12 days, it was on the whole extremely
orderly. Following the decision of the Metropolitan
Police Board to order compulsory arbitration of the con-
troversy under authority of the Labor Dispute Arbitration
Law, the employees announced that the strike would be sus-
pended on September 17 because of the order of compulsory
arbitration an because the Tokyo City Electric Bureau
had resolved to reserve enforcement of its wage-cut plan.
A large part of the strikers returned to work, and on
September 2 the arbitration proceedings were begun. The
arbitration committee met with a firm stand on the part of
the strikers' representatives, who were reported by the
press to be determined in their demand for a substantial
reduction in the announce 40% wage cut, and at the end
of the month it appeared likely that another strike would
be called unless the employees' demands were complied with.
(b) Emigration
Nothing to report.
GDA.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
-59-
MISCELLANEOUS
(a) Osaka Typhoon and Tidal Wave of September 21.
On the morning of September 21 a heavy typhoon
etruck Osaka and surrounding districts, followed by a
tidal wave that broke over the waterfornt and inundated
the principal industrial section to a depth of from six
to eight feet.* Official reports issued by the Home Of-
fice gave the number of persons killed as 1,66l, the
largest percentage of whom were school children; the
number of persons injured as 5,4l4; and the number of per-
sons missing as 589. The Home Office also reported that
211,163 houses and buildings had, according to estimates,
been totally or partially damaged, 18,39l having been
totally damaged, 22,037 partially damaged, 862 ashed
away, and 169,872 partially submerged. As stated in
Section V of this report, the total estimsted losses
amounted to just over half a billion yen. This figure,
however, does not include indirect losses which will ensue
from enforced idleness of factories.
In view of the serious nature of this catastrophe, the
Chargé a'Affaires, ad interim, on behalf of the American
Government, conveyed orally to the Japanese Government,
through the Foreign Office, the deep sympathy of the
American nation.** On September 2: the President of the
United States sent a message of sympathy directly to the
Emperor of Japan.***
GDA.

Prominent American in Kobe Lost in Typhoon
Mr. E.B. Terry, prominent American business man of
Kobe, set out in his sailing yacht for Imaharu on September

Tokyo's political report for the month
of October (No. 1050, November 19, 1934).
Relations with the United States:
The departure of Ambassador Saito for
the United States, and the tenor of speeches
made incidental to departure, is reported.
The speeches reported have mostly been
received by the Department. The Embassy
reports that "the general tenor of his
(the Ambassador's) speeches was that Japan
was progressing favorably in its diplomatic
relations with the United States and that
there was nothing to fear in the future
relations between the two countries."
The Japanese press reports the
Ambassador as stating that tre Japanese
were determined to eliminate the disgrace
and dishonor resulting from the Washington
and London naval treaties; his opinion that
Japan would have more difficulty with
Great Britain than with the United States at
the 1935 naval conference; his belief that
the powers would not find reason to oppose
Japan's naval claims if its intentions
rogarding peace were fully appreciated;
that he would do all possible to remove
American misgivings on this score. The
Ambassador

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
The Ambassador also saw indications toward
the adoption of the quota system for Japa-
nese immigrants; that in newly acquired
markets in Latin America, Japan was not in
competition with the United States, as the
latter sells machinery and such products
to these markets while Japan exports
cotton textiles and "general products".
A leading Japanese newspaper (NICHI
NICHI SHIMBUN) reporta that the following
autline of polioy grew out of conferences
between the Ambassador and high Government
officials:
1) Affirmation of the friendly
agreement expressed in the recent Hull-
Hirota good-will notes;
2) Thoroughly to acquaint the
United States with Japan's naval formula,
but in case of failure of the disarmament
conforence to negotiate an agreement which
would pledge the two countries to abstain
from a naval race;
3) To follow an independent
diplomatic policy with ospect to "Manchukuo"
and China, at the same time respecting the
American demand for the open door and equal
opportunity;
4) The

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
4) The Ambassador fully to inform
the United States in regard to "real condi-
tions in 'Manchukuo', and to prevent inter-
national legalization of the Stimson Doctrine;
5) To minimize the reported
seriousness of Soviet-Japanese relations
and to advise of their improvement as result
of the Chinese Eastern Railway project;
6) Japan to rely on America's
"justice and faith" in regard to the
immigration issue, and not of its own accord
bring up the question;
7) Japan to persist in securing
markets in South America as the only remain-
ing ones; and
8) Japan to rely on the appropriate
authorities, in regard to the anti-Japanese
movement in Arizona, to solve the problem
quickly and fairly.
Visit of Ambassador Bullitt en route to the
United States.
The Ambassador's visit was made without
notable incident except for the publication
by two newspapers of fabricated interviews.
These were harmless, however, and Ambassador
Grew has already discounted them.

Speeches by Dr. Garfield, former president of
Williame Collego.
Dr. Garfield spoke before the America-
Japan Society, and made numerous indiscreet
references to various difficulties affecting
American-Japanese relations. This incident
was previously reported and apparently had no
special repercussions.
Several matters of which the Department
has already been informed are reported. These
include principally the alleged violation of
Philippine laws by the Japanese vessel KAIUN
MARU, and the reported assault upon members
of the Philippine patrol; press comment on
the anti-alien movement in the Salt River
Valley, Arizona; and Japanese reaction to the
American angle of the preliminary naval con-
versations in London. The Japanese press and
reportedly Japanese naval authorities vigorously
attacked the stand of the American delegation
at London. At the beginning of the conversa-
tions Japanese news despatches from London
and editorial comment in Tokyo stressed the
alleged amenability of Great Britain in con-
trast to the obduracy of the United States.
The impression was created that a new pro-
Japanese policy was formulating in Great
Britain, but Japanese ardor over this was
somewhat checked by later reports from London
thich reflected closer British-American
cooperation. On the whole, however, America's
attitude

- 6 -
Lord Barnby, head of the mission. The
mission was feted a great deal, and
apparently secured orders for steel and rail
equipment estimated at $40,000,000. (4ater
Representations by the British Ambassador
to the Japanese Government in regard to the
petroleum law are reported, the substance of
which the Department has already received
from the Embassy.
The Japanese press apparently has
widely conjectured in regard to the possible
conclusion of a non-aggression pact between
Japan and Great Britain. The traditional
friendship between Japan and Great Britain,
as two island empires in similar position,
and upon which the Anglo-Japanese alliance
was concluded in l902, is chiefly stressed.
The specific intentions, and friendship of
the two countries for each other, it is
contended might well find expression in a
non-aggression pact.
(b) Netherland India.
In regard to the conference at
Batavia, "the press states that the Japanese
have insisted and still insist on three
points, namely, that import quotas be based
on the volume of trade in 1933, that equal
ratios be established for Dutch and Japanese
imports

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-7-
imports in Netherland India, and that the
application of quotas be confined to
approximately ten articles;while the
attitude of the Netherland delegation is
that quotas must be fixed separately and
should not be based on the year 1933, that
Dutch traders in Netherland India should be
given a higher ratio of imports than the
Japanese traders and that quotas must be
fixed for approximately sixty artioles."
Numerous other disagreements are reported,
most of which have been separately covered
in despatches from Batavia.
(c) China.
The disinclination of the Japanese
Government to raise the Legation in China to
the rank of embassy, as indicated by the
reply of the Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs to a British inquiry, is reported.
However, the Foreign Minister is reported to
have toid the Chinese Minister "that the
elevation of the Japanese Legation at
Peiping to the status of an embassy might
be realized at any time depending on the
Chinese attitude toward Japan."
(d) International.
The Fifteenth International Red
Cross Conference was held in Tokyo from
October 16 to October 29. Among resolutions
passed was one to invite Soviet Russia to
representation

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 8 -
representation in the League of Red Cross
Societies, which invitation was officially
accepted; a resolution by the Soviet dele-
gation urging the Red Cross to seek methods
for preventing war and guaranteeing peace
among nations was approved; a resolution by
Colombia and Peru urged other nations to
use only such amicable means to settle their
differences as were employed by them in the
recent settlement of the Letica boundary
dispute. The next meeting of the conference
is to be held in Madrid in 1938.
(e) Relations with Soviet Russia.
Considerable disagreement exists
between Japan and Soyiet Russia over the
details of the tranger of the Chinese
Eastern Railway to "Manchukuo" under terms
of its recent sale. Points in dispute are
numerous, consisting of methods of payment
and dismissal compensation for Soviet
employees, and Soviet demands concerning
the exemption of building and non-railway
property. Nothing additional to despatches
previously reported.
Discussion has taken place in Tokyo
regarding the possibility of Soviet re-
cognition of "Manchukuo" in connection
with the sale of the railway. Foreign
observers

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 9 -
observers agree that this is unlikely, as
the Soviets have previously indicated their
belier that "Manchukuo" is a puppet of
Japan; also the Soviets are not likely to
disregard the wishes of the League of
Nations in the matter.
Soviet-Japanese hostilities appear to
be less or a probability than heretofore.
Poreign Minister Hirota adheres to a policy
of expedieney, which is endorsed by the
business world, and tacitly agreed to by
military leaders.
In an effort to reduce friction between
"Manchukuo" and Soviet Russia, plans have
been discussed for a permanent border
commission, and a demilitarized zone.
Japanese concern in the matter arises from
the appearance of larger Soviet military
forces in the region.
In Inner Nongolia, Chinese influence
appears to be waning, attributed in part to
non-payment by the Nanking Government of
promised subsidies to the Government of
Inner Mongolia, and the activity of Japanese
agents in that region. The Mongol population
1s reported to be on the point of revolt and
the Japanese military to be making prepara-
tions for possible operations in Inner
Mongolia.

(f) "Manchukuo".
This section deals chiefly with a
redistribution of Japanese administrative
authority in Manchuria including the Kwan-
tung Leased Territory. The developments
recounted relate largely to a centralization
of Japanese administration under the Japanese
Ambassador at Hsinking, the details of which
have been reported in separate despatches
from the Embassy.
(g) Naval Conference.
Reviews developments at London.
Japanese newspapers report that at the
close of the month the American-Japanese
delegates had been unable to agree on any
point, while the British and Japanese
delegations had apparently agreed on a
mumber of points. Both Japanese press
reports and editorial opinion give the
impression of a lack of harmony in the
naval conversations, due to American
opposition to Japan's proposal. (See
Embassy's telegram No. 239, October 31,
1934).
(h) Japan and the League of Nations.
Japanese news despatches from
Geneva indicate desire of Japan to retain
representation in certain of the League's
committees.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

Internal Political Affairs.
There have been newspaper rumors that
the Genro and other liberals close to the
Throne are dissatisfied with the Okada
Government, because of its failure to with-
stand heavy demands of the Army and Navy
and acquiescence in the Navy demand to
abrogate the Washington Naval Treaty, and
support of the Army demand for unification
of Japanese administrative organs in
Manchuria. As candidates for the post of
Premier, in case of the resignation of
Okada, Viscount Ishii, Viscount Saito
(former Premier) and Mr. Takahashi (former
Finance Minister) are mentioned. The
creation of an official commission to
investigate and advise the Cabinet in regard
to national problems and policies appears
about to materialize according to the
KOKUMIN. Diplomatic matters are not to be
in the scope of this commission. Its
definite organization and authority, however,
has not been announced.
In regard to Army and Navy affairs
nothing new is reported, except that accord-
ing to the CHUGAI SHOGYO the Japanese
Navy is considering the purchase of a large
Zeppelin dirigible from Germany as a war
reserve and intends to turn it over to a
commercial company to operate on a route from
Tokyo

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 12 -
Tokyo to Singapore via Osaka, Shanghai
and HongKong. The same paper says that the
Mavy intends to order two more Zeppelins in
the near future.
With a special session of the Diet
approaching, political parties have in-
creased their activities. The Seiyukai
leaders were making efforts to effect a
coalition with the Minseito in a common
cause against the Government. The Minseito
was cold toward the proposal. Diet dis-
cussion and interpellation is expected to
center around
1) the administrative reorganiza-
tion in Manchuria,
2) the Army pamphlet issued on
October 1,
3) the Naval Conference, and
4) agrarian relief.
There is still evidence of political opposi-
tion to the "Manchukuo" reform plan and
alleged lack of definite state policies.
Tokyo courts were active in imposing
sentences on alleged communists, including
a number of primary school teachers.
Procurators

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 13 -
Procurators were still occupied with the
the case of "God Sent Troops" in connection
with the reactionary conspiracy of July 1l,
1933. Indecision is reported as to whether
charges of intended murder, incendiarism,
eto., or a charge of rebellion, the convic-
tion for which would entail death or life
imprisomment, would be made.
Government Finance.
Increased taxation for the purpose of
reducing the anticipated deficit in the
1935 budget, as already reported in the
Embassy's despatch No. 1037, October 31, 1934,
was the center of interest. The Finance
Ministry on October 31 fixed the national
budget for the fiscal year 1935-36 at yen
2,028,800,000, a decrease of yen 113,728,000
compared with the budget for i934-35. (It is
now kmown that this figure has been greatly
exceeded owing to subsequent demands of the
Army and Navy)
Foreign Trade and Financial Trends.
Japan's foreign trade continues favorable
development, a favorable balance of trade for
October of yen 23,935,000 was achieved.
The press reported tariff movements in several
countries against Japanese imports, most
important of which was the Union of South
Comment
Africa,

Africa, and in consequence Japanese importers
are planning to increase wool purchases from
that market in an offort to forestall tariff
action unfavorable to Japan. The newspapers
commented favorably on the report of the
International Labor Office to the effect
that Japan is not guilty of "social dumping".
In financial quarters, the stock market
was erratic with a bearish tendency result-
ing from the Finance Minister's proposed tax
increase. Exchange was irregular and the yen
reached its lowest level since April, 1934.
The roeling prevailed that the yon-sterling
rate me would be maintained by the Yokohama
Specie Bank. The bond market was stronger
during October than it had been in September.
Fixed deposits reached an all-t ime high
record while loans fell to the lowest point
since November, 1922.
