I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES:
(a). The Departure of Mr. Hiroshi Saito for
the United States.
The Japanese Ambassador to the United States,
who returned to Japan on July 17, 1934, sailed for the
United States on October 11, 1934. During the month of
October Mr. Saito continued to make public utterances,
the substance of which was reported to the Department
in the Embassy's despatches No. 998 of October 5, No. 999
of October 5, and No. 1029 of October 31, 1934. The
general tenor of his co ment was that Japan was progressing
favorably in its diplomatic relations with the United States
and that there was nothing to fear in the future r lations
between the two countries. On October 4, at a meeting
of the America-Japan Society, he spoke on the curiously
chosen subject of "Menchukuo", with particular reference
to his satisfaction over the beneficial results that have
accrued to that region through Japanese "mediation". On
other occasions the press reported that Mr. Saito had made
the following statements, most of them in the course of
newspaper interviews: that the Japanese were determined
to remove the disgrace and dishonor imposed upon Japan
by the Washington and London Naval Treaties and that in
his opinion Japan would have more difficulty with Great
Britain than with the Ünited States at the 1935 naval
conference; that, in his belief, the Powers would not
find reason to oppose Japan's naval claims if Japanese
intentions for peace were fully appreciated and that he
would do everything in his power to do away with American
misgivings

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
misgivings on this score; that there were indications point-
ing toward the adoption of a quota system by the United
States for Japanese imnigrants; and that Japan was not in
competitien ith the United States in its newly acquired
markets in Central and South America, since the United States
sent machinery and such products and Japan exported cotton
textiles and general products to those regions.
The NICHI NICHI SHIMBUN of October 9 stated that the
result of Mr. Saito's conferences with Japanese higher offi-
cials during his threc months' stay in Japan was the formu-
lation of the following general policy with respect to the
United States:
1. To affirm tho friendly agreement expressed in
the Hull-Hirota goodwill notes, to the effect that
there is n: issue between Japan and the United
States which is incap ble of solution through diplo-
matic means.
2. Thoroughly to acquaint the United States with
Japan's naval disarmament formula, but in case of
failure of the disarmament confercnce to negotiate
the conclusion of some sort of an agreement between
Japan and the United States which would pledge the
two countries not to start a naval building race.
3. In view of the special nature of Japan's relations
with "Manchukuo" and China, Japan will continue to
follow an independent diplomatic policy with those
countries, at the same time fully respecting the
demand of the United States for the Open Door and
Equal Opportunity.
4.

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
4. Ambassador Saito is to describe to the United
States the real conditions in "Manchukuo", based
on the experience of his recent visit there, and
he is to prevent international legalization of
the Stimson Doctrine.
5. Mr. Saito will explain to the United States Go-
vernment that Soviet-Japanese relations are not
so serious as is frecuently reported in America
and that these relations have improved as a re-
sult of the agreement reached on the sale of the
Chinese Eastern Railway.
6. With regard to the immigration issue, Japan will
rely on America's justice and faith and will not
bring up the question of its own accord.
7. Markets in Central and South America for Japanese
goods will be secured as the only remaining markets.
8. Respecting the "anti-Japanese movement" in Arizona,
Japan wil? rely on the central and local authorities
to solve the issue quickly and fairly.
(b)
The Visit of the Honorable William C. Bullitt to
Japan en route to the United States.
Mr. William Christian Bullitt, American Ambassador to
Soviet Russia, arrived in Japan on October 23, and was the
Wm.
guest of the Ambassador during his visit of ten days in Japan.
Immediately after his arrival he was pressed for an inter-
view by a correspondent of the TOKYO NICHI NICHI and repeatedly
asked to express his opinion on various contentious subjects
upon which he firmly refused to comment. Nevertheless the
TOKYO NICHI NICHI of October 24 fabricated the following
interviey:

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
interview:
"Japan-America relations today seem to be
highly favorable as far as my observation
goes. But a section of the Japanese people
seems to entertain an anti-American sentiment.
I wonder why such an antagonistic sentiment is
entertained by a section of the people. I think
the Japanese navy, with the present ratio system,
will be quite sufficient to fulfill its duties for
national defense. I fail to understand why Japan
is so eager as (sic) to demand the abolition of
the ratio system. In my prosent trip, I shall in-
vestigate conditions of Japan and China as fully
as possible and submit the results of my investi-
gations to President Roosevelt as a reference for
the carrying on of his pro-Japanese policy."
The OSAKA MAINICHI of October 25, whose proprietors also
publish the TOKYO NICHI NICHI, quoted Mr. Bullitt erroneously
as follows:
"I cannot as a diplomatic representative, express
my views about conditions in Russia, but I can
say that to those who have not visited Russia since
1914 or thereabout, Russia at present is quite
different. The educational systems, sports, and
all industrial systems are ighly developed. A
subway is now being built in Moscow, and all other
cities are being modernized. All the Russians are
now very energetic."
"The Soviet's foreign policies have been greatly
changed within the past few years, and the solution
of the negotiations for the transfer of the North
Manchuria Railway is an outcome of it. The tension
along the Sovict-Manchukuo frontier regions is now
gone as far as I cen see."
"The Roosevelt administration will last for some
time, I think, and President Roosevelt has a very
goo understanding of Japan. I am going to make a
close study of conditions in Japan and China during
my present visit, so that I shall be able to assist
President Roosevelt in carrying out his policy of
bettering American relations with Japan."
Subsequently the offending correspondent issued a denial that
Mr. Bullitt had made the above-quoted statements.

Statements made by Dr. Harry A. Garfield. President

Dr. Harry A. Garfield was the guest of honor of the
America-Japan Society on October 4, and apparently in a
misguided attempt to improve American-Japanese relations,
compared the American attitude toward Japanese over-popula-
tion and Japanese immigration to the United States with Marie
Antoinette's famous remark about cake; referred to the Ex-

naval conference there would be danger of more blundering
"than has yet been permitted in the name of peace, if we
take counsel of fear and jealousy"; and praised the accom-
plishments of the Japenese Ambassador in Washington.*
(a) Japan and the Philiopine Islands.
Several members of the Philippine Police Patrol boarded
the MAIUN MARU within territorial waters of the Philippine
Islands on September : to investigate an alleged violation
of Philippine laws. They later reported that they had been
assaulted, injured, robi ei, and thrown overboard and abandoned
without visible refuge or succor under circumstances which
evidenced treachery and criminal premeditation. On October
19 the Embassy requested of the Japanese Foreign Office the
provisional arrest with a view to extradion of the master and
twenty-two members of the crew of the KAIUN MARU on a charge
of robbery and attempt to commit murder.** At the close of the
month it was not clear whether under the terms of the extra-
dition treaty between the United States and Japan the offenders
would be surrendered to the Untted States or would be prosecuted
in

In Japanese territory.

CONFIDENTIAL

to the Government by several reactionary societies urging
strong action in the Arizona case and that the Foreign Office
had in each case ma e efforts to minimize the situation.*
The Embassy was not molested by these ultra-patriotic societies.

had changed and a new al iance can be arranged if Japan de-

(b) The Netherlands Indies.
The negotiations between the Japanese and Dutch delegates
to the Trade Conference at Batavia went from bad to worse
during October and at the end of the month it looked as though
a standstill had been reached. The press states that the
Japanese have insisted and still insist on three points,
namely, that import quotas be based on the volume of trade
in the year 193?, that equal ratios be established for Dutch
anc Japanese importers in the Netherlands Indies, and that
the application of quotas be confined to approximately 10
articles, while the attitude of the Netherlands delegation
is that quotas must be fixed separetely and should not be
based on the year 1933, that Dutch traders in the Netherlands
Indies should be given a higher ratio of imports than the
Japanese traders, and that quotas must be fixed for approxi-
mately 60 articles. A further difficulty arose when the
Netherlands delegation made a suggestion that Japan increase
its imports of Javanese sugar in order to remedy the present
unbalanced state of tra e between the two countries. The
nemmpmporteported that the Dutch proposal contained the follow-
1ng

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
Ing requests: that the Japanese Government guarantee the
purchase by Japan of 150,000 tons of Javanese sugar this
year and 500,000 tons during each of the three succeeding
years; that the production of sugar in Formosa and the
mandated South Sea Islands be curtailed; that tn case the
production of sugar in "Manchukuo" should increase in the
future the production of this commodity in Formosa be cur-
tailed in proportion; that a maximum limit be fixed for the
production of sugar in Japan; and that Japan agree not to
buy sugar from any third country. The ASAHI SHIMBUN des-
cribed the alleged Dutch proposal as "highly presumptuous!
Another newspaper thought that the proposal proved the in-
sincerity of the Netherlands and several editorials decried
the proposal as an attempt at interference in Japanese domes-
tic affairs. The Japanese delegation, acting under orders
of the Japanese Government, is reported to have suggested
that the Netherlands delegation reconsider its proposal.
The Batavia parley appeared to be on the verge of a break-
down toward th close of the onth. However, a despatch
from Batavia dated October 26 appearing in the OSAKA MAINICHI
of October 28 stated that the trace conversations would con-
tinue in consequence of an eleventh hour decision on the
part of the Dutch delegation to consider a compromise on the
sugar issue. The Secretary of the Netherlands Legation in
Tokyo, in a conversation with one of the Secretaries of this
Embassy, seemed to be very pessimistic concerning the out-
come of the tra e conversations and stated that it was likely
that soon "there would be no more Batavia parley".

(1)
The Reform of Japanese Administration in Manchuria,
the Dispute betwcen the War Office and the Ministry
of Overseas Affairs and the Objections of the Civil
Police in "Manchukuo" against being placed under the
Army.
The change in the system of administration in the Kwan-
tung Leased Territory and in "Manchukuo" and the dispute
between the War Ministry and the Ministry of Overseas Affairs,
as well as the protest of the civil police in Manchuria
against being placed under the control of the Commander of
the Gendarmerie of the Kwantung Army, were fully discussed
in the Embassy's despatches No. 1005 of October 10 and 1019
of October 20. The final plan drawn up and unanimously ap-
proved by the members of the Cabinet provides that the former
triple system of control in Manchuria under the Ambassador
to "Manchukuo", the Commander-in-Chief of the Kwantung Army,
and the Governor General of the Kwantung Leased Territory
shall
Embassy's despatch No. 1035, November 1, 1934.

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
- 24 -
all be replaced by a dual system of control through the
Ambassador and the Commander-in-Chief of the Kwantung Army,
with the provision that the Ambassador and the Commander-
in-Chief shall be one and the same person who will, it is
understood, be a high ranking Army officer; this official
will be under the direct control of the Premier although
partly responsible to the Minister for Foreign Affairs for
purely diplomatic activities. He will have supervisory
and administrative powers over the South Manchuria Railway
Company, the Manchuria Telegraph and Telephone Company, and
the Civil Governor of the Kwantung Leased Territory. The
carrying out of this plan will practically eliminate the
Ministry of Ove seas Affairs from any part in the administra-
tion of anchuria and hence that Department bitterly pro-
tested against the reform. In ad ition to the abolishment
of the Overseas Affairs Ministry's administrative rights
in Manchuria, the new plan provides that the Commander of
the Gendarmerie of the Kwantung Army shal! hold the post of
Director of Police Affairs, and as such he will control the
Japanese civilian police in Manchuria, incluing the Kwan-
tung Government police. There ar about 5,Ooo of th se police.
The civil police who wil? be thus affected protested vigorous-
ly against the new measure, probably because they thought
that they would eventually lose their positions through usurpa-
tion of their functions by the military, and sent fourteen
members of their organization to Tokyo to present the point
of view of the civil police as against the Army in Manchuria.
This delegation issued a statement to the newspapers of which
the following is the translation that appeared in THE JAPAN
ADVERTISER:
"We

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
- 25 -
"We are not resorting to the underhand measures
practiced by some participants in this dispute.
The military is projecting an autocratic adminis
tration in Manchuria, which will not promote the
welfare of Japan and is prone to disturb order
in Manchuria. The population of Manchuria does
not enjoy military administration. To cite an
example, the Army has set fire to and consumed
in flame all the villages along the road stretch-
ing for 20 miles between Shanchengchen and Tunghwa,
alleging that the villages have been harboring
bandits. We question that that is the way to
enhance the friendly feeling of the Manchurian
people.
If the military really believes in the reorgani-
zation formula, why does it apply pressure on
freedom of opinion in Manchuria? We demand fair
play in this dispute, and we mean to fight to the
last man literally as protectors of the Constitu-
tion."
Five of the Tokyo newspapers, including the ADVERTISER, were
suppressed for publishing this statement.
The Cabinet, reputedly under pressure from the Army,
issued an announcement on October 18 that the original scheme
for the reform of Manchurian administration would be carried
out. As a consequence, a number of the civilian officials
of the Kwantung Government sent in their resignations and
the newspapers stated that the Kwantung police had threatened
to resign en masse. These officials and the police, however,
remained at their posts. When a peaceful settlement of the
controversy appeared probable Premier Okada appointed Count
Hideo Kodama as Minister for Overseas Affairs and this ap-
pointment was reported to be satisfactory to the military.
According to a RENGo despatch from Port Arthur dated October
27, the whole trouble in Manchuria was settled when it was
officially announced that all civil officials and policemen
would withdraw the resignations which they had tendered to
General Hishikari. Apparently the civil elements felt that
they

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
- 26 -
could no longer hold out any resistance against the
my, which was firmly determined to carry through the whole
plan of reorganization.

(g) Naval Conference.
The preliminary naval conversations between the United
States and Japan, the United States and Gr at Britain, and
Great Britain and Japan were opened in Lon on on October
23 with the following delegations: Mr. Norman Davis, Ameri-
can Ambassador-at-large, and Rear Admiral Standley, Chief
of Naval Operations, for the United States; Premier Ramsay
MacDonald and Sir Bolton Eyres-Monsell, First Lord of the
Admiralty, for Great Britain; and Hr. Tsuneo Matsudaira,
Ambassador to Great Britain, and Rear Admiral Isoroku Yamamoto
for Japan. On October 23 the first meeting between the Bri-
tish and Japanese delegations tool: place and on the following
day the American and Japanese delegates met for iscussion.
According to Japanese newspaper despatches from London, the
Japanese proposals and arguments, made separately to the
British and American delegations, may be summarized as follows:
Japan has definitely decided to abrogate the Washington Naval
Treaty of 1922; Japan demands naval armament based on the
principle of non-threat and non-invasion; Japan insists
upon abolition of the ratio system and favors the adoption
of

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-27-
a "common upper limit" under which each nation may
build as it sees fit; Japan favors the abolition of capi-
tal ships and aircraft carriers; Japan would like & redue-
tion in the tonnage of cruisers; and Japan is opposed to
the abolition of submarines.
The Japanese newspaper version of Great Britain's at-
titude in the preliminary naval conversations up to the close
of the month is as follows: Gr at Britain desires to retain
the substance of the Washington and London Treaties, prefers
limitation of tonnage by categorical classification instead
of by global tonnage, would like to lower the maximum ton-
nage of capital ships to 5,00 tons, opposes the abolition
of aircr ft carriers, wants to maintain the status guo of
10,Oo0-ton cruisers, and advocstes either the abolition of
or restriction of the number of submarines.
With regard to the position of the United States, most
of the newspapers in Japan reported that at the close of the
month the American-Japanese celegates had been unable to
agree on any point, while the British and Japanese delegations
had apparently agrced on a number of points. Both Japanese
press reports and editorial opinion gavc the impression that
there was a lack of hor ony in th proceedings of the naval
conversations and that this was due to American opposition
to Japan's proposals.*
The actual progress of the preliminary naval conversations
as reported confidentially from Loncon are not included in
this report.

(m) Uruguay.
According to a RENGO despatch from Dairen dated

INTERNAL POBITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinete
There were newspaper rumors that the Genro, the Keeper
of the Privy Seal and other liberals close to the Throne
were dissatisfied with the Okada Government, chiefly because
it had been unable to resist the demands of the Army and
the Navy, since it consented to the Navy's demand that the
Washington Naval Treaty be abrogated and gave in to the
Army's demand for the unification of all Japanese adminis-
trative organs in Manchuria under the control of the Army.*
The possible candidates that have been mentioned for
the post of Premier are Viscount Ishii (formerly Minister
for Foreign Affairs an Ambassador to the United States),
Viscount Saito (recently Premier), and Mr. Takahashi (Fi-
nance Minister in the Saito Cabinet). THE TEITO NICHI NICHI
thinks that Premier Okada"has proved himself virtually useless
in controlling the military," and with refe ence to the Man-
churian administrative reform controversy THE CHUGAI of
October 19 stated that from the standpoint of official dis-
cipline "his (the Premier's) attitude vas deplorable".
On October 5 Count Hideo Kodama, former Governor of
the Kwantung Leased Territory an former Civil Governor of
Korea, was appointed inister for Overseas Affairs, a post that
had been held by Premier Okada.
The creation of an official commission to investigate
national problems and advise the Cabinet in the formulation
of policies which has been so frequently mentioned in the
press in recent months** has been decided upon, according
to the
*Embassy's despatch No. 1019 of October 20, 1934.
**See previous Monthly Reports.

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
- 31 -
the KOKUMIN, by Premier Okada after his study of the sug-
gestions submitted to him by Home Minister Fumio Goto,
Takejiro Tokonami, Minister of Communications,and Chuji
Machida, Minister of Commerce and Industry. According to
this newspaper, the three Ministers above-mentioned are
said to have recommended to the Premier that the proposed
Commission should not be used as an organ thoough which
the Government might negotiate with the political parties,
but that it should be simply an institution which the
Government might consult in reference to national policies
other than iplomatic matters an questions of national
defense.
(b) The Army.
On October 1, the Press Section of the War Office issued
a pamphlet the Japanese title of which (when translated)
means "A Dissertation on the Basic Purposes of National De-
fense an Means or Strengthening Such Defense". A transla-
tion of this document was transmitted to the Department with
the Embassy's despatch No. 1031 of November 1, 1954, which
contained a summary and an analysis of it. There have been
about 20 of such pamphlets issued in the past two years, but
this one was the first that suggested reform of the national
Government. In describing the various elements which compose
the defense of a nation and the affect of present-day in-
ternational relations upon the national defense of Japan,
it contains suggestions for increasing and coordinating
Japan's defenses. It mentions the United States as Japan's
possible maritime enemy and Soviet Russia as Japan's possible
land enemy. It contains a threat of arms against Great
Britain

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
-32 -
ritain and The Netherlands in the event that those coun-
tries should carry on "unjust (commercial) competition to
the last". The features of the pamphlet which called forth
the greatest comment were its advocacy of a kind of state
socialism and its criticism of the present economic system
in Japan. This particular aspect of the pamphlet caused
fears of a military revolution to bring about the desired
economic reforms. The War Minister, General Hayashi,
is reported by RiliGo to have encorsec the pamphlet but to
have st ted at the same time that "the Army itself does
not mean to carry out what is mentioned in the publication."
General apprehensionsregarding a possible revolution were
allayed by this statoment of the inister of War. Adverse
criticism of the War linistry's publication appeared in the
Tokyo JIJI, the KOKUIIN, ASAHI, MIYAKO and the HOCHI.
(c) The Navy.
According to the CHUGAI SHOGYO of November 11, the
Japanese Navy is considering the purchase of a large Zeppelin
dirigible from Germany as a war reserve, and intends to turn
it over to a commercial aviation company to be organized for
the purpose of establishing a commercial route from Tokyo to
Singapore, via Osaka, Fukuoka, Shanghai and HongKong. This
newspaper also reports that the Japanese Navy intends to order
two ad itional Zeppelins in thc near futurc. An article
which appeared in the JA A C O ICLE of October 13 regarding
the report that Japan is to acquire thrce Zeppelins was ob-
jected to by the Ministry of Home Affairs at Tokyo and this
edition of the CHRONICLE was suppressed by the police, practi-
cally all of the copies being confiscated.
(a) Political Parties.
As the

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.98)
- 33 -
As the reported convening of the special session of the
Diet approached, the political parties increased their acti-
vities. It was reported during the month that Seiyukai
leaders were making renewed efforts to persuade the Minseito
to agree t a coalition of the tvo parties to make common
cause against the Government, but that the Minseito had
been cool towards these proposals. Among the subjects which
it is understood will come up for discussion and interpella-
tion in the Diet are (1) the administrative reorganization
in Manchuria, (2) the Army pamphlet issued on October 1,
(3) the Naval Conference, and (4) agrarian relief.
Dr. Suzuki, Pr sident of the Seiyukai, delivered a
speech on October 1l in which he attacked the Government
on the inadecuacy of relief measures, the "Manchukyo" reform
plan and the alleged lack of a definite state policy. He
anunciated the following platform:
1. We strive for the establishment of foreign
policies tending to promote industry.
2.
We strive for the abolition of the naval ratio
an for the autonomous right of national defense.
We strive for th establishment of a sound finan-
cial policy through proper operation of the loan
policy an readjustment of taxes.
4.
We strive for thorough-going relief measures for
the entire population. engaged in farming, fishing
an forestry, an fc the restoration of rural
communities.
(e) Communism.
The Tokyo Appellate Court imposed sentences varying from
two to three years on thirteen former primary school teachers
of Nagano Prefecture who had been arrested in a round-up
of communists in February, 1932. These men were convicted
of spreading subversive ideas among school children.
(f) Reactionaries.

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
- 34 -
(r) Reactionaries.
With regard to the seemingly endless proceedings
against the "God Sent Troops" in connection with the reac-
tionary revolution conspiracy frustrated on July 1l, 1933,
by the arrest of a large part of the conspirators, THE
JAPAN ADVERTISER of October 27 reports that the procurators
are still undecided as to whether to try the six leaders
and their fifty-six followers on charges of intended murder,
intendec incendiarism and violation of the explosives law,
for which only prison sentences can be decreed, or on the
graver charge of rebellion, the conviction for which would
entail death or life imprisonment for the leaders and long
prison sentences for the followers.

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a) Foreign Trade.
According to the preliminary figures, Japan's exports
for October were ¥201,356,00 (approximately $60,406,800)
while imports were ¥177,431,00, representing a favorable
balance of trace of ¥25,925,000.
The press reporte that an increase in customs tariff
which would adversely a fect Japanese imports was being
considered by Costa Ric, the Union of South Africa, and
Haiti, and tiut Japanese importe's ha decided to buy
more wool from South Africa and more raw cotton from Haiti
in an effort to forestall the cont plated tariff increase
in those two countries.
Favor ble comment appearec in the newspapers concern-
ing se ar te reports issued by the Foreign Policy Associa-
tion of the United States, r. Maurette of the International
Labor Office, and Mr. J.G. Latham, former Australian Minis-
ter for Foreign Affairs, to the effect that Japan is not
guilty of "social dunping".
(b)
Financial Trends.
The stoct market was very erratic, with a bearish
tendency resulting from the Finance Minist r's announcement
that taxes would be increased. Although the exchange was
and
irregular/the Yen reached its lowest level since April,
1934, there was a general feeling that the yen-sterling
rate would be maintained by the Yokohama Specie Bank. The
bond market was stronger curing October than it had been
in September. Fixed deposits attained an all-time high
record, while loans reached their lowest mark since
November

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
November, 1922.
(c) Rice.
The price of rice soared phenomenally during October.
It is predicted that this season's rice crop wil! be the
smallest that has been produced in the past twenty years
as a result of drought, flood, the Osaka typhoon, and in-
sect ravages.
(d) Industries.
According to the newspapers, the industrial establish-
ments in and around Osaka which suffered from the tidal wave
and typhoon of September Sl*, were rapidly restored to normal
operating conitions.
It is reporte that the Japanese import tariff on auto-
mobile parts wil be materially increased if the Government's
policy of encouraging the domestic automobile industry fails.
THn JAPA TIMES of October 15 reported that the Nissan Jidosha
Kaisha planne to produce 10,Oo0 Datsun automobiles a year.

IX. LABOR AND EMIGRATION.
(a) Labor.
At the beginning of the month the Tokyo tramway and
bus employees declared another strike following a temporary
truce. On October 13 this strike came to an end through
a compromise agreement and approximately 10,400 employees
returned to work on the following day.
(b) Emigration.
The leading newspapes contained advertisements which
832.5594
set forth the advantages of becoming a land-owming farmer
in Brazil and described the aid offered by the Federation
of Unions for Promoting Emigration to Brazil, which includes
reduced railway rates in Japan, free lodging, free ocean
passage, and assistance to the emigrants in the matter of
settling on the land. These advertisements also called
attention to a pamphlet issued by the Ministry of Overseas
Affairs, which is a sort of official advertisement urging
the Japanese people to emigrate to Brazil.*
GDA.

Tokyo's monthly polifieal report for
November, 1934. (Portions of this report
covered by separate despatches are not re-
viewed here below.)
The Japanese press commented favorably
on the Democratic victories in the November
elections and praised President Roosevelt's
administration.
The Chinese Eastern Railway problem
attracted less public attention during
November. The general belief prevailed that
a complete settlement would be reached
eventually. The Japanese-"Manchukuo" side
seems reluctant to bind itself to detailed
torms, and the Soviets are apparently con-
vinced that time is in their favor.
Negotiations looking toward revision of the
Shidehara-Troyanowsky fishery agreement are
expected to begin in Moscow in May, 1935.
Although there is less tension between Japan
and the U.S.S.R. at present, a feeling of
uncertainty persists. The alleged Franco-
Soviet "alliance" occasioned some uneasiness,
and Ambassador Bullitt's interviews with
Chinese officials aroused suspicion that a
secret

decreased.

(a). The O1l Monopoly Lay in "Manekmima"
On November 5 the Japanese Foreign Office
submitted a reply to the Embassy's informal memorandum
dated August 31, 1934, which constituted the seoond
representations of the Amerioan Government on the
"Manohukuo" oil monopoly project. The Japanese reply
stated that the plans of the "Manchukuo" Government for
the regulation of the oil industry were projeots of that
Government and were not within the knowledge or conoern
of the Japanese Government; that although respect for the
prinoiples of the Open Door by "Manchukuo" coincided with
the wishes of the Japanese Government, in a case of a
difference of opinion between "Manchukuo" and a third
government regarding the application of the Open Door
principle the Japanese Government could not be responsible
for the industrial policy of "Manohukuo"; that the Manchuria
Oil Company was given no monopolistic privileges and the
Japanese Government could find no reason to forbid Japanese
investments in that company; and that according to the
information at hand the Government ofManchukuo" did not
intend to purohase from the Manchuria Oil Company all the
oil which it would sell and that the interests of foreign
concerns would be considered as far as possible in petroleum
purchases.* This reply made almost no attempt to refute the
arguments set forth in the Embassy's informal memorandum of
August 3l, entirely ignoring the question of the treaty rights
of American nationals in Manchuria and in effect renouncing
the various deolarations of the Japanese Government that the
Open Door principle would be preserved in Manchuria. **

(b) The Japanese Petroleum Industry Law.
The foreign oil companies operating in Japan
received letters from the Minister of Commerce and Industry
on November 6 which demanded that they submit their import
and storage plans by November 15. Each letter concluded
with the following statement: "In case you fail to respond
to our request promptly, a smooth execution of this company's
business plan for 1935 might be interfered with".* The
foreign oil companies requested an extension of time for
the submission of import and storage plans for 1935. The
Department of Commerce and Industry on November 20 again
requested the Tokyo representatives of the Standard Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company to file the
companies' import and storage plans for l935 immediately,
with the further statement that failure to do so would result
in penalization of the companies.
At a conference held on November 20 between the Vice
Minister of Commeroe and Industry and Mr. Kurusu, Chief of
the Commeroial Bureau of the Foreign Office, on one side
and the representatives of the American and British oil
companies on the other, Mr. Kurusu stated that the Japanese

) The Request of the Philippine Government for the

In connection with the request of the Governor
General of the Philippine Islands for the provisional arrest,
with a view to extradition, of the Master and twenty-two
members of the crew of the KAIUN MARU, a Japanese vessel,*
Mr. Serafin Hilado, Solicitor General of the Philippine
Islands, proceeded to Japan, arriving in Tokyo on November 17
with the formal request for a Writ of extradition, the
Warrant for Arrest, and supporting affidavits containing
the
* See Monthly Political Report for Ootober, 1934.

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
testimony in the case. On the same day the Embassy
received the reply of the Japanese Foreign Office to its
request for extradition. This reply stated that no ground
had been found by the oompetent authorities for the charge
of robbery and attempt to commit murder and that accordingly
the Japanese Government did not consider extredition an
appropriate action. The Foreign Office was informed by
the Embassy of Mr. Hilado's mission before his arrival and
of his presence in Tokyo after his arrival. The Embassy
received from the Foreign Office the intimation that
collaboration between the Japanese authorities and Mr.
Hilado would not be feasible and accordingly the Solicitor
General returned to the Philippines, since it was his and
the Embassy's view that if he remained longer in Japan it
would be to no avail. The Embassy had been informed orally
by the Foreign Office that "the Japanese Government would
be glad to have the papers in the case". In view of the
attitude of the Foreign Office toward the facts of the case,
the Embassy addressed a further note to the Minister for
Foreign Affairs dated November 26, enclosing the documents
which Mr. Hilado had brought to Japan. The Embassy's note
expressed the hope that a careful review of the case would
bring about the granting of extradition by the Japanese
Government and also took exception to the Foreign Office's
statement that no ground had been found for the charge
alleged against the master and orew of the KAIUN MARU.

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Soviet Russia.
Although the Foreign Minister and the Soviet
Ambassador have continued active negotiations for the
sale of the Chinese Bastern Railway, progress has been
slow. Towards the end of the month the Embassy learned*
that an impasse had been reached over the question of a
Japanese guarantee of the purchase price. Japan has
sought to evade this question by maintaining that it is
not in accordance with the dignity of "Manchukuo" for the
Soviet Government to question its good faith while the
Soviets have quite naturally insisted on securing the
contract with a guarantee from "Manchukuo's" sponsor.
Another difficulty has to do with machinery for the
arbitration of disputes. Soviet Russia proposed that a
neutral Chamber of Commerce arbitrate any questions which
might arise during the exeoution of the sales contract,
notably, it is believed, in connection with the payments
in kind, but Japan has objected. Inasmuch as Japan is
causing the purchase of the railroad in part for the
purpose of clearing North Manchuria of foreign influence,
It is difficult for her to compromise on this point.
The Chinese Eastern Railroad problem attracted less
public attention during November, but the general belief
prevailed that a complete settlement would be reached in
the end. Nevertheless there has been little disposition
to make haste. The Japanese-"Manchukuo" side seems
reluctant to bind itself to detailed terms, and the
Soviets are apparently convinced that time is in their
favor.
Meanwhile

Meanwhile several items having to do with the

Although there is less tension between Japan and the
USSR at present**, a feeling of instability persists under
the surface. The alleged Franco-Soviet "alliance" occasioned
uneasiness and was prominently reported by the vernacular
papers; the NIPPON DEMPO report of Ambassador Bullitt's
interviews with Chinese officials voiced the suspicion that
Mr. Bullitt was negotiating a secret agreement between the
United States, the Soviet Union, and China; and several
articles in the reputable KAIzO magazine betrayed anxiety
rogarding
*mbassy's despatoh No. 1058, November 16, 1934.
** Embassy's despatch No. 1070, November 30, 1934.

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.98)
regarding the Russian situation and advoeated plans for a
general settlement between the Ussk and Japan in the Far
East. One such article urged the purchase of the Maritime
Province and the Amur Basin by Japan and another the oon-
clusion of a non-aggression pact. The Japanese people as
a whole are beginning to realize that it is no longer pos-
sible to disregard the Soviet Union as a political and
military factor. A prominent Japanese recently stated
privately that the powerful military preparations made by
the Soviet Government in Siberia during the past two years
had contributed more to preserving peace in the Far East
than any other single factor.
The military, however, continues to sponsor anti-Soviet
propaganda. A recent moving picture (The Crisis of) "1936"
depicts the Soviet menace vividly. It is probable that there
are few subjects on which the cleavage between the civilian
and military point of view is so distinct. A powerful and
costly military machine must justify itself and, viewed in
this light, the Russian situation is an asset to the Japanese
army. For this reason, so long as the present situation
persists in Japan, it is difficult to foresee how the pro-
ponents of reason and moderation can effect a general
rapprochement with the USSR. A piece-meal settlement through
negotiation and barter appears more likely and this, in fact,
coincides with the professed policy of the Foreign Minister.

or three of the signatories.

Japan, the League of Nations, and the Japanese
Mandated Islands.
A newspaper despatch from Geneva stated that the
League of Netions Mandates Commission would take note of
reports concerning the alleged fortification of Japan's
mandated islands in the South Seas. This report called
forth an emphatic denial from the Japanese Foreign Office
that Japan had established military struotures on the

the newspapers, also asserted that the League of Nations
had no ultimate authority over Japan's mandated territory
and that Japan would continue to administer the Islands
after it ceased to belong to the League of Nations in
March, 1935.

INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRB.
The Cabinet.
(a)
The Appointment of Mr. Korekiyo Takahashi as
Minister for FAnance.
Mr. Sadanobu Fujii, the Minister for Finance,
resigned on November 26, alleging ill health as his reason
for this step.* On the same day Premier Okada summoned Mr.
Korekiyo Takahashi, who had been Finance Minister in the
Saito Cabinet, asking him to succeed Mr. Fujii, which offer
was accepted by the veteran statesman on the following day.
It was believed in political circles that the acceptance
of the post of Finance Minister by Mr. Takahashi, who had
previously. occupied that portfolio on six oocasions and
had once been Premier, would definitely strengthen the
position of the Okada Cabinet.**
(b) The Political Parties.
On November 26, two days before the convening
of the extraordinary session of the Diet, the Seiyukai and
Minseito political parties issued a joint statement announcing
a "coalition". The so-called coalition appeared to be more
in the nature of a temporary truce during the special session
of the Diet than a definite coalition of interests.***
Public statements made by Seiyukai and Minseito leaders
prior to the convening of the Diet on November 28, combined
with the actions and words of Party leaders since the
formation of the Okada Cabinet, indicated that the Seiyukai
would continue to oppose the Government and the Minseito
to support it, despite the announced "coalition". The

After it had become known that Mr. Takahashi had
accepted the post of Finance Minister, the directors of
the Seiyukai approached Mr. Takahashi, requesting him to
resign from the Party voluntarily, to which the Finance
Minister is reported to have replied that he saw no reason
why he should retire from the Party. Subsequently, on
November 28, the Seiyukai issued a newspaper statement to
the effect that they had been forced by circumstances "to
part with" Mr. Korekiyo Takahashi.* The probable reason
that Mr. Takahashi was not formally expelled from the
Seiyukai Party in a more positive fashion was that he had
once been its President and had always held an influential
place in its councils.

of 1932. According to these newspapers, Konume was accused

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 15, 1934.
Tokyo's monthly report for December,
10s4, was prepared practically with a view
to meeting the Department's wishes in
rogard to the form and scope of monthly
reports. It is only about one-third the
longth of the monthly reports that the
Department has been receiving from Tokyo
during the past eighteen months.
You will note that most of the items
consist of summaries of previous despatches
and telegrams. There is, therefore, little
that is new of interest to the Division.
Your attention is drawn, however, to the
following:
"Provocative statements" (page 2).
The Foreign Ofrice spokesman is reported
to have stated that various agencies of
the Japanese Government have been cooperat-
ing with a view to preventing the expres-
sion of provocative views by Japanese and
that the results have been satisfactory,
but that irritating utterances were being
made "on the other side of the Pacific".
Antimony monopoly in Hunan Province
(page 9). Following protests by the
Japanese Consulate at Changsha, the
negotiations for the granting by the Hunan
Provincial

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Provincial Government of an antimony
monopoly to a British firm have been
discontinued.
Land loases in Manchuria (page 13).
Announcement was made that Japanese have
been granted the right to lease land in
North Manchuria for a period of thirty
years, subject to renewal.
Japanese-Netherland East Indian
Commercial Negotiations (page 13).
A
recent telegram from the Commercial
Attaché at Tokyo reports that the shipping
conference which, it was agreed at the
Batavia conference, was to be held in
Kobe, was never opened, owing to the
inability of the Japanese and Netherland
delegations to agree upon the language
which was to be the official language of
the conference.
Air defense law (page 15). What was
claimed to be the first air defense law
was drafted by the War Office and is to
be submitted to the Diet now in session.

POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES:
(a) The Foreign Office Spokesman's Commants on
Representative Vinson's Statements concern-
Ing the Necessity of Constructing a Canal
through Nicaragua.
On December 17 the Tokyo newspapers featured a
statement said to have been made by Representative Vinson
to the effect that the time was near when the United States
would be forced to construct a canal through Nicaragua,
principally because of the inadequacy of the Panama Canal
locks. At the Foreign Office Press Conference on the same
day, Mr. Eiji Amau said that from the standpoint of Japanese-
American friendship Mr. Vinson's plan for a Nicaraguan
canal was "most untimely", that the Panama Canal was still
sufficient for commercial requirements, and that hence the
Nicaraguan project was obviously for military purposes.*
(b) The RENGO Statement concerning the United States Navy.
The RENGO News Agency on December 19 issued an article
asserting that President Roosevelt had pursued a policy of
attempting to intimidate Japan by bluff, that the shipbuild-
ing ability of America was inferior to Japan's, and that
the Japanese Navy's personnel was far superior to that of
the American Navy in morale and courage.**
(c) Adverse Reaction to a Reported Change in the United
States's "Freedom of the Seas" Policy.
During the month several Japanese newspapers commented
adversely on the report that the United States was contem-
plating a change in its policy of "freedom of the seas" in
time of war.*** One newspaper went so far as to interpret
such
*Department's telegram No. 207, December 18, 1934, and
Embassy's telegram No. 279, December 19, 1934.
*Embassy's despatch No. 1116, December 29, 1934.
**Embasy's despatch No. 1119, December 31, 1934.

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Juch an alteration in American policy as a subtle desim
to enable President Roosevelt to declare war.
(d) Foreign Office Opinions regarding Provocative State-
ments.
1934,
According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of December 25, the
Foreign Office spokesman welcomed the report that the Ameri-
can Government desired to prevent the utterance in the
United States of disquieting statements regarding the naval
issue. The Foreign Office spokesman is said to have made
the statement that the Foreign Office, cooperating with the
War, Navy, and Home Offices, had been endeavoring for the
past year to prevent the expression of jingoistic views that
might provoke foreign opinion and that the result had been
satisfactory. He is also reported to have said that no pro-
vocative statements on the part of Japanese had come to the
notice of the Japanese authorities but that from the other
side of the Pacific" many irritating utterances had been
heard.
(e) KAIUN MARU CASE
By a note dated December 17 the Japanese Minister for
Foreign Affairs informed the Embassy that the Japanese Govern-
ment could not see its way under the Treaty of Extradition
to surrender the master and crew of the KAIUN MARU in view
of the fact that al? the men concerned were Japanese subjects.*

Anti-Alien Agitation in Arizona.
Interpellated on December 8 in the House of Peers con-
cerning the steps taken by the Japanese Government in the
anti-alien Arizona case, Mr. Hirota replied that he had
lodged a protest at Washington "grave in comparison with
usual diplomatic eticuette". When his interpellator remarked
that no arrests had been made in Arizona and described the
state of affairs there as "barbarous", Mr. Hirota expressed
doubts that the acts of violence had been committed by Ameri-
can citizens and suggested that these acts had been perpe-
trated by aliens who wished to disturb Japanese-American
relations.*
(1)
Reaction to Senator Nye's Statcments Supporting Japanese
Naval Claims.
As is always the c se when prominent Americans make
statements detrimental to the United States or complimentary
to Japan, the Japanese press gave considerable attention to
Senator Nye's speech delivered before the Foreign Policy As-
sociation on November 4, in which the Senator supported
Japan's claims to naval parity.** The OSAKA MAINICHI of
December 23 attributed the following stat ment to Senator Nye,
alleged to have been made by him in an interview by trans-
Pacific telephone:
"Japan has a right to feel secure, with a free hand in
the Far East and the same degree of freedom from interven-
tion without that America enjoys in the western hemisphere.
The United States ought to be foir enough to agree on naval
equality with Japan....."

ported that one purpose of the plan is fish conservation, but
that the principal object is to unify Japanese interests be-

Treaty which expires in 1936.

Drance.
1. The Police Accusations of Espionage against Lieutenant
du Cros, Assistant Maval Attaché of the French Embassy.
All the Japanese newspapers, with the exception of the
ASAHI, on December 11 published an attack against Lieutenant
Tessier du Cros, Assistant Naval Attaché of the French Embassy,
accusing him of espionage and immoral conduct. The allegations
were given to the press by the police without prior notice to
either the French Embassy or the Foreign Office. On December
12, the French Ambassador addressed a strong note of protest
to the Minister for Foreign Affairs, asserting that the charges
of espionage had absolutely no foundation. A week or so later
areply was received fron the Minister for Foreign Affairs re-
tracting the charges of espionage. Subsequently the French
Naval Attaché, Captain de la Noe, obtained a written communi-
cation fron the Navy Department which expressed regret over the
du Cros incident and stated that the Navy Department consider-
ed to be without foundation the charges of spying published
by the press.* Captain de la Noe issued a statement to the
press embodying this letter fro. the Navy Department, which
was published in the JAPAN ADVERTISER and in THL JAPAN TIMES
but did not appear in any of the Japanese language newspapers,
despite the fact that the Japanese correspondents had agreed
to have it published in their respective papers.**
2. Projected French Commercial Mission to "Manchukuo".
A Secretary in the French Embassy informed a member of
the Embassy's staff that the projected French commercial
mission to "Manchukuo"*** had been postponed but that the
mission would probably proceed to "Manchukuo" some time
during the year 1935.

(h) "Manchukuo"
1. Appointment of General Minami to the Dual Post of
Commander of the Kwantung Army and Ambassador to
Manchukuo"
General Jiro Minami, Ministor for War at the time of the
Manchurian outbreak in l93l, was appointed to the dual post
of Commander of the Kwantung Army an Ambassador to "Manchu-
kuo" on December 10, succeeding General Takashi Hishikari. It
is understood that this change in personnel will not involve
any change in Japan's Manchurian policy.** According to the
JAPAN ADVERTISER of December 16, the Cabinet, under pressure
from General Minami, has decided to convoke a Japan-Manchukuo"
economic conference for the purpose of establishing an inter-
national economic policy that will operate to the mutual ad-
vantage of the two countries. En route to his new post,
General Minami is reported to have stated in a speech at Osaka
on December 2l, that "spiritual cooperation" between Japan
and "Manchukuo" could be perfected only through economic ties.
After having presented his credentials to Emperor Kangte at
Hsinking, he is said to have told a newspaper correspondent
that no Japanese Cabinet would dare to put obstacles in the
way of Japan's fundamental policies toward "Manchukuo" and
that these policies, already definitely formulated, would not
be changed even though there should be political changes in
Japan.**

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS:
(a) The Cabinet.
In a New Year's message issued on December 31, Premier
Okada stated that Japan was facing "a mountain of difficulties"
at home and abroad but that it would steadfastly pursue the
course fixed for the nation 3,0o0 years ago; that there was
nothing strange in the difficulties encountered by Japen in
its process of expansion; and that the task before every
Japanese was to promote national welfare and to exert efforts
to make the principles of international justice prevail.
The Minister for Forcign Aff ir: declared in his New
Year's message of December 31 that difficulties always beset
the path of a rising Power, but thet Japan was steadily conso-
lidating its position as the s'rongest stablizing force in the
Far East.
(b) The Army.
The December newspapers reportcd that the first comprehen-
sive air defense law "t ic promulgated" in thc worlc had been
drafted by the War Office and would be submitted to the Diet
for appoval, the principl purpose of the proposed law being
the training of civilians in the matter of air defense during
peace time in order that they may be prepared for an emergency.
Japanese Army officers, particularly the junior officers,
werereparted to be distinctly dissatisfied with the War Minister's
"mild" answers to interpellations in the Diet concerning the
last Army pamphlet.*
(c)
Embassy' despatch No. 1031,November 1, anc also despatch No.

The Navy.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of December 31 reported that, in-
terviewed by a representative of the ASAHI, Admiral Nobumasa
Suetsugu, Commandant of the Yokosuka Naval Station and former
Commander of the Combined Fleet, had said that he was un-
impressed" by the threats (presumably American) of a naval
race and that the Japanese preferred a non-treaty situation
to the present ratio.
(d) The Diet.
The extraordinary session of the Diet, which had been
convened on November 8, was formally adjourned on December
10, after it had passec th relief budget as submitted by
the Government. Aside fron the legislation enacted, there
were the following developments in the Diet proceedings: a
loss ofprestige by the Seiyukai owing to its defeat by the
Government; the failure of efforts to restore the functioning
of constitutional Government in Japan; further proof of the
overwhelming influence of the military in Japanese political
life; ani an additional demonstration of the political skill
of the veteran st tesman, Mr. Takahashi, who was appointed
Finance Minister one day prior to the opening of the Diet.*
The 67th regular session of the Diet was convoked on
December 26 and was adjourned on the following day to be re-
sumed on January 21, 1935.
(e) Political Parties.
The interpellat ons and actions of th political parties
during the proceedings of tic extraordinary session of the
Diet were fully dealt with in the Embassy's despatch No. 1088

cember 10. Mr. Kiyoshi Akita resigned as Speaker of
Lower House on December 12 and later resigned from the
Beiyukai Party. When the ordinary session of the Diet con-
vened on December 26, Mr. Kunimatsu Hamada of the Seiyukai
was elected Speaker of the Lower House. It was reported by
the newspapers that in the Diet the Minseito had made it clear
that they would not cooperate with the Seiyukai during the
present session.
(f) Reactionaries.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of December 3O, on
the preceding day the Japanese authorities lifted a press ban
and disclosed that seven persons, including three unemployed
men, two coal miners, the daughter of a colliery employee,
and a casual laborer, had been arrested for having plotted to
kill at least five leading statcsmen and business officials.
RENGo reported that the men who were to have been killed as
they returned from the Imperial Chrysanthemum Party in Novem-
ber, 1933, were: Prince Saionji, the Genro; Count Makino, Lord
Keeper of the Privy Seal; Mr. Takahashi, Minister for Finance;
Mr. Takakimi Mitsui;and Baron Koyata Iwasaki. The persons ar-
rested are said to have belonged to an organization which they
called "The Great Sacred War" and "The Patriotic Iron Blood-
hood".

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.98)

ENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS:
(a)
Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for December were ¥209,119,000 and its
imports were ¥252,572,000. There was an unfavorable trade ba-
lance of ¥43,453,000, as compared with a favorable balance
of ¥12,656,000 for November.
The Ministry of Finance announced the following foreign
trade figures for the year 1934: Exports, ¥2,258,000,000; Im-
ports, ¥2,400,000,000. Exports increased 16.9% and imports
increased 19% as compared with the year 1933. Japan's unfavor-
able balance of trade was 6 greater in 1934 than in 193.
At the end of the year 1934 the Foreign Office announced
that in l935 it would change its foreign trade policy from
one of passive resistance to one of progressive expansion.
The OSAKA MAINICHI states that Japan has gained the confidence
that because of "her advanced inustrial technology and her
army of industrial workers, she need not fear artificial trade
barriers set up against the current of natural demand and sup-
ply".
(b) Financial Trends.
The money market was tighter; the yen rate was weak, due
to approaching hecvy international commitments; during the first
weck of Deccmber the stock market suffered a slump that almost
resulted in a crash due to the announcement of the Government's
special profits tax plan.
(c) Farming Problems.
In his New Year's message, the Finance Minister urged
the sufferers in agricultural communities not to depend entirely
on sympathy and donations, but to exert efforts to help them-
selves.
(e)

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
Potroleum.
The three principal Japanese oil companies (Japan Oil
Company, Mitsubishi Oil Company, and Ogura Oil Company) filed
a petition in December for governmental sanction of an increase
in the price of gasoline of 10 sen a gallon over the current
price of 40 sen. This petition was rejected by the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry, which, however, approved an increase
of 3.5 sen per gallon on December 28, to become effective on
January 1, 1935.

MISCELLANEOUS:
Government Censorship of News Reel Pictures.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER reporte that on December 27 a
Foreign Office official hac informed one of its correspondents
that foreign news reels intended for showing in foreign coun-
tries would probably undergo some form of official censorship
in the near future before these films are allowed to leave
Japan, in order to assure thc presentation of pictures which
will depict "the real and modern Japan". The Foreign Office
official is stated to have made the observation that at present
news recl films being sent out of Japan by such companies as
Fox Movietone News, Hearst Metro-tone News, Paramount Sound
News, Universal News, and Pathé News, are under no form of cen-
sorship, while the films of Japanese cinema companies, design-
d fo howing in Japan, are subjeoted to rgid Government
censorship.

Attention is drawn to the following
items of interest in Tokyo's monthly
political report for January, 1935:
Relations with China (pp. 5 to 8).
Please note particularly the last
paragraph thereof, in regard to the
attitude of Japan toward the extension
by the League of Nations of assistance
to China.
Relations with the Netherlands (p. 10).
The abortive trade conference at
Batavia decided that there should be held
in Japan a conference between Japanese and
Dutch representatives on shipping. You
will note that the latter conference was
abandoned before it was even started, owing
to inability to agree upon the official
language of the conference.
Relations with Siam (pp. 10 and 11).
The political report for November
from Bangkok reported that the Japanese
Minister

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2-
Minister to Siam had been unusually active
and that there had consequently been
observed signs of an enhancement of Japa-
nose prestige in Siam. such as the replace-
ment by Japanese of certain European
advisers to the Siamese Government, the
sending of Siamese students to Japan, and
so on. Further information along these
lines is now furnished by Tokyo.
Relations with Soviet Russia (pp. 12 to 14).
There is nothing that is new in this
section, but I suggest that you may find
it a useful review of recent developments.
Domestic Political Developments:
National Policy Commission (p.16).
It is proposed that there
shall be set up a commission for t he
study of national policies which would
act as an advisory body to the Cabinet,
and to be composed of eminent persons
representative of political, social,
and industrial elements. This would
not be the first body to be set up
of this kind in Japan. In 1917, the
Dinlomatic Advisory Body, organized
substantially along the foregoing
lines, was appointed, but as soon as
liara, one of the great leaders of the
Ceiyuka1

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
Seiyukai, came into power in 1920,
he set about to bring about its
abolition, on the ground that such
a body was repugnant to the principles
of government by political parties.
The reestablishment of this body would
serve to indicate the present low state
to which the political parties have
fallen.
Minseito (p. 18).
Apparently Baron Wakatuski
has resigned from the presidency of
the party, as Mr. Machida, formerly
Minister of Agriculture and Commerce,
has assumed that office.
Economic and Financial Conditions (p. 20).
Your attention is drawn to paragraphs
on Japan's foreign trade and financial
trends, which contain indications that
improyements in Japan's commercial and
rinancial conditions are decelerating.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) The Naval Issue.
There was evidence during the month that the
Japanese press interpreted stetements reported to
have been made by Mr. Noiman II. Davis on January 8
upon his return to the United States from London as
an indicatior that the United States was weakening on
the naval ratio principle:* In the course of an
interview, Mr. Amau, the Foreign Office spokesman,
made the following statement to foreign newspaper
correspondents on January 1l: "It is reported that
Mr. Davis does not stand adamant on the ratio principle
and suggests instead a new naval limitation agreement
based upon the present building programs." Mr. Amau
added, however, that such a proposal constituted a
denial in fact of parity in armaments, and he further
asserted that the desire of the United States to
possess naval armaments nearly twice as great as
those of Japan cculd only be considered as necessitate
by that ccuntry's intention "to extend her aggressive
operation into the Fer East".
Mr. Y. K. Kawakami. Washington correspondent of
the NICHI VICHI SHIMBUN, in an article dated January
16, expressed the opinion that the inited States was
beginning to rocognize that it would be impossible
to preserve the ratio principle.** It is not clear
from the few editorial comments on the naval question
that have appeared recently whether the Japanese
newspapers actually believe that the United States
is relaxing its stand on the ratio question or whether

(d) Case of the KAIUN MARU.
The captain and eight members of the crew of the
KAIUN MARU were committed to public trial in the Tainan
District Court, Formosa, on a charge of illegally
detaining and committing violence against officials
of the Philippine Government in September, 1934, but
the remaining members of the crew were not prosecuted.*
According to a telegram to the Embassy from the
American Vicc Consul at Taihcku, dated January 29,
the Court senteneed the captein to two years imprison-
ment at hard lasor and the chie? en ineer and the
boatswain to one year of imprisonment, while the
remaining six searen were given suspended sentences
of six months each.**

(3) Japan, China, and the League of Nations.
According to the NICHI NICHI SHIIBUN of January
28, Japan is firmly determined to pursue the following
policy should Mr. Haas, the successor of Dr. Rajchman
as assistant technical adviser of the League of Nations.
attempt to extend the Ieegue's assistance to China
without the participation of Japan: (1) If China
desires to enter into cooperation alone with Japan
in a politioal, economic, and military sense, Japan
will cooperate wholeheartedly toward liberating
China from "internationel bondage", particularly if
China should withdraw from the League of Nations;
(2) Japan will maintain an unfriendly attitude
toward any political. economic, or military assist-
ance to be rendered to Chine in the name of the
League of Nctions.

(d) Metherlands.
(1) The Adjourned Batavia Parley.

(2)
) Japanese-Dutch Shipping Conference
Postponed.
WE
A Japanese-Dutch Shipping Conference, designed
primarily to settle the question of dividing freight
retios between the Japaneso and Dutch shipping lines
that conneot Japan and Java, was scheduled to open
in Kobe on January 25. The Embassy is informed
by the Netherland Legation that this Conference
has been postponed owing to the refusal of the
Japanese delegates to accede to the Netherland
request that English be used as the official
language of the Conference and the proceedings
be recorded in English, with Japanese as a secondary
language. The Japanese insisted that their language
be used as the principal language, with the provision
that English might also be employed.

Reported Closer Relations between Japan
and Siam.
The ASAHI of January 5 stated that the year 1935
792.91
was expeoted to witness a marked improvement in the
already friendly relations existing between Japan and
Siam,* citing as evidenoe of this prognostication the
following alleged facts: the present Siamese Minister
in Tokyo, in conformity with the policy of his Govern-
ment, whioh is making special efforts to reform Siamese
politics
* See Monthly Report for September, 1934.

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
-11 -
politics and "to rid the country of Western influence",
has exerted his utmost efforts to cultivate closer
relations with Japan, and Japan is ready to help the

the desired political reform; it has already been
arranged that Siamese naval officers and cadets shall
be trained by means of cruises on Japanese war vessels;
Siam plans to increase the number of Siamese students
in Japan and also to send a group of native dancers
to this country in order to acquaint the Japanese
people with Siamese art; steps are being taken toward
the introduction in Sian of Japanese medical methods;
the Japanese Navy is hastening the completion of its
plan to carry out a long distance airplane flight to
Bangkok; and the Japanese Government is contemplating
the inauguration of an air route which will connect
Tokyc with Bangkok.

(i) Chinese Eastern Railway.

conclusion of the negotiations for the sale of the
Chinese Eastern Rcilway to "Manchukuo" were unfounded*,
nevertheless a substantially complete agreement was
reached on January 22.** The Soviet Embassy confirmed

indicated that although the negctiatcrs (Messrs. Togo
and Kozlovsky) had acted on their own initiative on
certain points, it was expected that the governments
concerned would shortly accept the terms agreed upon.
On January 25 the USSP reportedly sent its acceptance,
a drafting committee to draw up a formal document
was orgenized, and on February 1 this tommittee held
1ts initial meeting. It is widely predicted in Tokyo
that the document will be ready for signature early
in March.
The eagerness of the Foreign Minister to solve
the CER question before the current Diet session
probably accelerated the recent negotiations. After

Diet enhanced.
With
* Despatohes No. 1035, November 1, 1934. and No. 1147,
January 25, 1935.

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.97)
With regard to the recognition of "Manchukuo" the
USSR has thus far avoided any formal act or word which
can be construed as deliberate recognition. If the USSR
should seek to avoid explicit recognition and Japan should
seek to bring it about, the wording of the sales contract
would present difficulties which would delay the work of
the drafting committee. The precise nature of Japan's
guarantee also assumes importance when it is remembered
that originally Japan refused to guarantee "Manchukuo's"
obligations on the grounds that such procedure would be
incompatible with the dignity of a free and independent
state. Japan seems now to have abandoned this thesis and
is about to set a precedent of importance. In view of
her previous stand she is in effect acknowledging through
her guarantee that "Manchukuo's" dignity and hence
sovereignty may not be unlimited.
(2) Stctements of the Foreign Minister.
F4
In his formal address to the Diet on January 22 the
tone of Mr. Hirota's remarks on Soviet-Japanese relations
was more conciliatory than a year ago. He remarked that
OOF
the prime purpose of the sale of the Chinese Ecstern
Railway was to strengthen the friendship between Japan,
"Manohukuo" and the USSR, that the Government was
planning to "accelerate the peaceful development of Soviet-
Japanese relations", and he hoped that the Soviet Govern-
ment would give consideration to the effect in Japan of
Soviet military preparations along the "Manchukuo"
border. On January 25 in reply to an interpellation
Mr. Hirota reaffirmed the above and stated again his
belief that "an amicable settlement of such (Soviet-
Japanese) problems must absolutely precede the conclusion
of

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
of a non-aggression pact". He indicated that the de-
militarization of the "Manchukuo"-Siberian border was
the first of these probiems.
(3)
Buir Nor Inciaen.
During the last week of enuary hostilities broke out
between small detachments of Outer Mongolian forces and
893.01
"Manchukuo"-Japanese troops to the northeast of Lake Buir
on the southwestern border of Hsingan Province. Due to
a shift in the course of the Halha River, the generally
acknowledged boundary, a dispute had arisen over a strip
of land which is apparently of local importance due to
the fishing resources of the rivers tributary to Lake
Buir. Conourrently with the issuance of an official
communique at Hsinking on this and other border incidents,
"Manchukuo"-Japanese troops drove the Outer Mongolians
back across the former course of the river. The Foreign
Office spokesman has stated that the matter concerned
"Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolia only, the War Office has
minimized the incident, and a member of the Soviet Embassy
said that the USSR intended to ignore the matter as not
worth disputing.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a)
Proceedings of the Diet.
The 67th regular session of the Diet, which had been
894.032
adjourned on December 27, was re-opened on January 22.
The salient features of the proceedings up to the close
of the month were the following: Mr. Hirota's speech on
foreign

- 16 -
foreign affairs, partioularly that pert which dealt with
Sino-Japanese reiations; a somewhat irresolute attack by
members of the Seiyukai against the Government for its
failure to provide adequate relief funds during the extra-
ordinary session of the Diut for Tohoku and the typhoon
stricken districts;* censure of the War Minister by a
Minseito (at present pro-Government) member for the
issuance of the Army pamphlet of November 1, 1934; queries
concerning the disproportionate amounts allotted to the
War and Navy Ministries in the 1935-36 budget; and the
statement of the Minister for Foreign Affairs, in reply
to interpollations concerning Japan's future foreign policy
in view of the abrogation of the Washington Naval Treaty,
to the effect that he was confident that there would be
no war involving Japan during his tenure of office.**
(b) The Cabinet.
With further reference to the proposed commission
for the study of national policies which would act as an
advisory body to the Cabinet,**it was reported by the
502
894.

plan, this commission would be composed of representatives
of the three leading political parties (the Seiyukai, the
Minseito, and the Kokumin Domei), the Army, the Navy, and
diplomatic, financial, and educational circles, and that
among those considered as possible candidates were former
Premier Makoto Saito, Baron Tatsuo Yamamoto, Finance
Minister Takahashi, Viscount Kikujiro Ishii (former
Foreign Minister), and Baron Goh (President of the Tokyo
Chamber of Commerce and Industry). The Seiyukai are

The Seiyukei and the Government.
Among the issues separating the Government and the

According to the JAPAN CHRONICLD of January 20,

public and have discussed or made preparations for the
execution of their plens to injurc life, person, or
property, shall be subject to imprisonment for a maximum
term of ten yoars.
(e) The Press.
The plan for the amalgamation of the NIPPON DEMFO
and RENGo news agencies has been altered to include the
Japan Broadcasting Company in order that the latter
company, which has funds of several million yen, may
advanoe enough money to the Government to liquidate both
NIPPON DEMPO and RENGO.*

Labor.

on January 2? at a banquet of the Japan-Latin America
Society of Tokyo, that he had received a request from
the President of Paraguay that Japan send emigrants
to that country and that he had returned with a formal
permit, signed by the Paraguayan Minister for Foreign
Affairs, for the entry of 25C,000 Japanese emigrants
into Paraguay. The Embassy has been unable to obtain
any information on this report other than that which
appeared in the Japanese press.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVINION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
April 19, 1935.
Tokyo's monthly report for February,
loss, may be read in entirety with profit,
bat most of it covers ground which has
already been covered by separate despatches
and olippings.
On February 23 the Ambassador made
imformal representations to the Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs in regard to
the case of three Americans who were roughly
treated by Japanese police in Osaka. The
latest telegram from Tokyo states that
logal action against the Americans on the
grounds of assault against the Koreans,
has been dropped. The Embassy is reluctant
to press the case for fear legal proceed-
ings against the Americans will again be
instituted.
The press in Japan has had much to
say concerning negotiations toward a Sino-
Japanese rapprochement, and in general has
attempted to create the impression that
China, having seen the error of its ways,
felt the need of improving relations with
Japan and of obtaining economic assistance
from Japan.
According

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
According to the press the Foreign
office and the Navy take an optimistic
view of China's motives, while the Army
remains skeptical and is determined not to
mitigate its "vigorous" attitude until
further evidence of China's sincerity is
shown.
Telegrams from the Tokyo Embassy
emphasize the cleavage between the various
factions of the Government with regard to
the Sino-Japanese situation. The Embassy
perceives no indications of any Japanese
plans, other than Mr. Hirota's declared
program of gradually improving the
atmosphere between the two countries. The
Legation at Peiping concurs in this view,
and expresses the belief that the Chinese
can do nothing but reluctantly comply with
Japanese desires, in the absence of help
from other quarters.
There was little press comment in
Japan upon the failure of the Soviet-
American debt negotiations. It may be
safely conjectured that the Japanese feel
no disappointment.
The Navy Minister is reported to have
stated in the Budget Committee of the Diet
that Japan is willing to conclude a new
Pacific defense pact covering Hawaii and
Singapore,

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
Singapore, provided it be entirely separate
from any arms limitation treaty and not
prejudicial to Japan's naval limitation
polioy.
The press reported that the bill
drafted by the Ministry of Justice for the
control of rightist organizations had met
opposition from the Home Office and officials
of the Metropolitan Police Board. The Home
office has long been accused of showing
favor to hoodlums who, in the name of
patriotism, commit outrages against public
order and private rights.
According to the press, the Minister
of Communications stated before the Budget
Committee of the House of Peers that he
intended to ask for a special appropriation
of 200,000,000 yen 1n next year's budget
for the purpose of expanding Japan's com-
mercial air routes over a poriod of fifteen
years.

I.
RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a)
"Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly.
aburo
a
CONFIDETIAL. On February 6 Mr. Kurusu of the
Japanese Foroign Office advised the.representatives of
the Anerican, British, and Netherland oil interests to
EL
discuss the proposed "Manchukuo" oil sales monopoly
purely on a commercial basis and to leave the legal
W
aspects to be resolved by the governments concerned,
since the monopoly would without doubt be established.
Mr. Sansom of the British Embassy gave r. Kurusu the
following reply, which had been prepared by the representa-
tives of the foreign oil companies: that if the Manchurian
authoritics corried out the monopoly plan, the foreign
oil companies would be forced, as a purely commercial
proposition, to dispose of their investments in Manchuria.*
Two weeks later at a meeting held between the Japanese
Petroleum Adviser to the "Manchukuo" Government and the
Vice Iinister for Finance cf'Manchukuo" (also a Japanese)
on the one hand and the represontatives of the Standard-
Vacuum Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
on the other, the Japanese made the proposal, which they
termed an offer of "special consideration", that the
monopoly would buy annually during the ncxt three to five
years a limited quantity of gasoline and kerosene from
the Standard-Vacuum, the Rising Sun, and the Toxas com-
panies.** (The Texas Company has not sent a represcntative
to Japan for the conversations.) The quantity of gasoline
and kerosone to be bought annually, according to the
monopoly's proposition, amounted only to 20% of the
three companies' total sales in Manchuria curing the year
l934. The reply of the foreign oil companies was to the
effect that the Japanese offer was not a sound business
proposal
Embassy's telegram No. 27 of February 8, 1935
**
Eabassy's telegram No. 36 of Fehruary 21, 1935

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
conversations be suspended for a few weeks, principally
because of the danger of internal political embarrassment
should any action be taken before the close of the Diet
session toward altering the oil regulations.* The
representatives of the oil companies acceded to this
request, realizing that the negotiations were not progressing
successfully and believing that no concessions would be
made by the Japanese as long es the Diet was in seosion.
Accordingly, they arranged to leave Japan on March 2 and
to return on April 1 if the Japanese officials should
still be desirous at the latter date of continuing the
conversations.
(c)
Discrimination in the Application of the
"Manchukuoan" Customs Tariff against American
and British erosene in Favor of Japanese
Light Oil.
Under the authorization contained in the Department's
NE
telegram No. 20 of February 15, the Embassy joined with
the British Eabassy on February 19 in making informal,
oral representations to the Japanese Government on the
discriminatory duties levied by the customs of "Manchukuo"
on American and British kerosene as opposed to Japanese
"light oil".** In making these representations, Mr.
Dickover of this Eibassy and r. Sansom of the British
Embassy invited Mr. urusu's attention to the fact that
on December 19 the American and British Consuls General
at Mukden had made informal representations to the
"Manchukuoan" authorities on the same subject.
representatives of the two Embassies further stated that
the "Manchukuoan" authorities had failed to eliminatc
