(a)
Mistreatment of Three American Business Men
in Osaka by the Japanese Police.
On February 14 Mr. R. S. K. Irvin, an American citizen
and managing director of the Corn Products Refining Corpora-
tion, Osaka, who had been subject since August, 1934, to
an extortionist claim by a Korean named Kang Ki Ban, was
attacked in his office by this man and a Korean associate.
The Koreans were held off by Mr. Irvin with the assistance
of Messrs. J. W. Scott and W. G. Palmroth, American citizens
in the same office, and the police were called immediately.
In January, 1935, Irvin had requested protection from the
Osaka police against the Korean and at that time the police
had asked Irvin to advise them in the event that Kang caused
any trouble. In spite of the facts last mentioned, upon
their arrival at Irvin's office on February 14 the police,
seeing a melée in progress between the Korcans and the
Americans, proceeded to bind the wrists of all three
Americans. This was done by the police without any enquiry.
The Americans were ticd to the Koreans and humiliatingly
forced to march through the streets to the police station,
where they were held far about four hours. Only after
signing a statement that he would be responsible for their
future appearance was the American Consul at Kobe able to
obtain the release of Irvin, Scott, and Palmrcth.** Several
days later the spokesman of the Foreign Cffice, in reply
to questions from newspaper corrcspondents, stated that
the Americans had first attacked the Koreans, had attempted
to resist the police, and had been guilty of criminal
assault.
Embassy's despatch No. 1176 of February 20, 1935
**
Embassy's telegrams No. 32. Februcry 18, and No. 35,
February 21, 1935.

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
assault. These statements omitted several important facts
and differed decidedly from the sworn statements made to
the Consul at Kobe by the three Americans concerned.* In
accordance with the Embassy's recommendations and under
authority of the Department's telegram No. 22 of February 21,
the Ambassador made informal and friendly representations
to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs on February 23,
outlining the facts of the case as contained in the state-
ments and affidavits received by the Embassy, pointing out
the discrepancies and important omissions in the remarks
made by the Foreign Office spokesman to newspaper corres-
pondents, and appealing to the Vice Minister's cooperation
in securing an impartial investigation which would not be
based exclusively on the statements of the Osake police.
Mr. Shigemitsu gave the Ambassador assurances that the
investigaticn would be eminently fair and appealed for the
Ambassador's cooperation in trying to calm the American
press.**

(2)
Buir Nor Incident.
This incident has not been formally settled yet owing
to a dispute over the venue of the proposed conference
between "Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolian officials. The
persistent efforts of the "Manchukuo" side to dignify this
issue with a formal conference suggests that the Japanese
may be seeking an entering wedge for freer access to Outer
Mongolia as well as a general understanding with regard to
the "Manchukuo"-Outer Mongolian border.
(3)
Fisheries.
At the annual auction of fishing grounds in Northern
waters which was held at Vladivostok on February 27, 73
lots were offered of which Japanese interests obtained 30,
and Soviet interests 37, while 6 lots were not leased.
The
exchange rate for the Japnese bids was 32.5 sen per ruble.
As renorted previously* the Japanese Government has
been considering meesures in anticipation of the negotia-
tions for the revision of the Russo-Jepanese Fisnery
Convention of 1928 which commence in lay. Its first step
was to force most of the northorn fishing intcrests into
an amalgamation with
 the Nichiro Fishery Conpany, its
second, to introduce a "Law for the Control of Fishery in
Northern Waters" .t the present Diet session.
The proposed law not only gives the Government wide,
if general, powers to control the industry but creates
semi-monopolistic

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The C. binet
On February 22 the Cabinet decided to placc the

former Ambassador to Germany; Kyujiro vi. forer

(b) The Army.
Another Army pamphlet directed to the people at
large, similar to the one issued on Hovember 1, 1934,*
was published by the Press Section of the War Department
on February 25 and distributed to schools, young men's
associations, ex-service men's associations, and various
other organizations throughout the country.
This pamphlet
is entitled "Determination of the Japanese Nation in a
Time of Crisis" und was issued on the occasion of the
thirtieth anniversary of the Battle of M kden in the
Russo-Japanese ar. The following are sone of the principal
ideas expressed in the pamphlet: the crisis through which
Japan must pass exists in reality and is not a figment of
the imagination; modern civilization, which is based on
individualism, must be liquidated; the domestic crisis is
the result of intoxicetion with Western civilization,
degeneration of the national spirit, pressure on the
livelihood of the masses, and social insecurity; Japan's
crisis in relation to foreign nations is caused by pressure
that is being applied to the Japanese economic advance and
by
* Embassy's despatch No. 1031, November 1, 1934.

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.98)
-14 -
by the suspicion with which Japan's policies are being
viewed; Japan is one of those nations that reject imperial-
ism, and it proposes to transform the world into a paradise
wherein all the nations shall dwell on an equal footing,
guided by the principle of mutuol support and mutual
prosperity; the cooperation between Japan and "IManchukuo"
marks an initial move toward the above-mentioned world
mission of Japan; in the past the Fowers have with egotism
endeavored to make the Far East dependent on then, but what
they want of this part of the world is radically opposed
to what Japan desires; the Japanese nation retains the
natural and unspoiled virtues of the human being as
against the moral values of other nations, which are
characterized by individualism, egotism, and natcrialism;
and the Japanese sense of "self" is not the sanc as that
of the "ego" but is insepurable fron the State, and from
this conception spring filial piety and loyalty to the
Emperor.
In this connection, it may be of interest to note
the Army's reported attitude toward a book written by Dr.
Tatsukichi  inobe entitled "A Treatise on the Constitution",
which was defended by him before the House of Peers on
E
February 25 in reply to the contention of Baron Takeo
Kikuchi that the book improperly attempts to define the
Imperial rights and powers. According to the NICHI NICHI
of February 27, the Army's objections to and corments on
Dr. Minobe's book can be summarized as follows:
the
fundamental basis of Dr. Minobe's theory of the State is
individualism, which suggests the conclusion thet sovereign
rights rest with the people; the national structure of
Japan differs from that of European and American countries,
the

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.98)
-15-
the Japanese State being an extension of the family, and
therefore the question of relationship of rights and
obligations between the Sovereign and the people is non-
existent; the Diet is an organ to assist the Emperor, not
to coordinate or contend with him; and it would be dangerous
to the Nation to conduct Stat- uffairs on such a conception
of the State's structure as that set forth in Dr. Minobe's
book.

RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a)
Adjournment of the Arizona State Legislature
without the Passage of the Bill concerning
Aliens Ineligible to Citizenship.
A matter which had caused a certain amount of
unfavorable comment in Japan was closed on March 2l when
the Arizona State Legislature adjourned without the passage
of the bill providing that aliens ineligible to citizenship
be prohibited from preparing or cultivating agricultural
land.*
(b) "Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly.
In answer to the proposal presented to M. Sansom of
EA
the British Embassy by Mr. Kurusu on February 28 to the
effect that the "anchukuo" oil monopoly would annually
buy a limited amount of gasoline and kerosene from the
foreign oil companies, the special representatives of the
companies informed r. Kurusu on March 1 that they saw
little prospect of being able to depart from their principle
of not supplying monopolies and that they would prefer to
postpone any further discussion of the Manchurian oil
question until their return to Japan.** On the following
day the special representatives of the foreign oil companies
left Japan with the intention of returning on April 1.
The Government of "Manchukuo" issued an official
announcement on March 20 that the oil monopoly would go
into effect on Anril 10.***
During the month the A erican Enbessy did not receive
a reply from the Japanese Foreign Office to its represen-
tations of December 1, 1934.

Reports of negotiations toward a Sino-Japanese
rapprochement continued to occupy the front pages of the
press but through lack of factual evidence nothing tangible
can be reported as to the progress of such negotiations
and nothing can be predicted concerning heir eventual
outcome.** The Army authorities continued to question
Chinose sincerity, doubting China's alleged change of
attitude away from anti-Japanese agitation and boycott.***
This attitude of the Jepanese military may be an indication
that they are opposed to a peaceful, diplomatic rarprochement
between the two countries for reasons of their own.****
It is reported, for example, that certnin elements of
the military were displeased over the amicable settlement
of the Chinese Eastern Railway ccntroversy between Japan
and Soviet Russia, since the sale of the railway took

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.98)

(r)
Netherlands.
The proposal to hold a Japanese-Netherland shipping
conference at Kobe * came to naught with the departure
WE
from Japan on March 3 of the Netherland delegates, who
refused to give in to the demand of the Japanese delegates
that their language be used as the official language of the
conference in place of English. In accordance with their
threat, and immediately after the departure of the Nether-
land delegates, the Japanese shipping companies abrogated
the Java-Japan Freight Conference, which had been in effect
since July, 1933. It was predicted by the press that as a
result of these developments keen competition between the
Japanese and Netherland steamship lines would take place.
In fact, the four Japanese companies concerned announced
that they would form a freight pool among themselves and
one company reduced its freight rates by 50% on March 13.

scheduled to leeve for Brazil on April 8 is unofficial
in character and that its purpose will bc to promote trede
between the two countries. **
The Jupanese Foreign Office
on March 10 officially denied the report that
the Federal
Trade Council of Brazil had announced its apprcval of a
$100,000,000 loan from Japan for the reorganization of
Brazil's merchant marine and Navy and for the development
of its metallurgical industry. The Brazilian Embassy at
Tokyo placed no credence whatsoever in this rumor. **

(h)
Japan's Withdrawal from the League of Nations.
Japan's withdrawal from the League of Nations became
effective on March 27. Japanese newspaper comment on
this matter, of which there was remarkably little, wes
far calmer and much more self-assured in tone than the
voluminous press comment that appeared two years ago when
Japan decided to give notice of its resignation from the
League.* This fact can be explained by the events and
developments of the last two years. **

) The Naval Question.
On March 1 the Foreign Office and the Navy Ministry
categorically denied the report that Admiral Yamamoto had
WE
suggested to the Japanese Government that it propose to
the United States and Great Britain a naval building
holiday of three years' duration commencing January 1,
1936.***

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 6, 1935.
There is little in the Eabassy's
politieal report for April that has not
been cóvered by separate reports and
telegrams. I suggest that this report be
read only if an outline of ovents of that
period is desired.
It seems to me that some change is
necessary in the manner of preparing this
monthly report. At present the report is
simply a summary of the various despatches,
telegrams and reports which have been
prepared by the Embassy and, although it
is undoubtedly useful for the information
of certain recipients, is of practically
no value to this Division. If this report
1s primarily for the information of other
Divisions of the Department and of the
several missions and consulates to which
it is sent, the present form is probably
satisfactory. However, if it is primarily
designed for the information of this
Division I think a change is indicated.

I.
RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a)
Visit to Japan of Admiral Upham in the
U. S. S. AUGUSTA from May 3-17.
Admiral Frank Brooks Upham, Commander-in-Chief of
the United States Asiatic Fleet, paid an official visit
of two weeks to Japan, arriving at Yokohama on May 3 on
board his flagship, the U. S. S. AUGUSTA. A number of
entertainments were given in the Admiral's honor, including
an fficial luncheon at the American Embassy, a dinner
at the official residence of the Navy Minister, and a
buffet supper at the Anerica-Japan Society. Admiral
Upham was presented to the Emperor by the A bassador on
May 9. The U. S. S. AUGUSTA sailed on May 17 for Kobe
to join the rest of the Fleet consisting of ten destroyers
and the destroyer tender BLACK HAWK. In sumnary, the visit
of Admiral Upham at Yokohama was a successful one and
passed off in a spirit of friendliness.*
(b)
Activities in Japan of Benigm Ramos,
Filipino Sakdalista Leader, and of a Japanese
Army Officer.
Benigno Ranos, soi-disant leader of the Filipino
Sakdalista political party, was in Japan on May 1 when
the Sakdalistas attempted an unsuccessful revolt against
the Government of the Philippine Islands and was endeavor-
ing at the time to obtain support in Japan for his party's
cause, and presunaoly a supply of arms. Ramos probably
thought he could obtain the desired assistance through
the Japanese groups which advocate the Pan-Asia movement,
but as far as the Embassy is awarc he was unable to
obtain any concrete aid. While in Japan, Ramos distributed
articles, which had been translated into Japanese, in-
veighing against the exploitation of the Philippine
Islands by the white race and implying that there would
not
Embassy's despatch No. 1308, May 17, 1935.

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
not be the same objection against
exploitation by a
race other than the white. In one of his articles the
Sakdalista stated that any sacrifice which the Japanese
people might make in extending help to his cause would
not be made in vain.
It was revealed during the month that one Hiroshi
Tamura, a Major of Artillery attached to the staff head-
quarters of the Japanese Army in Formosa, had been writing
letters to well-known Filipinos in which he attacked
Quezon as a traitor opposed to the independence of the
Islands and assured the Filipinos of the sympathy of the
Japanese people.*
(c)
Arrest and Detention of Williams S. Gates,
U. S. N., Retired, in Taiwan on a Charge of
Espionage and Violation of the Navigation Laws.
On April 29 William S. Gates, U. S. N. Retired, was
arrested with two other foreigners at Taito, Taiwan, while
cruising along the coast in a sailing vessel.** The
American Consul at Taiwan was at first refused permission
to talk with Gates by telephone but later he obtained
permission from the Japanese authorities to interview
the arrested American. Gates was held in detention for
twelve days, examined by the Procurator, and on May ll
was fined ¥2o0 for violation of the navigation laws and
set free, the espionage charge having been apparently
dropped.***
(a) Activities of American Pacifists.
An open letter, signed by 30l religious and "other"
leaders in the United States, including Dr. Harry Emerson
Fosdick, the Reverend John Haynes Holmes, and Mr. Sherwood
Eddy, protesting against and apologizing for the American
naval
Embassy's despatch No. 1395, May 13, 1935.
** Embassy's telegram No. 95, May 2, 1935.
***Embassy's telegram No. 104, May 12, 1935.

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
naval maneuvers in the north Pacific, was sent to the
Japan Christian Association and published in the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of May 3.* This letter was less general in
scope than the message to the United States from American
missionaries in Japan which had been published by the
JAPAN ADVERTISER on April 21, and it placed special
emphasis on the American naval maneuvers, stating that
they were "highly unimaginative and mistaken" and had been
oppoped by many thousands of American citizens, particularly
members of churches and synagogues.
(e)
"Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly.
On May 9 the American, British, and Netherland oil
companies presented to the Finance Department of "Manchukuo"
declarations of the values of their businesses in Manchuria,
excluding stocks of petroleum products, with the request
that the companies be compensated for the full value of
business lost through the operation of the "Manchukuo"
Oil Monopoly.** No answer to the companies' request for
reimbursement was received from the "Manchukuo" Government
during the month of May. Several erroneous stories were
published by Japanese news agencies, the general tenor of
which was that the foreign oil companies would continue to
operate in "Manchukuo" and to sell oil to the authorities
of "Manchukuo" as they had done in the past. With a
view to counteracting this Japanese propaganda, on May 30
the foreign oil companies issued to foreign press corres-
pondents in Tokyo a statement to the effect that the
recent stories in the Japanese press were misleading and
that the foreign companies had no alternative but to with-
draw from "Manchukuo" and the Kwantung Railway Zone since
the
*
Embassy's telegram No. 97, May 3, 1935.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1303, May 15, 1935.

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the "Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly had deprived them of their
sales rights in these territories.
The "Manchukuo" Monopoly authorities invited
representatives of the foreign oil companies to talk over
with them the question of the withdrawing of the companies'
stocks from Manchuria and accordingly representatives of
the Standard Vacuum Oil Company and the Asiatic Petroleum
Company conferred with Mr. Hoshino,of the Finance Office
of "Manchukuo", on May 2l. Mr. Hoshino informed these
representatives that the Monopoly would permit the foreign
companies to sell their stocks of duty-paid petroleum
products either through Monopoly sales agents or through
other channels but that their bonded stocks could not be
sold except to the Monopoly. Acting on instructions
received from their head offices, the Mukden representatives
of the oil companies on May 27 advised the Monopoly that
its proposal was unacceptable and that the companies must
hold the Monopoly responsible for the losses incurred by
the re-exportation of their stocks unless a satisfactory
sale of the stocks en bloc were effected.*
(f)
Activities of George Bronson Rea.
At a luncheon of the Tokyo Pan-Pacific Club on May 31
Mr. George Bronson Rea spoke on the subject of his work
in the United States as the envoy of "Manchukuo".
In the
course of his speech, he stated that the attitude of the
United States toward "Manchukuo" was purely legalistic
and based on the American interprctation of the Nine
Power Treaty and the peace pacts, which the American
Government insisted had been broken; that he had found
it necessary to state the case for "Manchukuo" also from
a legalistic point of view and to show that the treaties
had
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1330, May 31, 1935.

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
had not been broken; that Japan had not violated the
terms of the Kellogg Pact because that country had the
right to define the time, place, and opportunity of
resorting to self-defense in Manchuria; that Japan had
adhered faithfully to the spirit of the Nine Power Treaty,
having reduced its armies to 230,000 men, "the minimum
for defense"; and that he had reason to believe that the
people of the United States had now a better appreciation
of Japan's viewpoint.
GDA
II.
RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a)
China.
During the early part of the month there was a
perceptible lessening of public interest in the Sino-
Japanese situation and particularly in the reported
negotiations for a Sino-Japanese repprochement.* However,
during the latter half of the month Sino-Japanese relations
came again to the front as a result of two developments,
the announcement on May 17 of the elevation of the Japanese
Legation in China to the status of an Embassy and the
delivery by the Japanese garrison at Tientsin on May 29
of certain deinands to the Chinese authorities, which were
understood to be the dismissal of General Yu Hsueh-chung,
Chairnan of the Hopei Provincial Government, withdrawal
of his and other nationalist troops from the Tientsin-
Peipinz area, the suppression of anti-Japanese activities,
and possibly the inclusion of Tientsin and Peiping in an
enlarged demilitarized zone.**
The Japanese press was

Fisheries.
During Ma action on the part of the Japanese Govern-

confine
*
Eibassy's despatch No. 1318, May 29, 1935.

confine the discussions to such items as they wished

concluded. The concensus of Japanese opinion seens to
be that the development of the fisheries situation in
recent years has been relatively more favorable to the
USSR than to Japan; that Japanese interests are endangered;
and that so far the Russians are lacking in "sincerity"
in their attitude to the problem.

(c)
Noval Matters.
There was no change during the month in Japan's
attitude toward the holding of a Naval Conference in 1935
and naval matters in general, except that a spokesman of
the Navy Department was reported to have stated that Japan
was not necessarily opposed to the British plan to invite
Germany and the Soviet Union to the Naval Disarmament
Conference. Formerly the Navy Department was reported
had
to haveobjcctions to the inclusion of those two countries
in the Conference. According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of
May 28, the Navy Department spokesman, while expressing
the view that Japan perceived no objection to the parti-
cipation by Germany and the Soviet Union in the Conference.
also stated that the Japanese Navy was strongly opposed
to a postponcment of the parley until the year 1936. In
an address delivered on May 7 at the annual conference
of prefectural Governors in Tokyo, the Navy Minister
re-affirmed Jaçan's stand on the naval question, stating
that Jan
ust increase its national strength still
further in order to assure its national cefonse in the
face of recent international developments; that the large
naval appropriations included in the present fiscal year's
budget were absolutely necessary; that the Vashington
Naval Troity had become "a menace to Japen's national
defense security" because of developments that had taker
place since the Tre t: was entered into; that he was
pessimistic

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
-11 -
pessimistic concerning the prospects for naval limitation
at present; and that the Gcvernors should help in
spreading "naval ideas" among the populace. In a press
interview at Osaka on May 23 the Vice Minister for the
Navy also stated that he was not optimistic on the
question of naval limitation since the trend in Europe
was toward armament expansion and not toward disarmament.
With the apparent desire of keeping the idea of the
1935-36 crisis year to the fore, Vice Admiral Takahashi,
Commander-in-Chief of the Combined Fleet, made a speech
on May 27 in which he said that the possible crisis this
year or next year would have its origin in the Washington
and London Nival Troties. I.: this speech, which was
delivered before the Naval Defense Society of Kcio
University, Vice Adniral Takahashi made one of the
frankest exposés that have come to the Embassy's attention
concerning the reasons for Japan's fixed determination
to have naval parity and ample navel forces. He stated
that at the time of the conclusion of the London Naval
Treaty the Japanese believed that their interests would
be completely protected by treaties; that subsequently
Japan's interests were infringed upon by the Chinese;
that the Manchurian incident was the turning point in
Japan's international relations; that during thc Manchurian
incident the Japanese Army was able to check any inter-
vention by a third country and that the Japanese Navy
contributed much to this successful result; that Japan
would remain adamant regarding the abolition of the
ratio system; that European countries would maintain
the peace and order of Europe, American countries would
do

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
do likewise on the American continent, and Far Eastern
countries would do the same in the Far East; and that
Japan, China, and "Manchukuo" were responsible for peace
end order in the Far East. Thus, the Admiral tacitly
admitted one of the two main reasons for the Japanese
demand for abolition of the ratio system,* namely, the
necessity of having a Navy sufficiently strong to enable
Japan to wield a dominant influence in "East Asia" to
the exclusion of the Occidental Powers. He touched on
Japan's other reason for being so strongly opposed to
the Washington and London Naval Treaties when he said
thet the existing ratio system was quite unreasonable
and that Japan wanted to fix a comnon naval strength
among the five naval powers.

contended that the Japanese computation was misleading

silk exports to Canada through the United States.

Tariff Investigation Comnittee was seriously considering
an increase in the duties on Canadian products, they did

crop could at present menace Anerican cotton exports to
Japan, it is understood that the Japanese Foonomic

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Auerican Oil Interests in Japan.
Although there were no new developments during the
month in the situation created by the application of the
894.6363
Petroleum Industry Law to Azerican end other foreign oil
interests in Japan, it appeared that the Japanese officials
concerned, particularly Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Commeroial
Bureau of the Foreign Office, were working slowly toward
a solution of the problems of stock-keeping and prices.
The foremost obstacles standing in the way of a settlement
of the matter were the desire of the Army to have large
stocks of petroleum and petroleum products in Japan and
the necessity of avoiding action detrimental to the
reputation of the Minister for Commerce and Industry, who
sponsored the Petroleum Industry Law.*
In compliance with the Department's telegraphic
instruction No. 110 of July 17, the A :bassador took
advantage of his farewell call on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs on July 18 to bring up casually and informally the
question of the present status of the American petroleum
interests in Japan and to ask the Minister's opinion
concerning a favorable settlement of the question. Mr.
Hirota was noncommittal in his reply, stating that he
hoped that a favorable settlement could be reached in due
course but not expressing either optimism or pessimism
in this regard.
(b) "Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly.
The only important development during the month in
893.6363
regard to the "Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly and the American
Maucku
oil interests in Manchuria was the decision of the Standard-
Vacuum
*
Embassy's despatches No. 1385
of July 10, 1935, and
No. 1408, of July 25, 1935.

made in June by Katakura & Company, Limited, to act as
the agents of the company in Manchuria.*
(o) Departure of the Ambassador for the United
States on Home Leave.
5841
The Honorable Joseph C. Grew, accompanied by Mrs.
123
Grew, departed for the United States on home leave on
July 19.**
On the occasion of a farewell luncheon given on July 8
by the America-Japan Society in honor of the Ambassador
and Mrs. Grew, the former delivered an address which
contained the following statement: "I go away in an
optimistic frame of mind concerning the future of Japanese-
American relations because my experience here has convinced
me that the influences tending to maintain and to build up,
not only sentimentally but practically, the friendship
between cur two countries are strong enough and determined
enough to overcome all adverse factors." ***
In calling on the Minister for Foreign Affairs and
on Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the
Foreign Office, to say good-byc, the Ambassador had con-
versations with these officials concerning American-
Japanese trade relations.****

The 50th anniversary of General Grant's death was

July 23 under the joint auspices of the City of Tokyo and
the America-Japan Society. On this occasion Mr. Neville,
the Chargé d'Affaires, delivered an address to honor the
memory of General Grant, who visited Japan in 1879.*****

(e)
Japanese Press Reaction to Reported American
Naval Policy.
The Tokyo ASAHI and the Osaka ASAHI of July 16
attacked what these newspapers termed the desire of the
United States to dominate the Pacific Ocean, the latter
journal stating that Japan must prepare against the
"trans-oceanic strategy" of the United States "which
challenges Japan". On the other hand, it was reported
by the NICHI NICHI of July 3O that the Japanese Navy was
neither surprised nor worried over the news that the
United States was preparing to fortify the Aleutian
Islands, Guam, and other Pacific possessions.
(f)
Japanese Comment on the Proposed American
Processing Tax on Raw Silk and Rayon.
Contrary to reports in the Japanese press, no protest
was made by the Foreign Office against the amendment to
the Agricultural Adjustment Act before the United States
Senate providing for a processing tax on raw silk and
rayon. In a talk with the Ambassador on July 18 Mr.
Kurusu, Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign
Office, referred casually to the proposed amendment
and remarked that if it should pass it would be a serious
blow to American-Japanese trade relations and would
adversely affect about 2,000,000 sericultural homes in
Japan.* In commenting upon the withdrawal on July 18 by
Senator Byrnes of his projected amendnent to the Agri-
cultural Adjustment Act, the Tokyo NICHI NICHI of July 21
stated that measures in the United States against silk
would force Japan to retaliate by checking the importation
of American cotton into Japan. The Foreign Office spokes-
man
of
* Embassy's telegram No. 155. July 19, 1935.

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
man was reported by the JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 80 to
have expressed gratification over the withdrawal of the
amendment, adding that its adoption would have created
a serious question between Japan and the United States.

II.
RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a)
China.
790.94
During the early part of the month the incident that
received the greatest amount of attention in the Japanese
press was the "lèse majesté "affair that arose during the
latter part of May over an article published in the Chinese
weekly, HSIN SHENG (NEW LIFE), which contained remarks
considered by the Japanese as disrespectful to their
Emperor. The Japanese Ambassador to China submitted
certain demands to the Chinese authorities, including a
formal apology in the name of the National Government and
the Kuomintang, punishment of the editor of the offending
magazine and the writer of the article, and a guarantee
that similar incidents would be prevented in the future.
It is interesting to note that Mr. Hirota stated at a
Cabinet meeting on July 5 that the lèse majesté case was
purely a matter for diplomatic parley and that the Japanese
Army was not concerned in the affair. However, a Rengo
despatch from Shanghai dated July 3 reported that vigorous
representations with regard to the HSIN SHENG article had
been made by the Military and Naval Attachés of the
Japanese Embassy in China. The affair was apparently
settled to the satisfaction of the Japanese on July 8
upon the complete compliance of the Chinese authorities
with the demands. Two other incidents reported in the
Japanese

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Japanese press were an attack on July 3 on a "Manchukuo"
border patrol by Chinese troops formerly under the command
of General Sung Che-yuang and the raiding of a Japanese
motor-cycle supply store by Chinese "patriots" during
the early part of the month. It would appear that both
incidents were settled to the satisfaction of the Japanese.
There were reports to the effect that the Japanese
Foreign Office was formulating a "new" policy toward
China, which would include the following points: China
must not only bring about a complete change in its anti-
Japanese policy but also must cooperate in a positive
manner with Japan; Chinese policy toward "Manchukuo" must
undergo a fundamental change (presumably recognition of
the new State*); and the Nanking Government must set
North China aside as a region entircly different from the
rest of China economically, socially, and culturally. As
as example of the proposed Sino-Japanese cooperation, the
ASAHI of July l4 reported that it had been learned from
responsible Japanose Government quarters that Japanese
military and economic participation in the Nanking Govern-
ment's campain against the communists would be granted,
provided Ciina was really desirous of such aid and was
prcpared to receive it with the "utmost sincerity".
General Tsiang Tso-ping, Chinese Ambassador to Japan,
sailed for China on leave on July 5, giving rise to rumors,
which were later denied by him, that he was going to resign
from his pcst in Japan. Interviewed prior to his departure
by the YOMIURI, General Tsiang Tso-ping was reported to
have cxpressed his belief that Sino-Japanese relations
were
* Department's telegram No. 115 cf July 29, 1935, and
Embassy's telegram No. 16l of July 31, 1935.

=== Page 476 === (confidence 0.99)
were gradually working back to normal and that recent
events in North China were only a "passing shower".
The Chinese Ambassador was also said to have made the
remark that Japanese newspapers and magazines were to
some extent responsible for exaggerating Sino-Japanese
disputes by giving prominence to small matters.
Although most reports were to the effect that at
the end of the month the Japanese Army was for the time
being satisfied with the situation in North China, a
Nippon Dempo despatch from Peiping dated July 27 reported
that the Japanese military authorities in North China
had abandoned their efforts to persuade Marshal Yen
Hsi-shan, "model governor" of Shansi Province, to
organize a government consolidating the Provinces of
Hopei, Chahar, Suiyan, Shantung, and Shansi outside the
influence of the Nanking Government. The news despatch
further stated that the Japanese military had now decided
to place a civilian at the head of the proposed North
China bloc.
(b) "Manchukuo".
(1)
Agreement for the Establishment of the
Japan-"Manchukuo" Joint Economic Commission.
893.50 ma.l.
On July 15 an agreement for the establishment of the
Japan-"Manchukuo" Joint Economic Commission was signed at
Hsinking by General Jiro Minami, Japanese Ambassador to
"Manchukuo", and Mr. Chang Yen-ching, the Minister for
Foreign Affairs of "Manchukuo". In the preamble of the
agreement it is stated that the Governments of Japan and
"Manchukuo" entertain a desire to realize the rational
co-ordination of the coonomics of the two countries with
the

=== Page 477 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
with the purpose of permanently consolidating the
relationship of economic interdependence now existing
between them. The agreement proper provides that the
seat of the Commission shall be at Hsinking; that the
Commission may be consulted by the Governments of Japan
and "Manchukuo" concerning important matters regarding
the inter-relation of the economics of the two countries
as well as important matters relating to the supervision
of the special companies set up as joint Japanese-"Manchu-
kuoan" enterprises; that the Governments of Japan and
"Manchukuo" shall take no measures regarding such matters
unless they have previously consulted the Commission and
obtained its views; and that the Commission may, when
necessary, make recommendations to the Governments of
Japan and "Manchukuo" in regard to all matters pertaining
to the rational co-ordination of the economics of the
two countries. In the annex of the agreement it is
provided that the Commission shall be composed of four
Japanese and four "Manchukuoan" members appointed by the
respective Governments; that the President of the Commission
shall be elected by the members from among their own
number; and that the proceedings of the body shall be
decided by a majority vote, the President deciding an
issue in the event of an equal number of votes.
In commenting on the reasons motivating the con-
clusion of the agreement, thc spokesman of the Japanese
Foreign Office on July 15 made reference to "Manchukuo"
as an independent State, to the "inseparable and inter-
depcndent", the "intimate and unique" relationship between
the two countries, and also to "thc precedents in practice
among the Powers".
(2)

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
(2) Proposed Abolition of Extraterritoriality.
Manbuna
At a press conference on July 29 the spokesman of
the Japanese Foreign Office asserted that the question
793.943
of the abolition of extraterritorial rights in "Manchukuo"
for foreigners other than Japanese did not exist, because
such foreigners had no such rights there for the reason
that the countries of which they are citizens or subjects
had not recognized the independence of "Manchukuo". With
reference to the extraterritoriality rights enjoyed by
certain foreigners in "Manchukuo" under treaties between
China and foreign powers, which treaties "Manchukuc"
proaised to observe in the proclamation issued at the
time of its establishment, the Foreign Office spokesman
stated that the powers had elected to recognize only
that part of the proclamation concerning the open door
and had ignored its main feature, which was the declara-
tion of the independence of "Manchukuo". Mr. Amau
reiterated that he had made this point clear at the time
of the Aierican and British protests against the establish-
ment of the Oil Monopoly in "Manchukuo". His remarks
implied that if the powers should protest against the
abolition of extraterritoriality in "Manchukuo", Japan
would advance the same argument, namely, that such
countries could not recognize certain parts of the pro-
clamation without recognizing what the Japanese considered
its principal point, the declaration of the independence
of "Menchukuo".
(3) Japanese Emigration to "Manchukuo".
According to the JAPAN TIMES of July 29, the
Ministry

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
7
- 9 -
Ministry of Overseas Affairs has under consideration the
organization of a "Manchukuo" Colonization Compary with
a capital of ¥15,000,000, having given up its original
plan for the establishment of a company with a capital of
¥50,000,000, since such a large capitalization had been
found impossible under existing conditions.
(4)
The Resignation of Count Hakutaro Hayashi
as Governor of the South Manchuria Railway
Company and the Cabinet's Decision to
Recommend the Appointment of Mr. Yosuke
Matsuoka in His Place.
The press of August 1 reported that Count Hakutaro
Hayashi, Governor of the South Manchuria Railway Company,
had on July 3l expressed a desire to resign and that the
Cabinet had decided to recommend as his successor Mr.
Yosuke Matsuoka, former Vice President of the South
Manchuria Railway Company and Japan's chief delegate to
the League of Nations when the Lytton Report was considered.
The YOMIURI of July 31 quoted Count Hayashi as having said
to the Premier that he wanted to retire in favor of a
younger man because of the new situation in North China,
in which the South Manchuria Company was expected to
play an important rôle.
(c) Canada.
An Imperial Ordinance was promulgated on July 19
under the Trade Adjustment and Protection Law imposing
50% additional ad valorem duties on wheat, wheat flour,
wheat starch, wheat gluten, pulp for the manufacture of
paper, boxing and match paper, and a number of varieties
of cut, sawn, or split lumber, when imported from countries
whose exports to Japan in 1934 excceded imports fron
Japan and which imposc special and arbitrary duties upon
Japanese

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
Japanese goods. By a declaration of the Minister for
Finance, the Imperial Ordinance was made applicable
from July 20 to imports from Canada.*
A Rengo despetch from Ottawa dated July 25 reported
that from August 5 the Canadian Government would impose
an additional 33-1/3% ad valorem duty on all imports
from Japan.
(a)
Great Britain.
On July 18 the Egyptian Government notified the
684.9431
Japanese Government of abrogation of thc provisional
trade convention bctween Egypt and Japan which providcs
for most favored nation treatment, the abrogation to take
effect on October 18 of this year in accordance with a
provision of the convention. The action of thc Egyptian
Government was believed to have been taken under British
pressure by the JIJI of July 2O, the NICHI NICHI of July
20, and the MIYAKO of July 22, these newspapers stating
that great indignation had been aroused in Japan by the
abrupt action of the Egyptian Govcrnment. According
to the Tokyo ASAHI of July 25 the Japanese Foreign Office
instructed the Japanese Consul General at Alexandria to
convey the Japanese official point of view to the Egyptian
Government, namely, that contrary to the Egyptian con-
tention the balance of trade is at present favorable to
Egypt and that the Japanese Government id displeased with
the action taken by the Egyptian Government without
previous negotiations.
(e)
Australia.
Mr. Katsuji Debuchi, former Japanese Ambassador to
the
*
Embassy's telegram No. 157 of July 20, 1935, despatches

(h)
Soviet Russia.
(1)
Soviet-"Manchukuoan" Incidents and the
Proposed Border Commission.
The Soviet-"Manchukuoan" border situation remained
761.
the most vital issue between the Soviet Union and Japan
throughout the month. Mr. Hirota having proposed to the
Soviet Ambassador in June the opening of negotiations for
the creation of a tripartite border commission among Japan,
"Manchukuo", and Soviet Russia, conferences were held
between the two officials concerning this question during
the month of July. It becane known that Soviet Russia
was opposed to a tripartite commission inasmuch as this
would give Japan two votes and the Soviet Union only one
vote. Toward the middle of the month reports from "Manchu-
kuo" indicated that the Kiantung Army was skeptical con-
cerning the value of a border commission so long as
Soviet Russia maintained its lerge concentration of forces
along the frontier. It appeared that the Soviets were
not averse to considering withdrawing somc of their forces
in the Far East but that they would not agree to the
establishment of a demilitarized zone on the Soviet
side
Embassy's telegram No. 92 of April 26 and despatch
No. 1281 of May 4, 1935.

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.98)
- 13 -
side of the frontier since such a concession would con-
stitute a relinquishment of a part of their sovereignty
over their own territory.* The Soviet Government apparently
holds firmly to the view that any border commission that
might be established must only adjudicate border incidents
and must not delimit the frontier, which was definitely and
permanently fixed by the Sino-Russian treaty of l860.
According to the Soviet thesis, the delimitation of 1860
included the large island at the confluence of the Ussuri
and Amur rivers which commands Habarovsk and the Trans-
Siberian Railway bridge over the Amur and for that reason
is coveted by the Japanese.
On July 1 the Soviet Ambassador in Tokyo delivered a
note of protest to the Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairs
against eight alleged violations of Soviet territory by
Japanese and "Manchukuoans" and the Foreign Office referred
the matter to General Minami, Commander of the Kwantung
Army, for investigation.** A reply to the Soviet note
of protest of July 1 was finally given to ir. Yureneff
on July 20. In his note Mr. Hirota dismissed the Soviet
Union's allegations of violations of Soviet territory by
Japanese and "Manchukuoans" as "either unfoundcd on fact"
or "as an attempt at disguising the issue where the
responsibility really lies on the Soviet side". Further-
more, the reply stated that the Japanese Government found
it difficult to comprehend "the real motive of the Soviet
Government in directing unwarranted abuse against the
acts of the Japanese authorities in "Manchukuo" and,
moreover
*
Embassy's telegram No. 146 of July 9, 1935.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1397 of July 12, 1935.

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
moreover, in apparently seeking to attract wide public
attention by giving sensational publicity to the exaggerated
misinformation in this connection". The report ended with
the following words: ". . . . . .. In the interest of
friendly relations between Japan and the Soviet Union,
the Japanese Government deeply regret the attitude of the
Soviet Government".
According to a Nippon Dempo despatch from Harbin
dated July 25, the "Manchukuoan" Government on that day
filed a strong protest with the Soviet Government regarding
a violation of "Manchukuoan" territory alleged to have
occurred on July 17 when Soviet political police on board
a gunboat photographed a "Manchukuoan" gunboat that had
grounded near the delta of the Ussuri and Amur rivers and
also landed on "Manchukuoan" territory, while soldiers on
the Soviet gunboat pointed machine guns toward the "Manchu-
kuoan" gunboat.
(2)
"Manchukuoan"-Outer Mongolian Conference
at Manchuli.
No progross was made in the conference convened at
Manchuli on June 2 between the "Manchukuoan" and Cuter
Mongolian delegates to settle the Buir Nor incidont.*
According to a Rengo despatch from Ulanbator which
appeared in the JAPAN CHRONICLE of July 9, Mr. Cheybalsan,
Acting Chairman of the Council of Ministers and Minister
for Foreign Arfairs of the Outer Mongolian Republic,
issued a statement to the local press on July 6 in which
he asserted that the "Manchukuoan" delegates had sought
to delay the progress of negotiations; that from the first
day of the conference the Outer Mongolian delegates had
begun
* See Monthly Report for June, 1935.

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
begun to doubt the sincerity of the intentions of the
"Manchukuoan" Government; and that the "Manchukuoan"
delegates had been unwilling to discuss the settlement
of the Buir Nor incident and instead had insisted on
discussing irrelevant matters. About the middle of the
month "Manchukuo" proposed to Outer Mongolia that diplo-
matic agents be exchanged by the two countries for the
purpose of preventing the repetition of border incidents
in the future. This proposal was refused by the Outer
Mongolian Government reportedly on the ground that
acceptance thereof would impair Outer Mongolia's status
as an independent republic. Instead, the Outer Mongolian
Government submitted e proposal for the establishment of
a joint "Manchukuo"-Mongolian border conmission. It was
reported from Hsinking that the Kwantung Army and the
"Manchukuoan" Foreign Office were indignant over the
Outer Mongolian refusal to exchange diplomatic agents.
(3)
Negotiations at Moscow for Revision of
the 1928 Fishery Convention.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 26 stated that, according
761.948
to a report received by the Japanese Foreign Office from
the Japanese Ambassador at Moscow, a deadlock had been
reached in the negotiations being held for revision of
the 1928 Fishery Convention owing to the rejection by the
Soviet Union of the proposals offered by the Japanese and
the Soviet insistence on acceptance of the Soviet pro-
posals by Japan.
(1)
Japan vis-à-vis the Italo-Ethiopian Dispute.
Tho Italo-Ethiopian dispute gave rise during the
month to further rumors concerning Japanese special
745.84
interests

=== Page 486 === (confidence 0.99)
-16 -
interests in Ethiopia. On July 10, however, the Foreign
Office spokesman stated that Japan had no special arrange-
ments with Ethiopia and denied that Japan had supplied
any ammunition to that country or that Ethiopia had
appealed to Japan for aid.* Probably acting not on
specific instructions but under the general instructions
issued tc him before his departure for Rome in October, 1934,
the Japanese Ambassador to Italy made a statement to the
Italian Foreign Office on July 16 to the effect that Japan
had no political interest in Ethiopia and no intontion of
intervening in the dispute between Italy and that country.**
This statement was immedietely published by the Italian
Foreign Office and was given much publicity in the Italian
press. At a press conference on July 19 the spckesman of
the Japancse Foreign Office stated that Ambassador Sugi-
mura had not acted on specific instructions from Tokyo
but that in the general instructions given to the Ambassador
before his departure for Rome he had been authorized to
dispel Italy's apparent suspicion concerning Japan's
interests in Abyssinia. Tho Japanese Government apparently
felt that it must partially disolaim Mr. Sugimura's assur-
ances in order that Japan's hands might not be tied in
future should it be deemed advisable to takc some action
in connection with the Italo-Ethiopian dispute.
The
Japanese Government's refusal to endorse its Ambassador's
statement to the Italian Foreign Office was intorpreted
by the Italian press as an unfriendly act while it was
apparently interpreted by the Ethiopian Emperor as a
manifestation of synpathy and good-will toward Ethiopia.

*

According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 28, Emperor
Haile Selassie told a correspondent of the Tokyo NICHI
NICHI in Addis Ababa that he was highly satisfied with
Japan's stand on the Italo-Ethiopian controversy, which
he regarded as one of sympathy toward his country.
Editorial comment on the merits of the two dis-
putants seemed curiously divided, some newspapers
decrying Italy's imperialistic policy toward a colored
race, others sympathetically comparing Italy's present
position with the Japanese position during the Manchurian
affair.
(j)
The Naval Issue.
With respect to the statement made in the House of
Commons on July 22 by Sir Bolton Eyres-Monsell, First
Lord of the Adniralty, in favor of the abandonment of
the ratio principle and the adoption of an agreement
based on prior announcoment of building programs, the
general feeling among Japanese naval circles, as reported
in the press, was that Great Britain had in some measure
recognized the justice of Japan's claims but that the
Japanese Navy must carefully examine any proposal for
prior announcement of building programs lest such a plan
should be an attempt to continue the ratio principle in
a disguised form.

III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS:
(a)
The Emperor.
Although it was reported in June that the judicial
authorities had decided to prosecute Dr. Minobe for
violation

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.98)
- 18 -
violation of the Publication Law in connection with the
theory contained in his books to the effect that the
Emperor is an institution, no action was actually taken
by the Judiciary during the month of July, nor did the
Government issue before the close of the month its much-
discussed statement designed to clarify national polity
in response to the demand of the Army and Navy. The
Government was reported to be contemplating a general
statement on the national polity, while the Army insisted
that in the Government's proposed statement the Minobe
theory be spccifically repudiated. When interviewed on
July 8, Premier Okada made the statement that Baron Ikki,
President of the Privy Counoil, may in the past have been
an advocate of the theory of tho .Emperor as an organ but
that he should not be molested since he no longer advocates
the theory. On July 9 the YOMIURI claimed to have been
told by Army circles that the Premier's declaration made
the Army suspicious of Admiral Okada's own views con-
cerning the nature of the Imperial prerogatives. Ac-
cording to the JAPAN CHRONICLE of July 18, General
Hayashi, Minister for War, dcclared on July 16 to a
deputation of the Seiyukai that if the Cabinet failed
to issue a statement condemning the Minobe thcory, ho
intended to take such action as he thought proper.
General Kunishig• Tanaka, President of the "Meirinkai",
a chauvinist society composed mainly of retired military
and naval officers, was reported to have called on the
Minister for War on July ll and to have submitted a copy
of a resolution passed by the society requesting that
steps be taken to suppress the Minobe theory. Further-
more, the Seiyukai continued to assail Dr. Minobe and to
clamor for a clarification by the Government of the
Emperor's position.
(b)

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
(b)
) Army and Navy.
(1)
Removal from Office of General Jinsaburo
Masaki as Inspector General of Military
Education in the Japanese Army and His
Replacement by General Jotaro Watanabe.
On July 16 the War Office announced that General
Jinsaburo Masaki had been removed from his post of
894.20
Inspector General of Military Education in the Japanese
Army and had been replaced by General Jotaro Watanabe.*
General Hayashi took this decision because of differences
with General Masaki regarding forthcoming Army shifts
and promotions and also because of past disagreements
between the two men, both personal and political.
The KokUlfIN, a newspaper said to be subsidized by
the Japanese Army, stated on July 16 that General Hayashi
had been impelled by two aims during his incumbency as
Minister of War, the unification of the Army, with the
establishment of stricter control over it, and the
assignment cf capable men in important positions. An
analysis of the list of August promotions and assignments,
unofficially released for publication by the War Office,
indicates that the conservative type of officer not
interested in political activities has becn favored over
the adventurous and undisciplined type addicted to
political scheming. However, these shifts in the Japanese
Army have been made from within the Army and cannot be
said to be the result of coercion of the military by the
civilian element.**
The JAPAN TIMES of July 18 and the JAPAN ADVERTISER
of July 19 reported that at a conference of the Army
members
*
Embassy's telegram No. 151 8f July 16, 1935.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 14l4. July 26, 1935, and Embassy's
telogram No. 151 of July 16, 1935.

=== Page 490 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
members of the Supreme Military Council held on July 17
had
there developed heated discussions on the question of
strengthening the central control of the Army. The latter
newspaper reported that one of the members of the Counoil
had declared that though the dismissal of General Masaki
could not have been avoided it was an outrage to allow
the impression to prevail among the people that General
Masaki, "who served the nation so faithfully during the
Manchurian crisis", was disturbing unity in the Army.
Another member was said to have expressed regret that
the removal of General Masaki had caused the public to
think that there were factions in the Army that could
not "see eye to eye", thus tending to weaken confidence
in the military. According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER,
after four hours of discussion the members of the Council
finally agreed that everything possiblc should be done
to strengthen the control within the Army and to take
precautions against future occurrences similar to the
Mesaki affair.
General Watanabe, the now Inspector General of
Military Education, was said by the Tokyo NICHI NICHI of
July 18 to have told a representative of that newspaper
on July 16 that he did not approve of participation by
the military in politics.
(2)
Reported Opposition of the War and Navy
Ministries to the Plan for an Aviation
Ministry.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 20, at 
Cabinet meeting held on the previous day both the War and
Navy Ministers expressed their opposition to the plan of
the Minister of Communications for the creation of an Air
Ministry to unify under a single administrative authority
the air services of the Army and the Navy as well as
civil

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.99)

civil aviation.
(3)
Appropriations for the Army and Navy in
the 1936-37 Budget.
Following the warning issued on July 26 by the Finance
Minister against increased issues of public bonds, Army
and Navy circles were reported to have stated the opinion
that if Mr. Takahashi's statement was issued as an attempt
to check the national defense budget, both the Army and
the Navy would be obliged to suggest new sources of
revenue to the Ministry of Finance. The Navy and the
Army Ministries were said to be adamant in their deter-
mination to obtain appropriations of over ¥710,000,000
and over ¥600,000,000, respectively. In a newspaper
interview on July 24 General Hayashi was reported to
have stated that the Japanese public lacked a proper
appreciation of the defense needs of the country; that
the pressure of the Pcwers on Japan and "Manchukuo" was
growing; that Japan's"emergency situation" was becoming
even more serious; and that he wanted the 1936-37 budget
to be compiled on the basis of "armaments first".
(c) Political Parties.
Premier Okada stated on July 25 that in a desire to
bring about honest elections at the next general election
for the Diet to be held in the spring of 193e, he intended
in the near future to have a conversation with the Presi-
the
dents of the Seiyukai,^Minseito, and the Kokumin Domei
besides conferring with the members of the election
rectification comaittee.
(a) Communists.
According to a news item which appeared in the JAPAN
TINES of July 28, in view of the steady increase in the
number of radicals in Japan and in view of their efforts
to strengthen their influence anong the agricultural
population

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.99)
-22 -
population, the Police Affairs Bureau of the Home
Department has decided to request an appropriation of
¥400,000 in the budget for the next fiscal year to
finance a drive against communists in this country.

IV.
COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The total national budget for the fiscal year 1936-37
is expected by Rengo and Nippon Dempo (news agencies)
894.51
to reach ¥2,900,000,000 or about ¥710,000,000 more than
thc current budget. As in former years, the Minister for
Finance has stated that he intends to scale down the
demands of the Ministries so that the budget for the next
fiscal year will not exceed that of the current year.
On July 26 Mr. Takahashi issued a statement on
government finance in which he refuted the view that the
issuance of deficit bonds over an indefinite period
would have no bad effect and added that excessive
issuance of such bonds would lead to the ruin of finance
and economy. The ASAHI of August 1 quoted the Finance
Minister as having stated on the last day of July that
out of consideration for the delicacy of international
relations he would recognize indispensable appropriations
for national defense but that he was not in favor of
approving unnecessary defense measures which were likely
to cause international competition in arms and which
might make Japan "hated by thc world".
There is little indication in the above-mentioned
ambiguous statoment of the Finance Minister that he
entertains any serious desire to engage in a struggle
with

=== Page 493 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
with the Army and the Navy for the purpose of substantially
paring down their reported combined demands of ¥1,3l0,-
o00,0o0. Furthermore, there seems to be no reason to
believe that he will be able to restrict military appro-
priations this year any more successfully than he and
other Finance Ministers have been able to do in recent
years.

VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a)
Industries.
(1)
Shipping
The JAPAN TI!ES of July 3 reported that at a con-
ference of representatives of the Nippon Yusen Kaisha,
the Osaka Shosen Kaisha, the Nanyo Yuscn Kaisha, and the
Ishihara Steamship Company and high officials of the
Department of Communications held at the official resi-
dence of the Minister for Communications on July 2, it
had been decided to establish a new shipping company
under the title, "Nanyo Kaiun Kabushiki Kaisha" ("South
Seas Shipping Company") which would operate betwcen Far
Eastern ports and Java and would be capitalized at
¥8,500,000.
(2)
Automotive Industry.
It is understood by the Tokyo NICHI NICHI of July 30
that the Minister for Commerce and Industry intends to
introduce a bill in the next Diet session for the establish-
ment on a firm basis and for the protection of the manu-
facture of automobiles in Japan. The principal pro-
visions of the proposed bill are said to be the follcwing:
the ostablishment of a system for granting franchises to
persons who desire to engage in the automotive industry;
the

=== Page 494 === (confidence 0.98)
- 24 -
the granting of special privileges to Japanese manu-
facturers of automobiles; and an inorease in the customs
tariff on automobile parts from 35% to 50% ad valorem.
(b) Civil Aviation.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 28 the
Comunications Ministry formally decided on July 27 to
launch a ten-year program, at a cost of ¥230,000,000,
for the development of civil aviation in Japan with an
initial outlay for the coming fiscal year of ¥13,700,000.
(c) Foreign Trade.
According to the preliminary ten-day figures, exports
from Japan for July amounted to ¥207,113,000 and imports
into this country amounted to ¥198,436,000. These figures
represent a slight increase in the value of Japan's
foreign trade as compared with June, during which month
exports were ¥191,252,000 and imports were ¥195,038,000.*
(a)
Financial Trends.
The stock market continued weak; the bond market
was fairly active; raw silk prices improved, while rayon
and cotton yarn prices were slightly weaker than in June;
the exchange was slightly stronger.*

Sekyo's political report for August
2036:
Aecording to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
August 1, Japanese exporters of cotton cloth
to the Philippines were "thoroughly frighten-
ed" by Governor General Murphy's reported
recommendation to the Philippine Legislature
that measures be taken for the protection of
American imports into the Philippines.
Probably the chief reason for the
rosignation of Count Hayashi and the appoint-
ment of Mr. Matsuoka in his stead is that
Hayashi was not considered by the Kwantung
Army to be forceful enough for its project
of using the South Manchuria Railway in the
economic penetration of North China.
Mr. Matsuoka has stated in public that his
prinainal task as President of the South
Hanchuria Railway is the economic penetra-
tion or North China. Incidentally General
Minami is reported as having stated that
not only North China but all of China
could be included in the Kwantung Army's
plan of economic penetration.
According

=== Page 500 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
According to a Hsinking despatch
to the NICHI NICHI, the opinion is growing
among "Manchukuo" officials that it is
time for "Manchukuo" to abandon its
attitude of indifference to continued
non-recognition by foreign powers and
to begin applying positive measures upon
the nationals of such powers by withdraw-
ing extraterritorial rights enjoyed by
such nationals.
Upon his return to Tokyo, Mr. Matsu-
daira, Japanese Ambassador to Great Britain,
is reported by the press as stating that
the general opinion in Great Britain
favored the maintenance of friendly rela-
tions with Japan rather than cooperation
with the United States to "suppress" Japan
as advocated by General Smuts and others.
The Japanese Government 1s reported
to be planning to approach the French
Government with a view to amending the
Treaty of Commerce between French Indo-
China and Japan. For the past few years
the balance of trade has been preponderant-
ly in favor of French Indo-China.
The press reports that a new Japanese
legation at Addis Ababa will be established
1n January 1936.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) The VANITY FAIR Caricature Incident.
There appeared in the August issue of VANITY
FAIR a caricature of the Emperor of Japan which brought
forth a protest from the Japanese Government, perhaps
partly because of a feeling on the part of the Japa-
nese that they must make an issue of the matter after
the vigorous manner in which they had protested against
the NEw LIFE magazine affair in China. The drawing in
question, under the caption "Not On Your Tintypo",
portrayed the Emperor of Japan carting away the Nobel
Peace Prize. The subject first appeared in the Japa-
nose pross on August 4 and on the following day it was
featured by the vernacular newspapers. Although the
caricature was not described, it was considered by the
press to be an insult to the Emperor and to the Imperial
House. It is probable that the whole affair was drawn
to the attention of the Japanese Foreign Office by the
newspapers, which appear to have influenced the Foreign
Office in the action taken.* While the article in the
NEw LIFE magazine was referred to as the"lose majest6"
case, the drawing in the American magazine was called
the "VANITY FAIR Disrespect Incident".
After the call at the State Department of the
Japanese Ambassador to the United States, the Secretary
of
* Imbassy's despatch No. 1426, August 9, 1935.

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of State made available to the press a statement
to the effeot that he was always sorry when ineidents
ocourred or situations arose which were taken amiss
and occasioned misunderstanding between the United
States and any other country. The use of the word
"sorry", taken out of its contert by the Japanese
press, was construed to signify a definite expression
of regret over the VANITY FAIR caricature, although
the Seoretary made no apology and did not express
regret in this line.*
(b). The "Manchukuo" Oil Monopoly.
During ugust there were no important develop-
ments in the "Manohukuo" oil monopoly as regards its
effect upon the American oil companies operating in
that district, the American oil interests continuing
to refuse t o sell crude oil to the monopoly and to
the Manchuria Oil Company.**
(o). American Oil Interests in Japan.
Although it appeared during the early part of
the month that a solution might soon be reached in the
difficulties confronted by the merican Oil companies
under the Japanese Petroleum Industry Law, prospects
for a happy settlement of this matter seemed less hope-
ful toward the end of August. The temporary solution
mentioned in previous monthly reports as having been
discussed
* Department's telegram No. 123, August 5, 1935.
** mbassy's despatch No. 1444, August 23, 1935.

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
discussed by the pertinent Japanese authorities would
seem to be a definite postponement of the date of en-
forcing the requirement that oil companies in Japan
carry six months' stocks in storage. It is not likely,
however, that the Japanese defense Departments will be
satisfied with such an arrangement, since the large re-
serves of oil desired by them would not be thus acquired,
nor is it likely that the American oil companies would
be content with the nebulous position which such a tem-
porary "solution" would place them in.*
(d) Proposed Legislation to Establish a License
System for the Automobile Industry in Japan.
On August 9 the Cabinet approved a draft bill,
sponsored by the War Ministry, which is to be submitted
to the next session of the Diet and which contains the
following provisions: a license system shall be estab-
lished for assembling automobiles and for the manufacture
of automobile parts; licenses shall be granted only to
companies organized under Japanese law and of which a
majority of the stook is held by Japanese subjects; and
companies already established shall not be affected to
the extent of present production capacity, but any ex-
tension or expansion in equipment or output facilities
shall be subject to control under the law.** If passed,
the bill will seriously affect two American companies, the
Ford Motor Company of Japan, Ltd., and General Motors
Japan, Ltd., which are the only large automobile
manufacturers
* Embassy's despatch No. 1465, September 6, 1935.
** Bmbassy's telegram No. 165, August 10, 1935 and
Imbassy!s despatoh No. 1431, August 10, 1935.

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.98)
一 4 -
manufacturers operating in Japan. It appeared from
statements made to Mr. Dickover, First Secretary of
the Embassy, by Mr. Kurusu, the Chief of the Com-
mercial Bureau of the Foreign Office, that the proposed
legislation was primerily & military project and was
aimed chiefly at the Ford Motor Company of Japan, Ltd.
The purpose of the projeoted law is apparently to pre-
vent further erpansion of the foreign automobile companies
and thus to enable Japanese automobile menufacturers
to obtain the natural increase in the demand for auto-
mobiles. It will be recalled that for some time the War
Ministry has given its support to the infant Japanese
automobile industry and has opposed the expansion of the
Ford Motor Company of Japan, Ltd.*

(r) Founding of the Philippine-Javan Socioty.

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
During August the Japanese public and press displayed
less interest in North China affairs than during previous
793.94
months, the situation having quieted down to some extent
on the surface. However, two incidents occurred which
were interpreted by some of the Japanese newspapers as
evidence that anti-Japanese feeling in North China was
still rampant. The first incident, which occurred at
Lwanchow, was the killing on August 4 of Li Tsuo-chou,
Commander of the Third Peace Preservation Corps of the
demilitarized zone and reportedly pro-Japanese in sym-
pathy, and the wounding of a Japanese gendarme at the
same time by Chinese who were alleged to be members of
the Blue Shirt organization. The other occurrence was a
raid by bandits on the Mukden-Peiping passenger train
near Shanhaikwan on August 15, in which meveral Chinese
and Koreans were killed and a number of passengers were
wounded. According to a RENGo despatch from Hsinking
dated August 16, the Kwantung Army authorities believed
that this bandit attack had been instigated by anti-Japa-
nese groups.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 19,
the opening of the conference of Chinese Government offi-
cials at Lushan called forth from the Japanese War Ofrice
a statement to the effect that similar conferences in
the past have resulted only in empty gestures of friendship
toward Japan; that despite the pledge of the Chinese
authorities to control anti-Japanese actions in North
China, "undesirable incidents" are happening in quick
succession;

=== Page 513 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
succession; that unless China creates a truly pro-
Japanese attitude in the district adjoining "Manchukuo",
the relations among Japan, "Manohukuo", and China can not
be placed on a satisfactory basis; and that China must
recognize "Manchukuo" before any Sino-Japanese cooperation
can be achieved.
With regard to the Japanese economic penetration of
North China desired by the Japanese Army*, Major Tan Taka-
hashi, Japanese resident officer at Peiping, was reported
by a NIPPON DEMPO (news agency) despatoh datod August 7..
to have obtained the agreement of General Yen Hsi-shan,
Governor of Shansi Province, to cooperate in creating a
Sino-Japanese economic entente in North China.**

was not considered by the Kwantung Army to be forceful

2. Proposed Abolition of Extraterritoriality.
LE
On August 9 the Cabinet reached a formal decision,
which received Imperial sanction on the same day, to abolish
Japan's extraterritorial privileges in "Manchukuo" and to
surrender to the Government of "Manchukuo" the administra-
tive rights in the South Manchuria Railway zone. In com-
menting upon the Cabinet's decision, the spokesman of the
Foreign Office cited the progress made by "Manchukuo" as
an independent country as the reason for the decision;
stated that the abolition of extraterritoriality in "Man-
chukuo" should be effected"in keeping with the spirit of
the treaties in force" and by gradual stages; and explained
that the proposed transfer of administrative rights in the
South
* Embassy's despatch No. 1424, August 8, 1935.

"Manohukuo" had no connection with Japan's rights in
the Kwantung Leased Territory.
According to a Hsinking despatch to the NICHI NICH
WE

chukuoan" ofricials that it is time for "Wanchukuo" to
abandon its attitude of indiffarence to continued non-
recognition by foreign Powers and to begin applying
positive pressure upon the nationals of such Powers by
withdrawing the extraterritorial rights enjoyed by these
Powers in "Menchukuo".

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Army.
On August 12 Major General Tetsuzan Nageta,
Director of the Military Affairs Bureau of the War Office
894.20
and as such the ranking official under the War Minister,
was assassinated in his office by Lieutenant Cclonel
Saburo Aizawa, who presumably bore a grudge against the
General because the latter had recently assigned him as a
military instructor in a school in Taiwan.* It is probable
that Aizawa had glorified his personal spite in his own
mind with the "patriotic"motive of avenging the netion
for General Nagata's alleged insincerity in connection
with the industrial preperedness of Japan, of which the
General had been accused by certain army pamphlets.**
General Hayashi, the Minister for War, decided not to
resign immediately despite the Japanese traditiou that
in similar cases the Minister should resign to show his
sense of responsibility. On ugust 26 he issued in-
structions to the Army in which he expressed his regret
over the assassination and admitted his responsibility,
but
* mbassy's despatch No. 1450, August 23, 1935.
** Embassy's despatch No. 1455, September 5, 1935.

=== Page 520 === (confidence 0.98)
- 15 -
but at the same time reiterated his program for the
unification of the Army.
(b). The Navy.
It was reported by the JAPAN TIMES of Angust 17
that at a conference held by officials of the Navy and
894130
Finance Ministries on the previous day the officials of
the Ministry of Finance had erplained the general financial
condition of the Government and the policy of Mr. Taka-
hashi to reduce the national bond issue, and had urged
the officials of the Ministry of the Navy to reduce their
demands for the 1936-37 budget. According to this news-
paper, the officials of the Navy Ministry replied thet
the amount demanded by them was the minimum consistent
with the maintenance of national defense.
(c). The Emperor.
As a consequence of the widespread opposition to
the views of various commentators on the Constitution,
particularly Dr. Minobe, on August 3 the Cabinet issued a
statement clarifying the position of the Emperor as the
supreme authority in Japan and condemning the theory
that the Emperor is only the orgen for the exercise of
the supreme power.* No direct action was teken during
the month against Dr. Minobe himself, who is reported
to have stated to a TOKYO NICHI NICHI interviewer on
August 8 that he could not elter his convictions even if
made to serve a term in prison for them.
(d). The Political Parties.
On August 7 the Seiyukai made public its platform
for
* mbassy's despatch No. 1429, August 10, 1935.

for the prefectural elections to be held in September,
1955, and for the Diet elections to be held in February,
1936. This platform contains the following points, as
reported by the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of August 8: economic
oollaboration between Japan and "Manchukuo" and Sino-

appropriations in the l935-36 budget. According to the

less than those made the previous year, es a menifestation
of the collaboration of the other Ministries with the Ministry
of Finence.

with 51 foreign and 250 Japenese delegates ettending.
At the opening meeting addresses were delivered by the
following persons: Prince Iyesato Tokugawa, Honorary
President of the Conference; Premier Okada; Mr. Hirota,
the Minister for Foreign ffairs; Mr. Matsude, the

owing to the active participation of the foreign dele-
gates. It was decided that a World New Education Conference
would be convened in Tokyo in 1940 in connection with the

II.
RELATIONS WITH OTIER COUNTRIES.
(a)
China.
(1)
Reported New Japanese Policy in North China.
Further evidence appeared during the month thet the
Japanese Government was formulating a definite policy for
North China.* As reported by the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of
September 26, Major General Tada, the Commander of the
Tientsin Garrison of the Japanese Army, outlined the
Japanese Army's attitude toward No th China as follows
in statements made to Japanese newspapermen on September 24:
the Japanese Army would find means of eliminating anti-
Japanese and anti-"Manchukuo" elements in North China,
if necessary even resorting to the Army's "power and
authority"; the five North China provinces "might find
it necessary" to rid themselves of political and financial
relations with the Nankine Government in order to eliminate
such anti-Japanese and anti-"Manchukuo" elements; and in
such case the Japanese Army would guide the five provinces
to form a united self-governing body to the end that the
Sovietization of the region might be prevented. :hether
or
* See Embassy's Monthly Report for July, 1935, Section II (a).

=== Page 536 === (confidence 0.97)
一5
or mot Major General Tada's statements were authorized
by the Japanese Ministry of Har, it became apparent
during the month that a section of opinion in the Japanese
Army was in favor cf diesoclating the five provinces of
North China from the Nanking Government and of establishing
there an autonomous government friendly to Japan and willin
to grant to the Japanese the economic privileges dosired
by them.* Furthermore, it appeared that some of the
Japanese Army ofiicers in North China were determined to
follow this course, preferably with the consent of the
Japanese Government but perhaps even without such consent
if the plan should not meet with the approvel of the home
Government. Although the Japanese Foreign Office pro-
fessed to have no knowledge of any new Japanese policy
toward China, the newspapers discussed at length the
alleged new policy, reporting that the Ministries of
Forelen Affairs, Finance, Wer, and Navy hed been holding
mectings to study the basic principles of this polioy.
Japencre public opinion seemed to favor a course of action
similar to that advocated by Major General Tada. More-
over, it appeared from the general tone of the pronouncc-
ments of the military and of newspaper Iticles and
caitorials that the Japanese nation in concrel had now
a point of view toward North Chine similer to the attitude
toward Manchuria which prevailed beforc l93l, looking upon
that region as e sphere in which the Japaneso have special
interests and over which Japan is entitled to a dogree
of political and economic control.*
