(h) Leaguo of Nations.
WE
Nippon Dempo and Rongo despatches from Geneva dated
Septomber 18 roported that at a meeting of the Assembly
862i.01
of the League of Nations Dr. Christian Lange, of Norway,
had expressed dissatisfaction with Japan's explanation
to the Mandates Commission that the large sums of money
spent by that country on its mandated islands were for
The

despatches from Geneva also stated that Dr. Lange had

territories on an equal footing with products of members
of the League. At a press conference on September 20
the spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office reiterated
Japan's contention that the harbor improvements had been
made in its mandated islands purely for economic and
civil purposes. Mr. Amau characterized as untenable
the suggestion that equality of treatment in trade
should be denied Japan because it had left the Loague of
Nations.

(1) Canada.
According to press reports, the Premier of Canada
informed the Japanese Minister in Ottawa on September 5
that if the Japanese discriminatory 50% surtax on certain
Canadian goods was not discontinued, the Canadian Govern-
ment would be obliged to give notice that it considered
the Anglo-Japanese commercial treaty no longer appli-
cable to Canada and that the Dominion was free to take
such steps as national interests might require. A week
later, it is reported, the Japanese Minister to Canada
communicated his Government's reply to the effect that
the Japanese Government earnestly hoped that, by further
negotiations, the present issue would be brought to an
amicable solution and that if Canada rescinded its
arbitrary measures against Japan the latter country in
return would remove its discriminatory import tariffs
against Canada.*

a Japanese reactionary organization. There appeared

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Army.
(1)
The Resignation of General Hayashi, Minister
of War and the Appointment of General
994.20
Kawashima in His Stead.
It will be recalled that General Hayashi decided
not to resign immediately after the assassination of
Major General Tetsuzan Nagata, Director of the Military
Affairs Bureau of the War Officc, which took place on
August 12.* The reason for the General's decision vas
that he was desirous of carrying out his plans for the
unification of the Army, under stricter control, without
division into dissonant cliques and without interest'
in political affairs.** He therefore called a scries
of conferences of military officers and officials of
the War Ministry and explained to them his ideas on the
enforcement of stricter control within the Army. Having
inaugurated his program, General Hayashi formally
resigned as Ministor for War on September 4, giving
as the reason for his step his responsibility for the
assassination of General Nacata. General Yoshiyuki
Kawashima, a momber of the Suprome :Var Council, was
appointed to succeed Goneral Hayashi. A general opinion
prevailed
See Embassy's Monthly Report for August, 1935.
** Embassy's dospatch No. 1455 of Septembor 5, 1935.

=== Page 547 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
prevailed that Kawashima would continue the policies or
Hayashi.
(2) The Appointment of Lieutenant General Furusho
as Vice Minister for War.
General Kawashima, the new Minister for War, obtained
the appointment of Lieutenant General Mikio Furusho as
Vioe Minister for Jar on September 21. Lieutenant General
Hashimoto, former Vice Minister for War, was, according
to reports, expeeted to be appointed a Divisional
Commander but refused to accept such a high appointment
because of his feeling of responsibility for the assassina-
tion of Lieutenant General Nagata, and consequently was
attached temporarily to the General Staff.
(3) Japanese Army Pamphlet of September 5.
A further pamphlet was issued by the War Ministry
on September 5 concerning the international "crisis",
1ts apparent aim being to keep the Japanese public
convinced of the necessity of continued military
expenditures.* This pamphlet represents Soviet Russia
as Japan's principal enemy and the United States as its
secondary enemy; it describes Japan as China's only true
friend; it expounds Japan's great mission for the
establishment of universal peace; and it insists upon
a redistribution of the world's territory and natural
resources to bring about a durable foundation for
international justice.
(b) The Navy.
The spokesman of the Navy made public during the
month the Navy's interpretation of the relationship
between
* Embassy's despatch No. 1466 of September 7, 1935.

=== Page 548 === (confidence 0.98)
-17 -
between national defense and naval appropriations in
the budget. This interpretation included the following
points: an attempt to restrict naval expenditures at
this time might prove fatal to Japan's national existence:
expenditures for naval defense must be maintained at a
high level regardless of revenue deficits and despite
the possible necessity for the continued issuance of
deficit-covering bonds; and, as has been proved in the
past, there is no definite limit to deficit-covering
bond issues.
(c) The Emperor.
Not content with the statement issued by the Govern-
ment on August 3 in condemnation of Dr. Minobe's
institutional theory of the Emperor,* the Japanese
military continued to press the Governnent to take more
drastic steps for the extermination of heretical ideas
concerning the Emperor. The judicial authorities decided
not to prosecute ur. Minobe on the charge of violating
Article 26 of the fublicetion Law after the receipt from
him of a letter in which he steted that he was eoine to
resien from the House of Peers and expressed regret for
the controversy that had taken place in the House over
his theory.** At a Cabinet mecting held on September 25,
the War and Navy Ministcrs presented the following joint
demands to Premier Okada: that the Justice Ministry
publish the faots attendant upon the decision of the
Judiciary not to prosecute Dr. Minobe; that the Govern-
ment categorically state whether its views on the
constitutional theory of the Emperor coincide with the
rlat
* Embassy s despütch No. 1429 of August 10, 1935.
** Embassy's despatch No. 150l of October 3, 1935.

=== Page 549 === (confidence 0.98)
- .0 -
Flat rejection or that theory by the rmy and thc Navy:
and that the Cabinet issue an additioncl statement
setting forth the measures which are heing taken for
the olimination of the theory in questicn. According
to press reports, officials of the Ministry of Justice
answered the first demand by stating that they would
consider issuing a statement of the facts of the case.
With rogard to the second demand, the Premier said that
the Cabinet's views coincided with those of the Army
and the Navy. Admiral Okada also promised to comply
with the third demand.
The Army and the Navy appeared to be momentarily
setisfied with the Government's attitude toward Dr.
Minobe himself and toward his theory. However, the
defense services and also the Seiyukai were, according
to reports, determined to force the resignation of
Baron Ikki, President of the Privy Council, and Dr.
Kanamori, Director of the Legislative Bureau, on the
charge that they were advocates of Dr. Minobe's theory.
The foregoing developments are further proof of the
complete victory won by the Army and of the hold which
the fundamentalist theory of the Emperor has on the
Japanese nation as a whole.
(d) The Cabinet.
Mr. Takejiro Tokonami, Minister of Communications,
diod on September 8. On September 11 Mr. Keisuke
Mochizuki, who had served as Communications Minister
in 1926 and 1927, was appointed to this post to succeed
Mr. Tokonami and was expelled from the Seiyukai Party
because of his having agreed to associate himself with
the Okada Cabinet.

month; the stock market was active on account of

According to a report which apperared in the JAFAN

and New Zealand in view of the incrcasing trade with
that Dominion. It is understood that three ships will
be placed in this servicc, cach of 6,700 tons.

Tokyo's despatch No. 1588,
December 11, 1935, 1s 1ts monthly
political report for November 1935, and
contains a resumé of subjects otherwise
currently reported. In addition it
mentions several subjects which should
be noted, as follows:
A German economic mission arrived
at Tokyo during the first week of November
and at the end of the month left for
Manchuria. The German mission urged
larger purchases by Manchuria of German
products, pointing out that in 1934
Germany bought Manchurian soy beans to
the value of Yen 53,000,000 while
Manchuria purchased but Yen 12,000,000
worth of merchandise from Germany. In
Tokyo the mission encountered several
expressions of Japanese resentment of
German trade restrictions (page 10).
According to press reports, the
Italian Ambassador at Tokyo submitted
to the Foreign Office "for reference"
the text of Italian protest of November
12 to those powers contemplating the
application of sanctions against Italy
(page 12).
It

=== Page 581 === (confidence 0.99)
-.2-
It is understood that, after the
usual adjournment following its opening
meeting, the Diet will reopen for business
on January 21, 1936 (page 13).
On November 2 Lieutenant Colonel
Aizawa was indicted for the murder on
August 12 of Lieutenant General Nagata.
Simultaneously the Ministry of War issued
a statement that Aizawa had convinced
himself, before committing the crime,
that the country was drifting away from
the principles upon which the Empire was
founded (page 14).
On November 5 Japanese police
frustrated another reactionary plot
against the lives of cabinet ministers
and other high government officials
(page 15).
On November 2 the Minister of
Communicatior. officially announced
that the Japai.sse Government would grant
a charter to a new national news agency
to be called the Domei Tsushinsha. The
Embassy is informed that the new agency
1s to include Rengo but not Nippon Dempo
(page 15).
On November 30 the Cabinet approved
for presentation to the D1et a 1936-37
budget of Yen 2,272,000,000, an increase
of Yen 78,586,000 over the previous
budget. Almost 47% of this amount is
accounted for by the combined army and
navy estimates (page 16).

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American Oil Interests in Japan.
On November 2nd the representatives of the Standard
and Shell oil interests informed the Vice Minister for
Commerce and Industry that the directors of their respective
companies were relying on the Japanese officials to carry
out the program in the Five-Point Memorandum agreed to
in April, l935.* Four days later officials of the Japanese
Government informed the representatives of the two companies
that the Five-Point Memorandum represented what the Japanese
officials hoped to be able to ac omplish but that it did
not constitute a promise; that a compensetion plan had
been developed end the enforcement of the tockholdine
requirements of the law had been postponed, it having been
found im"ossible to alter the stcckholcinf recuirements;
th t the six months stockholding requirement must be
considered as fixed and beyond further ne otietion; and
that the oil comoanies must decide vhether or not they
would continue to do business in Japen under the present
law and rerulations.* The oil companies' representatives
were summoned to the Ministry cf Conmerce and Industry on
November 15th and were told thet the comanies should
indicate, as soon as possible, their intentions with
rerard to the six months stora e obli. tion. Toward the
end of the month the question cane up of makine further
representations to the Jananese Government.***
Therefore
no answer was iven to the Janese Ministry of Jomnerce
and Industry as to whether the oil companies desired to
continue operatin in Japen under the present law and
regulations.

Although utterances made by officials of the
Japanese Foreign Office and members of the Jananese
Foreign Service concerning the autonony movement in
North China were incongruously worded, those officials
in effect endorsed the movement, stating variously that
Japan wanted a reorganized Government in North China which
would be friendly to Sino-Jananese economic cooperation
in that region and would work for the welfere of the people;*
that Janan was interested in the question of autonomy
because of the propinquity of "Manchukuo" and the large
Japanese interests in the area**; and that although the
Japanese Government had not contemplated the use of armed
force it micht be compelled to do so if trcops from
Nankins moved north to suporess the autonomy movement.
Much more positive in their views, the Japanese Army
authorities stated that the Army would positively resist
any movement of Nankin troups intc North China and that
the autonomy movement deserved the sympathetic support
of Janan since it had arisen fron the desire of the
pecole cf North China to rid themselves of the Nanking
Government. The Arny's attitude clearly showed that the
autonomy movement had been encineered by the Japanese
Military in China. Furthermore, the fact of Japanese
assistance was not denied by the Foreign office.***
November 20th the Embassy learnec, on cood authority,
that the Japanese Army would move south of the Great Wall
if troops from Nanking attempted to enter Hopei Frovince,
the implication bein thet in such case Imperial sanction
would te siven.****

(c)
Soviet Russia.
The fundamental nature of Soviet-Japanese relations
remained unaltered during the month under review, the
Russians continuing to enjoy a strone diplomatic position
vis-à-vis Japan and the Japanese seemingly being too
engrossed with North China to devote much a ttention to
Russia. No settlement of the border problem was reached,
as the two countries maintained their diametrically
opposed standpoints with regard to the proposed border
commission.* The Soviet Government sent a reply tc the
Japanese Government's counter-protest of October 25th,
stating, according to a Minpon Dempo-United Press despatch
from Moscow dated lovember 5th, that the Japanese counter-
protest was entirely unfounded and charein? the Japanese
with evadin investi ation of the recent border clashes
despite the fact that such investi ation would cive "the
createst guarantee of establishing the truth". The
"Manchukuc"-Outer Mongolia Conference at Manchuli,
desifned to settle frontier disnutes and to arranfe for
the exchane of representatives of some sort, ended in
a complete breakdown on November 25th, according to a
Ren o despatch from Manchuli. Apparently, the Soviet
Government saw no need to instruct the Cuter Mongolian
dele ates to accept "Manchukuo's" demand that diplomatic
representatives be exchan  ed between the t':o Governments.
Accordin to the above-mentioned engo renort, in a
tatement issued after the rupture of the Conference
the 'Manchukuo" delegates ceclared that they were convinced

The Naval Conference.

the Army eppointed Colonel Sotsude Suzuki, attached to

abclition or else a drastic reduction of war vessels
deemed offensive b: Japan, and the replenishment of
vessels considered by Janan to be defensive in nature.

The Emperor.

The Minobe Controversy.
As an aftermath of the Minobe controversy over the

decided to establish an Education and tudy Council as

the Japanese State. According to the OSAKA AINCII of

defects of Occiaental culture and the inculcation of the
spirit of Japan among the people.

told of the Tresident's words by press corresn ndents,

the countries to which he referred. The only editorial
comment th t a" eareu was in the JAFAN TI: IS AND :AIL of
January 6 which criticized the bold wording of the Hessafe
but nointed out that it v. s intended prineril: fcr domestic
consumoticn vith a view to insurin, the passare of further
neutrality le islation.**

V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Shortly after the withdrawal of the Jananese delegation
from the London Naval Conference, Mr. Takahashi, the Finance
Minister, made a statement to the press that the failure of
the conference was not an event that need lead to any marked
increase in Japan's naval armaments and warranted no general
increase in taxation. In his sneech before the House of
Representatives on January 21 Mr. Takahashi announced that
894.51
the national budget for the fiscal year 1936-37 had been
balanced with an income and expenditure of ¥2,278,000,000,
which represented an increase over the fiscal year 1935-36
of ¥62,000,000; stated that the international situation
demanded a large expenditure for netional defense; declared
that there must be no decrease in the appropriation for
public welfare, cwint to the impcverished state of the
rural districts; remarked on the steady increase in Japan's
foreien trade; and expressed gratification over the closer
conomic ties with "Manchukuo".
In a New Year messace issued through the Reneo News
Agency, Baron Seinosuke Goh, President of the Tokyo Chamber
of Commerce and Industry, stated that there were already
signs that absorption of Government bonds would not be so
easy

=== Page 657 === (confidence 0.98)
- 19 -
easy in the future; thet due to the Government's lac: of a
financial policy for the future uncertainty had already
begun to be felt over the financial situation; and that
to remove this uncertainty it wculd be necessary for the
Government to establish a firm policy. Less pessimistic
views on the financial situation were attributed by the
Jananese press to Mr. Fukai, Governor of the Bank of Japan,
who was quoted as having said in the early part of the month
that in his belief economic activity would become even

Foreisn Trade.
Janan's exports for January were V174, 550,000, while
imports were ¥245,042,000, as ccmpared with exports for
December of ¥222,796,000, and imports of ¥246,53,000.
Exports were small for seasonal reasons, while impor's
did not decrease owin to unusually larce imports of raw
cotton and raw wool. Nevertheless, there were increases
both in exports and imports in January over exports and
imports for the same peried last year.
The JAPAN TIMES cf January 11 pointed out that Japan's
trade for the year 1935 was the hichest that the Empire
had ever known; that Japan had not had so srall an adverse
trade balance since the year 19l4; and that the foreign
trade figures for 1935 represented an increase of more than
900% over the year 1914.

higher than during the preceding month; industry and trade
maintained a satisfactory trend; wholesale and retail
commodity prices were considerably hirher; cnd the average
yen selling rate was 0.28896 as compared with 0.28625
for December.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American-Japanese Relations during the
Februarv 26 Incident.
News of the uprising of young Army officers and the
assassinations carried out by them was received by the
711.
Embassy at 8:15 a. m. on February 26.* The Counselor of
the Embassy succeeded in entering the Foreign Office at
9 o'clock in the morning but the officials interviewed
there seemed to have scant information about what had
happened. A censorship was placed on outgoing news des-
patches but the Embassy succeeded in getting its telegrams
through to Washington.
On the morning of February 27 the Ambassador left
with the Secretary of the Minister for Foreign Affairs a
note embodying the Secretary of State's condolences sent
on behalf of the American Government and people.**
The
Ambassador also left a p. c. card with the Secretary of
the Minister for Foreign Affairs to be delivered to the
family of Premier Okada, who was then believed to be dead.
He called on the families of Admiral Saito and Mr. Taka-
hashi to express his condolences and in addition he called
at the residence of Admiral Suzuki, Court Chamberlain, to
inquire after the latter's health.
On the same day the Embassy, along with other Missions
in Tokyo, received a statement from the War Office which
stated that the lives and property of foreign and Japanese
residents in the city were not in danger. By noon the
American Embassy was heavily guarded by Government troops.
On the following day a member of the American-European
Section of the General Staff attached to the Martial Law
Headquarters
of
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1721 March 6, 1936
**
Department's telegram No. 25 of February 26, and
Embassy's telegram No. 40 of February 27, 1936.

=== Page 667 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Headquarters came to the Embassy and stated thet there
might be an attack in the afternoon against the rebels
by Government troops; that therefore the Embassy might be
in the danger zone; and that the Martial Law Headquarters
had made arrangements to transfer the Embassy's personnel
to the Military Academy as a place of safety.* The Ambassador
declined this offer with thanks.
On February 29 the activities of Government and
insurgent troops, which culminated in the entire sunpression
of the latter in the afternoon, were easily observable
from the Chancery.
(b). Reaction in Japan to Senator Pittman's Speech.
Senator Pittman's speech in the Senate on Februery 12,
7119
in which he attacked Japan's policies, aroused less press
comment than might have been expected.** However, the
ASAHI, the NICHI NICHI, and the HOCHI attributed the speech
to anti-Japanese feeling and to the Senator's wish to be
in the public eye during election year; they denied the
charge that Japan was closing the door in China; and they
regretted that Senator Pittman, a man of high position in
American public life, had made such "unfounded" charces
against Japan. The spokesman of the Foreign Office refused
to discuss the speech. As far as the Embassy could judge
from the newspapers, there was a desire among the Japanese
authorities to minimize the importance of the speech rather
than lend importance to it by allowing the newspapers to
assume a belligerent attitude.***

(c).
Comment on Sirovich's Speech in the House of
Representatives.
On February 7 foreien newspaper correspondents asked
the Foreign Office spokesman to comment on the speech made
by Representative Sirovich before the Merchant Marine Com-
mittee of the House of Representatives, in which it was
alleged that Japanese fishing boats were being used for
espionage off the coast of Alaska and that Japan planned
to seize the Alaskan fisheries in the event of an American-
Japanese war. The spokesman replied that he had not read
the speech and added that it would be a "test case for
American common sense" to learn how much of the American
public believed in such a story.
(a).
Unfounded Rumors of Negotiations for an Americen.
Japanese Political Agreement.
According to the TOKYO NICHI NICHI (Japanese edition)
of February 1, as a result of conversations between the
Japanesc Ambassador to the United States and the Secretar,
of State and between the Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairi
and the Ambassador, two principles were established: that
there are no American-Japanese problems that cannot be
solved by diplomatic means and that each country should
refrain from irritating speech and actions. The TOKYo NICHI
NICHI further stated that measures were being taken for the
conclusion of a political agreement between the United
States and Japan based on the principle of the division of
the Pacific Ocean into American and Japanese spheres of
activity. The JIJI of February 1 reported that the Minister
for Foreign Affairs was negotiating for the conclusion of a
tripartite

=== Page 669 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
tripartite agreement among the United States, Great
Britain, end Japan, whereby each country would underteke
the maintenance of peace in its own sphere, the United
States in the Americas, Great Britain in Eurooe, and Japan
in the Far East. These unfounded reports may possibly have
been trial balloons put forward by the Foreign Office.*
(e). Proposed Discriminatory Automobile Legislation.
It appeared almost certain during the month that
legislation would be enacted to place tne autonobile
manufacturing industry of Japan under a system of control
by means of licenses because both the Army and imoortant
industrialists were determined to build up a purely Japanese
motor car industry.**
EA
(f). Imports of Japanese Textiles into the Philippires
Negotiations between the Embassy and the Foreien Office
on the question of importation of Japanese textiles into
the Fhilippines continued throrhout the month but no
agreement was reeched. Judging from the statements of Mr.
Kurusu, Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreirn Offic
both the Japanese Government and the Osake textile exporters
were distinctly at variance with the Department's point of
view regarding the method of restricting imports of Japunese
textiles into the Philippines. It was mede clear by Mr.
Kurusu that the Osaka textile manuracturers would not
consider any proposition whatsoever for the limitation of
exports of Japanese rayon textiles into the Philippine
Commonwealth. In fact, Mr. Kurusu told & member of the
Embassy staff that he had used this question as a weapon
in attempting to extract concessions from the Osaka business..
men
*
Embassy's telegram No. 21 of February 1, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1659 of February 5, 1936.

=== Page 670 === (confidence 0.98)

men. One of the significant remarks made by Mr. Kurusu
was to the effect that, as far as adverse legislation
against the Japanese was concerned, the United States could
no longer make laws for the Islands, since they had become
a Commonwealth. When asked whet measures the Japanese
textile interests would adopt in case efforts were made
in the United States to restrict the importation into the
Philippines of Japanese textiles through legislation and
tariff increases, Mr. Kurusu replied that if his proposals
could not be accepted and if the American textile interests
tried to restrict imports of Japanese textiles through
legislation or in other ways, the Japanese textile interests
would just "have to fight it out".*
(g). General American-Japanese Trade Relations.
EA
On February 16 Mr. Dickover, of this Embassy, had a
conversation with Mr. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs of the Foreien Office, who expressed his
disagreement with the alleged tendency in the United States
toward the restriction of Japanese imports, particularly
on the round that imports of Anerican products into Japan
far exceeded imports of Japanese products into the United
States.**
In the course of his conversation Mr. Kurusu
stated thet apparently the Department of State gave con-
sideration to every petition received from American manu-
facturers for restriction of Japanese imports; that if the
United States hoped to receive more liberal treatment of
important American exports to Japan, the Americans should
not irritate the Japanese by asking them to restrict their
unimpc:tant exports to the United States, such as cotton
textiles
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1669 of February 7, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1690 of February 19, 1936.

=== Page 671 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
textiles, canned tuna, pencils, porcelains, matches,
velveteen and woollen εloves, and cotton rugs; that he
could expect little from his efforts to obtain liberal
treatment for American im orts of lerge items, such as
petroleum products and automobiles, so long as the American
Government attempted to restrict the importation of the
before-mentioned small items; and that the possibility
of Japan's finding sources of supply of raw cotton in
place of the United States was inconceivable for many
years to come. Mr. Kurusu's remarks, as well as editorials
in the Japanese press, were evidence of an active resentment
in Japan against reçuests from the United States for the
voluntary restriction by Japanese exporters of shipments
to the United States.
(h)
American Oil Interests in Japan.
Further discussions took place between Mr. Kurusu and
the local representatives of the Standard Vacuum and Rising
Sun Oil Companies concerninr the haraships imposed upon
these conpanies by the Petroleun Industry Law, particularly
the storege requirements. Hovever, no egreement was reached
before the close of the month. Various proposals were made
by the Japanese authorities to connensate the oil interests
for the six months' storece requirements but none of them
proved acceptable to the oil companies.
II. RELATIONS WITH OTIER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
793.9
No development of perticular significance appears to
have taken place in Sino-Japanese relations during the
month of February, although more emphasis was placed on
closer

=== Page 672 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
closer cooperation between the Army, the Navy, and the
Foreign Office and also between the Tokyo authorities and
the Japanese authorities in China. Major General Isogai,
Military Attaché to the Japanese Embassy in Peiping, on
February 5 reported to the then War Minister, General
Kawashima, on conditions in China. According to the
Japanese press, officers in the General Staff consulted
with General Kawashima and Major. General Isogai and agreed
with them on the following progran for China: that a
unified policy, as mentioned above, should be adopted; that
efforts should be made to have the Nanking Government
"liquidate" its policy of resistance to Japan and "Manchu-
kuo" and to suppress the "malign" activities of the Kuo-
mintang; that the Army should give as much friendly help
as possible, within the limits of non-interference in
China's internal affairs, to further the health develop-
ment of the Hopei-Chahar Folitical Council. It was reported
on February 8 by Domei (news agency) and several of the
vernacular newspapers that on the previous day high officials
of the Ministries for Foreign Affairs, War, and Navy, had
come to a complete agreement of views on measures for
carrying out Japan's foreign policy as set forth by Mr.
Hirota in his speech to the Diet on January 2l.* Probably
in connection with the above-mentioned plan to aid in
development of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council, two
Japanese advisers were appointed to the Council on
February 10: Mr. Seiki Yano, Chief of the Asiatic Affairs
Section of the Manchukuo" Ministry for Foreign Affairs,
and Mr. Minoru Aoki, Counsellor of the Planning Department
of
Embassy's Monthly Political Report for January, Section
II (a).

=== Page 673 === (confidence 0.99)
of the Manchukuo" State Council. During the early part
of the month, Major General Isogai and Mr. Ariyoshi,
retiring Ambassador to China, appeared to share the view
that Japan's policies toward China should be unified and
strengthened. However, et the end of the month Isofai was
apparently more satisfied with Japan's policy, judging from
the following statement attributed to him by a Domei (news
agency) despatch from Shenghai dated February 28: "Both
the Japanese Government and civilian quarters are completely
in accord with my views with regard to Japan's policies
toward China, as was revealed in the series of conversations
held durine my recent stay in the country." Furthernore,
Mr. Arita, who on February 8 was appointed Ambassador to
China to succeed Mr. Ariyoshi, was reported by the OSAKA
MAINICHI of February 25 to be in substantial agreement with
Major General Isogai, with whom he departed for China on
February 24. This newspaper cuoted the new Ambassador as
having said: "You see, the General and I are travelling
together on the same ship to our posts, which should indicate
that we are showinf a united front." Major General Isogai
was stated to have confirmed the Ambassador's remarks
regarding their complete agreement in policy.
According to the YOMIURI of February 10, the Army
General Staff decided to increase the Japanese forces in
North China without waiting for the appropriation of
special funds at the special session of the Imperial Diet,
because of the "momentous situation following the foundation
of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council in Peiping."
EA
It was reported that the Overseas Ministry had decided
to have Japan undertake cultivation of raw cotton in North
China on a five-year plan to be participated in by the
three

=== Page 674 === (confidence 0.98)

three principal Japanese cotton spinning companies.
The only clash reported by the Japanese press during
the month was the alleged physical mistreatment of 13
Japanese and 28 Koreans at Tsangchow, Hopei Province, on
February 20 by Chinese police. The Japanese Consulate
General at Tientsin was said to heve started an investi-
gation of the affair.
(b). Soviet Russia.
IEE
There was a very noticeable decrease in the number of
741.90
clashes reported along the "Manchukuoan"-Outer Mongolian and
"Manchukuoan"-Siberian borders durine the month under
review. However, accordin to news items in the Japanese
press, on February 12 an incident occurred which involved
larger military forces on both sides than has usually been
the case.* A detachment of the Kvantung Army encountered a
force in "Manchukuo" territory variously estimated at 200,
500, or 1,000 Mongols near Clahodka at the southwestern end
of Lake Buir. Reportedly there were a dozen Japanese
casualties and during the fifhting two Soviet airnlanes
dropped bonbs on the Japanese-"Manchukuo" troops. Soviet
reports were to the effect that twenty truckloads of
Japanese and "Manchukuo" soldiers had invaded Outer Mon-
golian territory with the aid of two tenks and had been
repulsed by the Mongols. On February 21 the Japanese
Ambassador in Moscow was said to have been informed that
the Soviet Government would be willing to consult the
Outer Mongolian authorities concerning the establishment
of a commission to deal with border disputes if "Manchukuo"
so desired. A report from Urga dated February 29 stated
that

but thet the Soviet Union would expect to have as many

(c).
Great Britain.
Two or three days after the outbreak of February 26,
the British Ambassador conveyed to the Minister for Foreign
Affairs a message of sympathy from the British Government
on the loss suffered by the Government and people of Japan
"through the untimely death at the hands of assassins of
the Prime Minister and other distinguished servants of
their country".
(d). The London Naval Conference.
WE
Rear Admiral Yasutaro Iwashita, Chief Technical Advise
to the Japanese Delegation to the London Naval Conference,
arrived in Japan on Februery 21. The JAPAN ADVERTISER of
February 22 attributed the followine statements to him:
that as long as the other major world Powers maintain the
attitude which they disolayed at London, ideal disarmament
cannot be realized; that the Japanese plan for a common
upper limit contained the vital and major necessities for
disarmament; that it is possible for minor problems to be
solved at London but that there is little use for the
nations

=== Page 677 === (confidence 0.98)
- 12 -
nations to waste time and money discussing unimportant
matters while neglecting important questions vital to the
peace of the world; and that it is absurd to anticipate
a competitive naval race among the Powers as a result of
Japan's withdrawal from the Conference. Before the end of
the month, the Navy Ministry had ready for distribution a
pamphlet discussing Japan's part in the Conference and
Japan's mission in the future. The first chapter of the
pamphlet takes up the various attitudes of the other Fowers
and "their responsilility" for the breakdown of the Con-
ference; the second chapter outlines the British plan for
the mutual exchange of building programs, the American
proposal for a 20 per cent all around reduction, and the
qualitative limitation plans put forth; the third chapter
discusses a possible Anglo-American entente, present naval
streneths, and the Americen and British construction plans;
and the fourthh and last chapter is entitled "The Mission of
the Japanese Empire and Deterrination Incumbent upon Us."
Althouch the pamphlet was ready for distribution, the Naval
Attaché was unable to obtain copies before the close of the
month owing to the disturbed state of affairs.
(e). Turkey.
The new Turkish Ambassador to Japan, Mr. Husrev Gerede,
arrived in this country on February 22, replacing Mr. Nebil,
the former Chargé d'Affaires.
(f). Siam.
Yoshitaka Ono, . Naval Atteché to the Japanese Legation in
PUDLICAI
Bangkok, was reported by the OSAKA MAINICHI to have stated
that there was a growing demand in Siam for Japanese
technical

=== Page 678 === (confidence 0.99)
- 13 -
technical knowledge in railway construction, road building
and other industrial developments; that Japanese goods
were held in high esteem in that country and that there
was a considerable number of persons in Siam, headed by
Home Minister Luang Pradit, who desired the establishment
of an Asiatic League under the chairmanship of Japan.
(g). International.
WE
Questioned by foreign newspaper correspondents on
February 9 concerning the debate in the British Parliament
on the redistribution of raw materials, colonies, and
markets, the spokesman of the Foreign Office was reported
by Domei (news agency) to have stated that the measures
hitherto taken for the maintenance of peace, such as the
balance of power before the World War, the League of Nations,
and the Kellogg Pact, ignored the real causes that menaced
peace; that due consideration should be given to rising
nations if the causes for the disturbance of peace were to
be removed; that et present a few large Powers monopolized
vast territories, naturel resources, und markets; and that
nations fuil of vitality, hampered by present international
arrangements, should be given raw materials and merkets
necessary for their national existence. It is interesting
to note the similarity of Mr. Amau's statements to argu-
ments put forth by the Hitler régime during recent months.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
Mr. Genji Matsuda, Minister for Education, died on
February 1 and was succeeded in office by Mr. Takukichi
Kawasaki, Chief Secretary of the Minseito.
Further evidence that the controversy over the
institutional theory of the Emperor was not dead* was
afforded
Monthly Report for January, Section III (e).

=== Page 679 === (confidence 0.98)
8
- 14 -
arforded by the attack on Dr. Minobe at his residence in
the suburbs of Tokyo on February 21. According to the
JAPAN CHRONICLE of February 22, one Juso Oda, a member of
a Rightist organization in the city of Fukuoka, called on
Dr. Minobe and shot him with a pistol, though not fatally.
This newspaper stated that a paper had later been discovered
on the person of Oda in which was expressed his determination
to inflict "the judgment of heaven on Dr. Minobe" because
that was the only step to take for the punishment of e
trator who was neglectful of the Imperial favors bestowed
upon him.
The trial of Lieutenant Colonel Aizawe for the murder
of Lieutenant-General Nagata, which was opened on January 28,
l936, and was indefinitely postponed on February 27, had a
close connection in its fundamental aspects with the
attempt on the life of Dr. Minobe, since one of Aizawa's
motives for the murder was his conviction that Nagata had
hindered the Army in the "clarification of the national
polity spirit". (A loose phrase meaning emphasis of the
Emperor's almost divine position as head of the Japanese
State, denial of the theory that he is only an institution,
and reaffirmation of the fact that the Japanese Army is
solely under the command of its Emperor). The trial of
Lieutenant-General Nagata's assassin was full of political
significance, since the defendant and his counsels accused
a number of prominent statesmen and officials of lack of
patriotism, several of whom were later killed on February 26.*
It transpired during the month that Aizawa's closest
associates, who had inspired him to his deed, were among
the ringleaders of the revolt of February 26.
Another
* Embassy's despatch No. 1707 of March 5, 1936.

=== Page 680 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
Another immediate cause of the insurrection was the
Diet election held on February 20, which gave the Minseito,
the pro-Government and relatively liberal party, 205 seats
in the Lower House as against 174 seats for the Seiyukai,
a more chauvinistic party. The Seiyukai, unlike the Min-
seito, had in its election platforn expressed a desire for
the abolition of the senior statesmen system, which was one
of the principal aims of the insurgents. It was probably
for
in part these reasons that the outbreak took place when it
did, since the younger Army officers behind it were alarmed
over the victory of the pro-Government Minseito party.*
On February 26, in the early morning, about 1400 younger
officers and men belongine to three of the regiments stationed
in Tokyo, armed with machine guns, rifles and bayonets,
attacked the residences of the Premier, Viscount Makoto
Saito (Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal), General Jotaro
Watanabe (Inspector General of Military Education of the
Army), Admiral Kantaro Suzuki (Grand Chamberlain), ana Mr.
Korekiyo Takahashi (Finance Minister).** Viscount Saito,
General Watanabe, Mr. Takahashi, and Colonel Matsuo, Premier
Okada's brother-in-law, were killed. Colonel Matsuo, who
was at the time living in the Official Residence of Frenier
Okada, was mistaken for the latter, who escaped. Admiral
Suzuki was seriously injured, although he was alive at the
end of the month. The rebels also attempted to kill Count
Nobukai Makino, former Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, in
an inn at Yugawara but their intended victim escaped unharmed.
It was reported that the assassins had attempted to kill
the Genro, Prince Saionji, but that he had escaped.
A.
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1706 of March 5, 1936, and
telegram No. 37 of February 26, 1936.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 38 of February 26, and despatch
No. 1721 of March 6, 1936.

=== Page 681 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
A group of the insurrectionaries took control of the
Orricial Residence of the Premier, the Metropolitan Police
Board, the War Office, the General Staff Building, and
subsequently the new Diet Building, the Sanno Hotel, and
the Peers' Club, the latter building being held only for a
short time. On the morning of the outbreak, groups of
soldiers left at the principal newspaper offices a mimeo-
graphed statement, signed by Captains Nonaka and Ando, of
the Third Infantry Regiment, which stated that the present
Government had been drifting away from the true spirit of
Japan and usurping the Emperor's prerogatives and that if
such conditions continued the relations of Japan to China,
Soviet Russia, Great Britain, and the United States would
"become explosive in nature."
Despite the general confusion the War Minister, several
Generals, the Navy Minister, and the Chief of the Naval Staff
succeeded in enterin the Palace and having a conference on
what measures should be adopted by the Army to quell the
uprising. A state of emereency was declared in the afternoon
of February 25 and Lieutenant-General Kohei Kashii, Com-
mander of the Tokyo Garrison, was placed in charge of the
situation and on the following day was appointed Commander
of the Martial Law Headquarters. The Yokosuka Defense
Squadron of the Navy arrived in Tokyo Bay on the afternoon
of February 26 and on the morning of February 28 the First
Fleet anchored in the Bay for protective purposes.*
Negotiations between the rebel officers and the Army
commenced on February 27 and continued altogether for
three days. The expedient of sending an Imperial Command
to
Embassy's telegram No. 45 of February 28, 1936.

=== Page 682 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17-
to the insurgents to surrender was not resorted to until
the afternoon of February 28. The revolting officers and
men were not declared rebels by the Government until they
refused to obey the Emperor's command on the following
morning. It was then that forceful measures were definitely
adopted by the Army and the Government. These measures
ended in the surrender of all the soldiers and all but
several of the insurgent officers by the afternoon of
February 29 without fighting, as far as the Embassy is
aware.
With reference to the immediate effect of the uprising
on the Cabinet, on the evening of Februery 26 Mr. Goto,
the Minister for Home Affairs, was appointed as temporary
Acting Premier because of his seniority in the Cabinet.
Mr. Goto continued to act in this capacity for the remaining
three days of the month.
During the four days that the revolt lasted, a
censorshin, relaxed from time to time, was placed on
outgoing news despatches.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V.
GOVLRNMENT FINANCE.
The death of Mr. Takahashi, the Finance Minister, on
February 26 was officially announced on February 28, this
news having been withheld probably to avoid upsetting
financial conditions. This announcement created little
perceptible effect in financial circles, although Japanese
bankers had expected that the news would have an unfavor-
able effect on the Japanese bond merket and might bring
about serious disturbances in the Japanese money market.*
Financial
Embassy's telegrams No. 41 of February 27, and No. 45 of
February 28, 1936.

=== Page 683 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
Financial and economic conditions were remarkably stable
from February 26 to the end of the month.* The exchange
rate was held practically to normal by the pegging opera-
tions of the Yokohama Specie ank. The stock market was
closed and
some of the exchane markets were open a
short time. Banks were nominally open but did little or
no business.
On the afternoon of February 28, Mr. Eiji Yasui,
Governor of Osaka Prefecture, addressed the following
radio broadcast to the public: "In financial circles,
the Tokyo district does not seem to show any marked
difference from ordinary days. Since yesterday there has
been no difference at all in Osaka. The benk clearing
house is functioning smoothly. Under such circumstances,
I wish the people to rest assured of peace and guard
against being misled by rumors and fabrications."
VI.
GENERAL EC ONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreicn Trade.
Exports fron Japan for February amounted to
¥208,420,000 while imports into this country amouted to
¥283,305,000, as compared with exports for January of
¥174,550,000 and imports of ¥245,042,000. The large
excess of imports over exports can be explained by
unusually large imports of raw cotton and wool.*
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock market was active until February 26, on
which date it was closed; the bond and debenture market
was active; and the average yen selling rate was 0.2901,
as compared with $0.28896 for January. The exchange rate
was

May 18, 1936.
The Embassy's monthly report for March
covers for the most part subjects with
which the Division is already familiar.
However, the following items may be of
interest:
Mr. Horinouchi in conversation with
Mr. Neville denied the report of a pro-
jected good will mission on the occasion
of the meeting of the Institute of Pacific
Relations to be held in July at Yosemite,
California, but added that there naturally
would be Japanese delegates at that meeting
who would have no official capacity.
In connection with the departure of an
economic mission to Siam on March 13 the
ASAHI stated that a reason for sending
the mission 1s that Siam, "hitherto muled
by British capital and under Chinese in-
fluence", had sought assistance from Japan,
and that the mission answered the popular
demand in Japan for an economic advance
toward the south.
A new "cultural" organization which
calls itself the Kenmu Society has been
organized by military, naval, political
and business leaders with a view toward
applying to the present situation the
spirit

=== Page 695 === (confidence 0.98)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). General American-Japanese Trade Relations.
EA
In the course of a conversation with Premier Hirota
on March 13, the Ambassador took occasion on his own
initiative to remark on the difficulties occasioned to
the American Government by the increasing imports into
the United States of cheap Japanese products. After
speaking of the cooperative proposals of the American
Government to solve the problem and of the pressure being
exerted on the American Government by domestic industrial
interests, the Ambassador advised Mr. Hirota that eventually
he might be forced to appeal to him for more restrictive
efforts on the part of the Japanese Government. It was
made plain to the Premier that these observations werepurely
informal and not made under instructions from the Department.*
(b). American Oil Interests.
Negotiations continued during the month between the
local representatives of the Standard Vacuum and Rising
Sun Oil Companies and Japanese officials on the question
of the application of the Petroleum Industry Law to these
companies.** Acting under instructions from their home
offices, on March 23, 1936, the representatives of the
foreign oil interests made the proposal to Mr. Kurusu,
Chief of the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office, and
Mr. Yoshino, Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, that
some sort of contractual arrangement be made between the
foreign oil companies and the Mitsui (Japanese) interests
so as to avoid the necessity of storage of non-commercial
stocks by the foreien firms.*** The Japanese officials
appeared
*
Embassy's telegram No. 74 of March 13, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1747 of March 20, 1936.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 1757 of April 1, 1936.

=== Page 696 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
appeared to be pleased with this proposition and Mr.
Yoshino voluntarily offered to continue to withhold
approval of the pendine applications for new refineries
in Japan and to allow the foreign companies quotas for
the second quarter of the year equal to those granted
for the first quarter. Mr. Yoshino further stated that
he would try to meet the desire of the foreien oil com-
panies for the suspension of the regulations after July 1,
1936, including the requirement that the foreign firms
have six months' stocks of oil in storage on that date.
However, he said that he could not officially state that
the regulations would be suspended after July 1, 1936.
It was decided by the American and British Embassies that
diplomatic representations would be withheld until the
negotiations between the Japanese authorities and the oil
company representatives had taken more definite shape.
(c). Discrimination in "Manchukuo" Against
EA
American Automotive Trucks.
In compliance with the Department's instruction
No. 950 of February 20, 1936, on March 17 the Counselor
of the Embassy invited the attention of the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs to the reported discrimination in
"Manchukuo" against American automotive trucks in favor
of Dowa trucks. Mr. Shigemitsu said that he would try
to obtain what information he could on the matter but
made no further promises.*
(a).
Aftermath of the February 26 Incident in
Its Relation to the American Embassy.
On March 2 the Ambassador addressed a letter to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs requesting him to convey
to
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1734 of March 18, 1936.

=== Page 697 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to the appropriate authorities his sincere appreciation
of the thorough and efficient measures taken by various
departments for the protection of the personnel and property
of the Embassy during the uprising of February 26.* In the
afternoon of March 22 the Foreign Office conveyed to the
Embassy the following message from the Home Office: that
although the insurgents had surrendered, it could not be
stated that everything was quiet; that the Home Office
was still anxious about the safety of the American, Soviet
Russian, and British Ambassadors, Counselors, Military, and
Naval Attachés; that the responsibility for anything that
might happen to these persons would have to be assumed by
the Home Office and that therefore that Department would
like to assign a plainclothesman to accompany each of these
officials in his automobile.** The Embassy accepted the
offer of the Home Ministry and on March 3 plainclothesmen
were assigned to the persons concerned. Several of these
men continued to appear at the Embassy for duty throughout
the month.
Premier Hirota told the Ambassador on March 13 that
the policy pursued by him as Minister for Foreign Affairs
would not be altered and stated in the course of the
conversatio n that he saw no serious problems in Japanese-
American relations.***

(f). K. K. Kawakami's Observations on
American
Opinion Concerning the February 26 Incident.
Mr. K. K. Kawakami, staff correspondent of the TOKYO
NICHI NICHI AND OSAKA MAINICIHI, sent a cable to his news-
paper on March 4 to the effect that Japan's prestige as a
civilized nation had suffered a serious setback in the eyes
of Americans as a result of the February 26 assassinations;
that persons in Anerica well disposed toward Japan were
unable to explain or to excuse the recent "Tokyo atrocities"
which they had thoucht possible only in semi-civilized
countries; and that Americans in eeneral felt that, what-
ever the composition of the new Japanese Cabinet, Japan's
policy with regard to the continent of Asia would be more
in consonance with "nationalistic clamors".
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). Soviet Russia.
/EE
Russo-Japanese relations were considerably aggravated
durin the month by further border incidents, some of them
2
severe in nature. Negotiations between Japan and Soviet
Russia and also between "Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolia for
the establishment of respective border commissions appeared
to

=== Page 699 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
EE
to be proceeding somewhat more favorably during the first
half of the month but these negotiations received a setback
with the occurrence of additional border clashes.* Before
these clashes took place the nature of the Mongol-Manchu
frontier problem became clarified to a certain extent
through a tendency on the part of both Soviet Russia and
Japan to acknowledge their concern over a region where
their own interests were definitely at stake.
There were two minor incidents on the "Manchukuoan"-
Siberian frontier on March 13. A clash reported to have
been serious took place on March 25 near Chanelingtsu, a
village not far from the Tumen River and on the common
frontier of the USSR, Korea, and "Manchukuo". According
to the Japanese newspaper versions of the affair, between
thirty and eighty Soviet soldiers crossed the border into
"Manchukuo" territory and fired on nine Jaoanese soldiers.
Subsequent fighting took place after the alleged refusal
of the Soviet soldiers to ne otiate a truce with the
Japanese soldiers. An announcement of the Jananese War
Office stated that a dozen or more Japanese and "Manchukuo"
troops had been wounded in all the fighting. A Japanese
officer and a Japanese enlisted m an were missing after
the encounter and several days later their bodies were
surrendered to the J anese in accordance with an arrange-
ment made through regular diplomatic negotiations. According
to information furnished to the Military Attaché by a
Japanese Army officer, by the end of the month the Russians
had massed about 2,O00 troops near Changlingtsu and the
Japanese-
Embassy's despatches Nos. 1740 of March 18, and 1755 of

Japanese-Korean Army had sent a mixed brigade to the Tumen
River.* As is usual in such cases both the Japanese and
Soviet Foreign Offices protested vigorously against the
incident.
A clash occurred on March 24 at Halhamio on the
"Manchukuoan"-Outer Mongolian border when a group of
Outer Mongolian soldiers opened fire upon a Japanese
frontier patrol of five men. According to a news renort
in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 26, the Japanese soldiers,
who were in "Manchukuoan" territory, returned the fire of
the Mongolians and one Japanese soldier was killed. Another
border skirmish took place on March 29 near Lake Buir which
was more serious than the Halhamio incident. A Domei (news
agency) despatch in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 31 reported
that two Mongolian military planes had opened fire with
machine guns on a Japanese-"Manchukuo" border patrol within
the territory of "Manchukuo" and that one Japanese soldier
had been killed and four others wounded. According to a
Domei despatch dated April 1 in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
April 2, on the last day of March an even more serious
incident occurred when twelve Outer Mongolian planes
dropped bombs on "Manchukuo" territory following which
Outer Mongolian land forces invaded "Manchukuo" in armored
cars and were repulsed by Japanese-"Manchukuo" soldiers,
who inflicted heavy losses on the invaders.
Soviet-Japanese relations were further complicated
by two events that took place in Japan. It transpired on
March 12 that the Japanese police had arrested eleven
Japanese interpreters, translators, and language students
employed
Embassy's telegram No. 81 of March 30, 1936.

employed by the Soviet Embassy in Tokyo on the ground of
subversive activities in gathering information during the
suppression of the February 26 incident. The Soviet
protests over this action appeared to accomplish nothing
and toward the end of the month it was reported that six
more persons connected with the Soviet Embassy had been
arrested. The second happening was the seizure on March 22
of two Soviet commercial vessels, the SUCHAN and the DVINA,
at Aomori because of alleged espionage in a fortified zone.
The Soviet vessels were not released before the end of the

When asked by the Ambassador for a definition of the
term "positive diplomacy" in connection with the new Govern-
ment, the Premier replied that the term had reference solely
to Soviet Russia and China and simply meant the expediting
of the policy already enunciated.** It would appear from
this statement and from previous statements made by Mr.
Hirota when he was Minister for Foreign Affairs that
Japan's "positive policy" toward Russia aims at an early
demarcation of the borders between "Manchukuo" and Siberia
and "Manchukuo" and Outer Mongolia, and the establishment
of border commissions for the settlement of disputes in
the regions concerned. The Premier also stated in the
conversation referred to that the Japanese Army wanted
an increase of its forces in Manchuria to approach more
closely the size of the Soviet forces in Siberia. At the
same time Mr. Hirota reiterated the statement made by him
as Minister for Foreign Affairs that there would be no
war
